HARVARD COLLEGE LIBRARY

Southern Glamour The glamour of the South Seas is known to have remarkable effects on men and women. Its effects on one man and one woman, as related in this novel, completely overcome their resistance to all romantic forms of glamour. Furthermore they are badly upset by the expert operations of a ransom racket.

Gloria Drake, whose Holly wood reputation is not demure, is immune to the charms of many men, but she enjoys the exercise of her power over them. 

Her power is challenged by

Alan Armitage, author, playwright, poet, and sportsman.

He plans to be alone on a cruise for writing. Women as characters are tremendously interesting to him; as a person, one woman is no more precious than another.

Both of these people need a lot of glamour to change them.

They get it on a yacht, with considerable adventure and a maximum of entertainment.

Southern Glamour by Juanita Savage AUTHOR OF "SPANISH RAPTURE" "SPANISH LOVE"

SOUTHERN GLAMOUR

CHAPTER I 

83 

SCORE of brown-skinned Kanakas, laughing and browny singing in a crazy canoe with a patchwork sail, evidently imagined that the whole Pacific Ocean was their own exclusive preserve, and that they alone had the right of way. Anyhow, the fact that the luxury cruiser S.S. Golden Gate, ploughing her 30,000 tons through the translucent blue waters at twenty-five knots an hour, was bearing straight for them, and that their crazy craft was heading across the Golden Gate's bows, seemed quite to have escaped their notice. 

About ten score of passengers, lounging in the bows or leaning on the rails of the Golden Gate, yelled and shouted in unison, as people both on land and sea invariably do when an accident or disaster seems inevitable. The laughing Kanakas paid no heed, but the navigating officer of the luxury cruiser shouted an order, the helmsman, galvanised into sudden life, twisted the wheel round at express speed, the Golden. 

Gate lurched suddenly as she answered the helm and changed her course, and a little gentleman, by name

Cyrus Oke Cole, who had been leaning far over the rail in his anxiety to shout unheard advice to the Kanakas, overbalanced and fell ungracefully from the upper deck into the Pacific Ocean. 

Five score passengers yelled again as they saw Cyrus

Cole disappear and hit the ocean, the officer on the bridge slung round the signal indicator to "Full Speed 

Astern," at the same time yelling "Man Overboard," a cry which was repeated by every uniformed man on deck. Before the shouts had become intelligible above

the excited clamour, and almost at the moment that

Mr. Cole disappeared into the depths of the Pacific, a tall, slim young man in a white drill suit took a running leap across the deck and dived into the sea. Before any of the passengers, or even the uniformed men, had time to gasp, a rotund little man scrambled up on to the rail as the Golden Gate swayed again, and he also dived into the sea and disappeared. 

Something approaching pandemonium ensued among the passengers. Hundreds of them, yelling excitedly, rushed to the side of the vessel to see that the slim young man who had dived overboard was supporting in the water Mr. Cyrus Oke Cole, who was struggling violently, and that the tubby little man, looking something like an inflated white balloon, was swimming fast to his assistance. His assistance was needed, for it appeared to the hundreds of watchers on deck that Mr. Cole imagined the best way to save his life was to get a sort of strangle-hold on his would-be rescuer, and he had grappled with him as if he were engaging in an aquatic "all-in" wrestling match.

The inflated white balloon, who had gone through the water like a torpedo, arrived in the nick of time, and his spluttering shout could be heard faintly on deck as he grabbed Cyrus Oke Cole. His methods were drastic, but they had the effect of compelling Mr. Cole to disengage his strangle-clutch from the slim young man, who disappeared under the water for a few seconds and then reappeared to assist his balloon-like companion in supporting Mr. Cole, who, unintentionally, of course, had done his best to drown him and himself.

In the meantime, by some miracle, it had occurred to the laughing, singing Kanakas in their crazy canoe that something untoward was afoot, or rather, afloat,

ΙΟ

and, with shrill cries, they turned their craft and got alongside the three men in the water and hauled them inboard before the boat from the Golden Gate cast off. 

The engine-room bell on the Golden Gate had clanged "Stop" after the vessel had gone full speed astern for some distance, and she hove-to within a couple of hundred yards or so of the Kanaka canoe, to which the men in the life-boat which was lowered rowed rapidly and retrieved from the hands of the jabbering natives the half-drowned Cyrus Oke Cole and his rescuers.

As Mr. Cole, in a state of collapse, was assisted up the accommodation ladder lowered down the side of the Golden Gate, hundreds of passengers dashed forward, squeaking with excitement and reminding the captain on the bridge of fowls on a poultry farm when the morning feed was being scattered. The two white rescuers of Mr. Cole climbed the ladder without assistance, and as the passengers swarmed around them cheering, patting their wet backs and trying to shake their hands, they, as if by pre-arrangement, lowered their heads, butted their way together through the throng, and bolted to their cabin, the door of which they locked behind them, shutting out the cheering crowd that streamed after them.

The crowd continued to block the gangway outside the cabin until one of the officers of the vessel, accompanied by a couple of stewards, appeared to disperse them, reminding them that the gentlemen inside probably needed medical attention, or, at least, restoratives, instead of cheers. Then he proceeded to hammer on the door of the cabin. "Let me in, Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower," he called, as the throng began slowly and reluctantly to disperse. "This is Grant, third officer, speaking, and 

I've brought you along brandy and whisky. . . . Shoo!"

II

he added fiercely to a young woman in futuristic beach pyjamas who clasped his elbow and begged to be allowed to be the first to congratulate the gallant rescuers. "Shoo! young lady, and shoo quick and good! These gentlemen inside, Mr. Armitage and Mr. 

Flower, are at the moment, I guess, wearing their birthday suits, and if they're not being sick they must be badly in need of brandy and such-like attention instead of female congratulations. You run along up on deck, or you'll miss getting your first glimpse of 

Hawaii. Shoo!"

He knocked again on the cabin door, which, after a pause, was opened to reveal the tubby, chubby, applecheeked little gentleman who had looked like a white balloon in the water, wearing now a huge bath towel draped around him, toga fashion, but looking quite unlike an ancient Roman. In the background Alan 

Armitage was vigorously massaging his lean, clean, sinewy body and limbs with a steaming hot towel, and he greeted the third officer and the stewards with a boyishly gleeful shout. "Hail, friends!" he cried. "You are thrice welcome. 

Blossom, my lad, feast your eyes upon the white-coated steward with a bottle of good Martell in one hand and, if I mistake me not, a full bottle of John Haig in the other. And, presumably, our good friend Mr.

Grant has orders to dispense both liberally at the expense of the Shipping Company, which may be contrary to his convictions, if he has any convictions against him, in addition to being saddled with at 

Scottish name. Brandy for me, steward, but I know 

Mr. Flower will prefer whisky, although he deserves none for following my damn'-fool action in diving overboard. One damn' fool is enough even on what is advertised as a luxury liner, for fools are often expensive luxuries, even if they are worth nothing." 12

"One moment, please, one moment!" exclaimed Mr. 

Horatio Flower, who was always called "Blossom" by his friend and employer, Alan Rodmell Armitage, author, playwright, poet and sportsman, liable to do any sort of damn fool act at any time on the impulse of the moment. "Let me make a note of that. It might come in useful as an aphorism." He tripped over his voluminous bath towel as he hastened across the cabin to snatch up a writing-pad and pencil, but recovered himself and laughed as he scribbled. "Now for the whisky!" he added. "Steward, when you begin to pour it out leave only enough room at the top of the glass for me to breathe a blessing into." "The perfect secretary and the prize whisky absorber!" laughed Alan Armitage, as the stewards hastened to serve the drinks. "I'll say that the performance of you two gentlemen was the slickest and pluckiest that I've ever witnessed," commented the third officer. "I congratulate you both, and I hope neither of you is any the worse for your adventure. I guess that but for your pluck and promptitude little Mr. Cole would now be inside a shark." "I hope the little fellow is all right," said Alan. "He looked pretty ghastly as he was hauled up on deck." "I guess he was more scared than hurt," responded 

Grant. "He was coming round when I left him, and the doctor was inducing him to disgorge a few gallons of the Pacific Ocean before dosing him with stimulants. 

Mr. Cole has made several trips with us and I know him rather well. I'll bet that he's already wondering how best he can show his gratitude to you for saving his life. Incidentally, although he's small physically,

Cyrus O. Cole is quite a big man in the film business. 

But I must bustle off," he added with a hasty glance at 13

his watch. "We should be berthed in Honolulu within an hour or so. I'll say there'll be some demonstration from the passengers when you gentlemen make your appearance on desk again, and when you land." "Damn it, I hadn't thought of that," exclaimed

Armitage with a frown. "I don't feel the least like a hero, and I detest the very idea of being lionised, particularly by a lot of sticky American girls." "I guess you'll have to endure it, Mr. Armitage, and of course, Mr. Flower will share the honours with you," commented Grant laughingly, turning to the door. "Absurd! Impossible!" gasped Blossom, after gulping down the last of his whisky. "I say, don't go, Mr. 

Grant! Just a minute. Tell the would-be hero-worshippers that we're both completely prostrated as a result of our exertions, and that we shall have to remain in bed for hours and hours and must not on any account be disturbed." "Yes, that's the idea, Mr. Grant," chimed in Alan. "We'll lock ourselves in here, lie doggo for a few hours after the ship has berthed, and then steal ashore at nightfall to avoid any fuss. I'm sure you'll be able to fix things for us." "I'll do my best," Grant replied, "but I doubt if 

I'll be able to convince anyone who saw you and Mr. 

Flower charge through the mob that you are now quite prostrated. Besides, there will be a gang of Pressmen thirsting for your blood, so to speak, besides a battalion of camermen, as soon as we reach port. However, I'll do what I can to save your blushes."

He went out, followed by the stewards, to find that groups of chattering would-be hero-worshippers had reassembled in the gangways and corridors outside Alan's suite, and he had occasion to lie brazenly almost 14

immediately. Inside the cabin Blossom regarded Alan ruefully, as he helped himself to more whisky. "I'm afraid your impetuosity promises to force us into the limelight and spoil the nice quiet, restful time we contemplated," he remarked.

Alan Armitage grunted and lit a cigarette. "What about your own impetuosity, Blossom," he retorted. "When I contemplate what may be in store for us I begin to feel almost sorry that I didn't let the little blighter drown." "I don't believe it, Alan. I'm sure your conscience would trouble you if you thought of the amiable Cyrus O. Cole reposing in nasty sticky pieces in the interior of sharks. Besides, you must remember his telling us, almost with tears in his eyes, how eagerly he was looking forward to seeing his little daughter who had been sent to Honolulu for the benefit of her health, and how worried he seemed by the idea that her life might be in danger." 99 "Blossom, you are allowing the sloppily sentimental streak in your character to get the better of you,' commented Alan. "Still, I don't mind admitting that 

I did think of Cole's pathetic tale of his little girl before I went overside, so it may be that I am developing a sentimental streak. Perish the thought!"

Even as he spoke, there came a knocking at the cabin door and a voice called "Mr. Armitage-Mr. 

Flower-can I see you, please?" "Go to hell!" snarled Alan. "Haven't you been told that we are both too ill and exhausted to see anyone?" "But I am Patsy, the steward, sir, and I've been sent along by the chief officer to see if you want anything to eat," explained the voice from outside the door. "Eat?" exclaimed Alan. "Do you want to eat, Blossom? It has just occurred to me that I am famished, 15

and a cut from an ox or something of the sort seems to be called for immediately." "It can only have been the excitement that has made me overlook the fact that I am starving, and that a saddle of lamb, with about a quart of green peas and a few pounds of new potatoes, and, perhaps, a bottle of vintage wine, are what my system is craving for," responded Blossom, gathering up his bath towel and girding it around him as he crossed the cabin to admit Patsy, the steward, and to shut the door with a bang almost on the face of an angular woman who had on deck had hysterics when Mr. Cole fell overboard. "The trouble is, of course, Patsy, that when the loiterers who want to crown us with laurel wreaths and all that sort of thing see you and your assistants coming along with tray-loads of food and drink, they will hardly be likely to believe that Mr. Flower and I are so exhausted that we may give up the ghost at any moment," remarked Alan, after he and Blossom had conferred earnestly with the steward and approved a menu. "You can leave it to the chief officer to deal with the mob, sir, and you can leave it to me to serve ye a lunch that'll make yer mouth water and that ye'll remember afterwards almost before ye've finished puting dry clothes on yerselves."

Patsy was as good as his word. Less than an hour later Horatio Flower, clad in an immaculately fitting and immaculately white suit, and looking even pinker and more Pickwickian than ever, heaved a deep sigh of satisfaction as he laid aside his table-napkin and proceeded to pierce and light a long cigar. "A consommé which seemed to be concocted from deliciously fresh cream, flavoured with the tips of the most exquisite asparagus, with perhaps just a soupçon of sherry," he murmured. "White fish which seemed 16

to be a sort of angelic version of a prime Dover sole. 

Saddle of lamb from a lamb which was surely the recent offspring of a ewe which had browsed in the Elysian Fields, cooked to a nicety and served with tenderly verdant green peas and the most delicious of sweet potatoes. Followed by an apple Charlotte such as only a pastry-cook qualified to wear a halo during life can produce, and not forgetting a savoury of unknown but delicious composition, the flavour of which still lingers pleasantly on my palate. And now coffee, which, to judge by its aroma, is coffee as it should be made, clear, yet dark and strong, and unless 

I am mistaken, brandy of Napoleonic days. Life has its compensations, my dear Alan, and truly the Hawaiian Shipping Federation Incorporated (I believe that is the official title of the owners of the Golden Gate) has, or have, I am not sure which, 'done us proud.'

I am now feeling that I am indeed a hero, and that the bay leaves and laurels will rest lightly on my noble brow."

Alan Armitage, now looking well-groomed and very "English" in white flannels, smiled across the table at him. "You seem to enjoy life so much, Blossom, that at times you make me feel ashamed of being disgruntled," he remarked in his slightly drawling but attractive voice. "I have certainly enjoyed a lunch fit for heroes, but it would not have occurred to me to rhapsodise about it, nor do I feel heroic. And I confess I am still feeling something more than nervous at the thought of having to endure the ordeal of being congratulated and mobbed by a crowd of nit-witted women, and having my hands wrung by big-fisted American business men on vacation. That'll be worse than having to sit, as you remember I did-God knows why!-in drawing-rooms in Washington and Boston and hear 17

people, mainly women, mouthing platitudes about my books; worse even than listening to the drivellings of 

Hollywood directors on 'Art.' In spite of the excellent lunch, Blossom, feelings of nausea predominate when I think of those gangs of female nit-wits with their dyed hair and enamelled nails, surrounding us and gibbering. Ugh!" "Will you have coffee with your brandy, Alan?” enquired Blossom in his best purring manner. "It might help to ward off the nausea. I hope my surmise about this being Napoleon brandy proves correct. 

Sample it, my dear Alan, and let me have your opinion."

People unaware of their identity usually guessed 

Horatio Flower to be a prosperous and benevolent uncle of the younger man, and elderly folk had, on occasion, felt quite shocked when they overheard Alan address his Pickwickian-looking secretary as "Blossom," and issue orders which the tubby cherub with the shiny, bald, pink head, and somewhat prominent and very blue eyes, twinkling behind a pair of unusually large gold-rimmed glasses, seemed delighted to obey. "Judging by the fact, Blossom, that the Golden Gate is now going dead slow, I fancy that we must be very near to Honolulu," resumed Alan Armitage, hoisting his long limbs out of his chair and stretching himself, after sipping his coffee and approving of the cognac. "What do you propose to do about escaping from this threatening hero-worship, not forgetting the pestiferous Pressmen and cameramen that Grant assured us would be waiting for our blood? I can't face it. Get that grey matter inside your pink head working, Blossom, and suggest something." "I suppose I shall have to face the music," responded Blossom, helping himself to another glass of brandy. "I doubt if we could both escape, but if you 18

could borrow a uniform I expect the officers could contrive to get you away unnoticed and unrecognised. 

Perhaps they might be able to put you aboard the yacht, and I could collect you after dark." "A great idea!" cried Alan. "I hope the chief will agree to help me out, and at the same time make your ordeal as light as possible."

As he spoke there was a sharp knock at the cabin door which Blossom opened cautiously, then flung wide to admit the chief officer and the ship's surgeon of the Golden Gate. up "Yes, I think we can fix it, Mr. Armitage," said the chief when Alan had explained his (or, rather, Blossom's) plan of escape. "I guess the captain will have no objection to letting you have the use of one of the ship's boats and I can arrange about the uniform. 

So you've chartered the Southern Glamour for a long cruise-eh? She's a beautiful yacht, but she's been laid here in Honolulu for over a year, and I guess she'll cost quite a lot to fit out. She belonged to Simon 

Leddra, an oil millionaire, who lost his fortune through trying to rig Wall Street at a time when the banks were collapsing like houses of cards, and he must have spent millions of dollars on her. The 

Southern Glamour is a lovely craft, Mr. Armitage, and 

I guess she'll suit your purpose down to the ground, as the saying goes, if you're thinking of writing any more novels or plays during your cruise, because old

Leddra had her fitted with sound-proof cabins. But she'll cost you a lot to fit out and run, as I said before. 

That's your funeral, sir. My immediate job is to get you aboard her. I think I can fix things."

He did. It seemed to the hundreds of passengers waiting to demonstrate that quite an unusual number of men in uniform found it necessary, not only to prevent any of them from even attempting to invade 19

the cabin jointly occupied by "Messrs. A. R. Armitage and H. Flower" (the names which appeared on the passenger list as occupants of the cabin suite), but an unusual number of uniformed men entered and left the cabin soon after the Golden Gate anchored at Honolulu.

So the fact that one man in uniform-one of many -strode out of the cabin, and soon afterwards boarded a boat which had been lowered from the Golden Gate, after the medical officer had given the vessel a clean bill of health, did not attract any particular attention, and no one recognised the tall, lean figure in uniform who dropped into the boat as Alan 

Rodmell Armitage, the famous author and playwright and the "heroic rescuer" of Cyrus O. Cole. 

But Mr. Horatio Flower had a terrific reception when he emerged from the cabin, and, in spite of the attentions of men in uniform, he was mobbed, kissed by women, and carried shoulder high to the Imperial 

Hawaiian Hotel by brawny "tired" American business men, and almost suffocated by wreaths of flowers which were hung round his neck as he was being photographed and interviewed. 20

CHAPTER II 

8 

HE coxswain of the boat from the Golden Gate

TH hailed the Southern Glamour several times without receiving any response as he steered alongside, but eventually a shabby-looking individual appeared on deck of the yacht and demanded to know what in the name of Hades all the commotion was about, and whether the son of a she-dog who was hailing thought that the whole of Honolulu harbour belonged to him; or words to that effect. "Jump to it, you whiskered barnacle," the man in the ship's boat snarled back at him, "and lower a ladder overside double-quick. We've brought your new owner to inspect ship, and if you're a specimen of the crew, God help you-and him!"

Three minutes later Alan Armitage, having left a wad of notes for distribution amongst the boat's crew of the Golden Gate, climbed aboard the S. Y. Southern

Glamour, and revealed his identity to the scrubbybearded individual in the shabby uniform. "So you are Mr. Armitage?" enquired the man who had been addressed as "a whiskered barnacle" and was still smarting from the insult. "My name's Simpson-Samuel Simpson-and I was first officer of the Southern Glamour when she was in commission and am now nominally in charge of her." "Glad to meet you, Mr. Simpson," said Alan, and shook hands formally. "Yes, I'm Alan Rodmell 

Armitage, and you have probably been advised that 

I have chartered the Southern Glamour for a year." 21

"Yes, I had that advice," replied Simpson, "but I'd like to see your credentials."

He was regarding Alan with a sort of surly suspicion, which was to a certain extent excusable in the circumstances. To start with, he had been informed by the agents in San Francisco that Mr. Alan Rodmell 

Armitage was a celebrated author, and Samuel Simpson's conception of an author was a long-haired anæmic-looking individual with a flowing tie, or else an old guy like George Bernard Shaw, pictures of whom he had seen in the illustrated papers. He opined that the tall, lithe and athletic-looking young man in a uniform which obviously had not been made for him was probably an impostor, so he examined Alan's papers with care.

Alan guessed what was passing through his mind, and explained why he had "bolted" from the Golden 

Gate and was wearing a borrowed uniform. "So that's it, is it?" said Simpson, not wholly satisfied, but deciding that he had better be civil and conceal his doubts, as there might be a good job in prospect. "I guess I should apologise for my own get-up, Mr. Armitage. You see the Southern Glamour has been out of commission for well over a twelvemonth, and so, in a way, have I, for I have been little more than a caretaker aboard, keeping the yacht seaworthy with the help of two or three natives and a young Scot who has been keeping the engines in trim. 

The Glamour doesn't look as glamorous as she might, for the agents haven't allowed me much for paint or repairs, but if you are prepared to spend a bit of money on her, she can be made as spick and span as in the days when Simon Leddra owned her and she the smartest thing afloat on the Pacific. You'll want to look her over right away, I suppose?" "Naturally," responded Alan, who had mentally 22

been "sizing up" Mr. Sam Simpson, and had decided that he was probably straight as a die and a highly competent sailorman, and that his grumpiness was probably due entirely to having been out of action too long.

His impression was confirmed, as, with Simpson as guide, he did the rounds of the Southern Glamour, exploring the yacht from stem to stern, for he found 

Simpson ready and willing to answer every question he put to him without hesitation, and found, moreover, that the vessel had been kept meticulously clean both above and below decks. "She looks as if she were fit to put to sea almost at once, Mr. Simpson," remarked Alan, as Simpson ushered him into what was ostensibly the captain's cabin, and waved him towards a seat. "She is," Simpson responded; "but the conditions of your charter stipulate that you have to recondition her. That needn't cost a pile of money, for labour is cheap here, and the owner of a slipway and a dry dock has been going about for months begging for a contract almost with tears in his eyes. I'll help you to figure out the cost right away if you wish. Meanwhile, can I offer you a drink? I've only got lager beer, for I find anything stronger doesn't suit a man with no real hard work to do in this climate." 

Alan accepted a long glass of lager, and he and 

Simpson wished each other luck as they drank. "I've pencilled out some figures here, Mr.

Armitage," said Simpson, producing papers from a locker, "and I think you'll find that I haven't calculated on a millionaire basis. Old man Leddra, of course, never had to think about money before the crash came, and when anything wanted doing on the 

Southern Glamour money didn't matter, and everything was sunshine and harvest for the people who 23

took on the job and super-charged him. Things, of course, will be different now."

He did not mention that he could not understand, to start with, how any author could afford to charter a yacht, his idea being that people who wrote for a living were generally wondering where their next meal was coming from and who they could find to stand them a drink. He remembered an occasion when old 

Simon Leddra had gone "highbrow" and invited a comic bunch of authors and artists for a cruise. They were a fuzzy bunch sure enough, and none of the men resembled in any way the clean-limbed, alert and capable fellow to whom he was talking. It was partly because of that that he was still vaguely wondering whether the man who called himself Alan Rodmell Armitage was not, perhaps, a crook who had stolen the real Armitage's papers and credentials and meant to bluff him and get nominal possession of the Southern Glamour maybe with the idea of using the yacht as a rum-runner or a gun-runner. "Thank you, Mr. Simpson," said Alan. "I'm not exactly hard up, but I am a long way short of being a millionaire, and you are quite right in assuming that 

I shall be unable to run the Southern Glamour on

Leddra lines, if that means that money will be no object." "Money wasn't any object with Simon Leddra, Mr. 

Armitage, except that it was his one and only object in life, unless he was on a cruise aboard this vessel, which was his pride and heart's delight," responded 

Simpson. "Even then, I'm not sure that he left money as his main object behind him when he invited a bunch of the big financiers for a cruise. I'll gamble that deals involving millions were arranged occasionally in those sound-proof cabins that I've shown you during short cruises. Simon Leddra never took the risk of having 24

a man with his ear to the door or keyhole of his private apartment knowing anything of what was going on inside. I guess you could likely write a story about those cabins, Mr. Armitage, if you knew or could conjecture what happened in them." "I am hoping to write a story in one of them, anyhow, and possibly a play, during the time that I shall be nominal owner of the Southern Glamour, but I don't know that it will worry me very much even if anyone discovers a method of listening-in while I am dictating to my secretary. I don't know that the soundproof doors and walls of the cabins will really be an advantage and help me in my work, as a matter of fact, for I have often found that the sound of the sea and the normal activities of the crew, while I am working, are more of a help than a distraction. However, I've chartered the Southern Glamour for a year, and we're going to make the best of things. Let me have a look at those figures you mentioned, Mr. Simpson."

Simpson passed over his little sheaf of papers, and produced two more large bottles of lager from the refrigerator in his cabin. 99 "You'll see I've put down the approximate cost of having the yacht dry-docked and overhauled, and I've tried to reckon roughly the cost of reconditioning,' he explained. "The principal suite, of course, as you probably observed, Mr. Armitage, will require refurnishing and redecorating, except for the—well, we used to call it the 'boudoir,' and I'll tell you between ourselves that it was occupied on some cruises either by one of the lady stars of the Broadway stage or a sweetie from Hollywood. Mr. Leddra had a nice taste in female companions, but his taste changed about twice annually. The boudoir, as you probably noticed, forms part of the main suite which has almost been 25

dismantled. That was because Pa Leddra had Old 

Masters on the walls, Persian carpets about a thousand years old on the floors, slept in an Elizabethan bedstead and used a Buhl cabinet as a dressing-table. The fittings of that suite, sir, realised something like 150,000 dollars after the crash, but I guess you can refurnish for something less than that." "I guess that might be managed, Mr. Simpson,"

Alan agreed, looking up at him smilingly, and then turning his attention again to the figures on the pages before him. "Your estimate is less than I expected. 

What is the shortest time in which you think that, if we get busy to-morrow, the Southern Glamour should be ready for sea?" 99 "Three weeks if they make a hustle job of it-call it a month at the outside-and, if you leave it to me, Mr. Armitage, I'll see that they do get a hustle on, said Simpson. "I haven't been marooned here in 

Honolulu for months without deciding on the right men to do the work and make a good job of it." 

Alan Armitage nodded, apparently still intent upon his study of the figures, then he looked up and smiled. again as he handed back the papers to Sam Simpson. "Our first impressions of each other, Mr. Simpson, were probably all wrong," he remarked. "I'll apologise for having put you down at first sight as a beachcomber who by some means had got a job as caretaker of this yacht, and you need not apologise if you put me down as some sort of crook who had pinched a uniform and the credentials of Alan Armitage." ... "You must be a thought-reader, Mr. Armitage," commented Sam Simpson with a sudden laugh. “I'll admit I was suspicious, but I realise I was wrong. . .

Now let's get down to brass tacks. I want a job, and when you've reconditioned the Southern Glamour I'd like to sail her for you. What about it, sir?" 26

"You have a Master's certificate?" "Sure! a Master's certificate, and you can ask anyone whether or not Samuel Simpson knows the Islands from A to Z. It doesn't matter to me whether you want a cruise for a year or ten years, I'm your man if you care to sign me on. And I'd ask you not to judge me by what I look like at present. I've been saving money on uniform and shaving expenses."

Alan Armitage rose suddenly to his feet and held out his slim, sinewy, brown hand. "Captain Simpson, shake hands with your new governor, and consider it settled that to-morrow, if you wish it, we shall have a formal contract drawn up, making you skipper of the yacht for the next twelve months," he said. "Now, to get down to those brass tacks you mentioned," he resumed, after Sam Simpson had almost dislocated his hand by the intensity of his grip. "I want you to get busy immediately on having the Southern Glamour reconditioned at prices approximating to your own figures, and to arrange to have her ready for sea within a month. I leave it to you to sign on a suitable crew who will serve you and me faithfully and well, and I leave it to you, also, to see that the cooks and the stewards you engage know their job. I might mention that I have a secretary, to wit, one Horatio Flower, otherwise Blossom, who is a connoisseur of cooking, food and wine, even if he is not a connoisseur of seamanship, and I fear he might go on strike during the cruise unless the catering arrangements are beyond reproach." "You seem to be talking a bit daft, sir, or else you are trying to pull my leg, but I guess we're going to get on all right together and you and your Blossom will have no cause for complaint," said Captain Simpson. "I think I can definitely promise that I can sign on two of the best cooks in the whole of the Hawaiian 27

Islands. If you like, Mr. Blossom can subject them to the third degree before we sail." "So that's that!" Alan heaved a sigh of relief and lit a cigarette. "I think you will like Blossom, Mr.

Simpson. He is really a nice little man-although he is not really little around the middle. By the way, do you ever drink whisky!" "On occasion, Mr. Armitage," replied Simpson, wondering if this was possibly a new "leg-pull" on the part of the tall Englishman. "Why?" "Well, never attempt to keep pace with Blossom.

I could no more think of entrusting the vessel to the care of a skipper who can consume whisky to the extent of Mr. Horatio Flower than of trying to fly from here to San Francisco in an aeroplane with a broken wing, a faulty engine, and a demented pilot. 

Yet, I do not wish, captain, to give you a wrong impression of Mr. Flower, otherwise Blossom." "You mean, I suppose, that he is never unpleasant, even when he is drunk?" "I mean to say, captain, that he is never drunk, no matter how much whisky he consumes. Which reminds me that we shall have to reckon very carefully, when we are putting out to sea, just how many dozen cases we shall have to include-I means cases of whisky-in the manifest." "Some drinker, I'll say!" commented the new skipper of the Southern Glamour, still wondering if his leg was being pulled, for it naturally seemed absurd to him that Armitage should employ a secretary whose chief accomplishments seemed to be eating and drinking. "I shall look forward to meeting Mr. Horatio 

Flower, otherwise Blossom." "Which reminds me, that Blossom pledged himself to send a boat here to collect me after nightfall and 28

will probably come in it himself, bringing, I hope, the 

Imp with him," said Alan. "The Imp?" repeated Simpson. "What sort of 

Imp? Is it a woman?"

Alan sat down and laughed unaffectedly. "No, my dear Simpson," he answered, "the Imp is not-emphatically not-a woman. He is a pedigree 

Sealyham terrier whose full name, duly registered at the Kennel Club, is Rodmell Homestall King, but I only call him by that name when he is showing off or being disobedient, and normally I content myself with calling him—as he is content to be called-Imp, just as I content myself with calling Mr. Horatio Flower

Blossom, which, by the way, is merely a native translation of his surname." 99 "Excuse me if I say again it sounds a bit daft," commented Simpson, after a pause for consideration. "Now, lemme see? There's yourself, nominal owner and governor, who has chartered the Southern 

Glamour. There's Mr. Horatio Flower, who you call 

Blossom-two. There's-can I get the name right?— 

Rodmell Homestall King, that you call Imp and that

I thought was perhaps a woman, but turns out to be a Sealyham dog." "And I can assure you, Mr. Simpson, that Imp is the finest Sealyham you ever saw in your life, and he has probably been breaking his heart all the way from 

San Francisco to here and wondering in his doggy mind why the hell his boss only came and saw him once, or, possibly, twice a day. The steamship people, you know, won't allow dogs in the cabins, but they look after them well during the voyage." "Well, I've only reckoned two men and a dog so far," remarked Simpson. "What about your wives and families, and servants and such-like? How many will your party comprise altogether, Mr. Armitage ?" 29

"As far as I know, just two men and a dog," replied

Alan, laughing again. “There are no wives or families, no servants and not even any 'such-likes.' Blossom and 

I are both bachelors without what are known as encumbrances, and we roam the world and sail the Seven Seas just as the fancy drives me. It may seem a daft idea to you that I should charter a yacht just for myself, my secretary, and my dog, without any very definite idea of where I want to go, but I do damn'fool things like that, and the Southern Glamour was on offer at a low figure, so I chartered her with option of purchase. As far as I am concerned, captain, there is no woman in the case, and I think I can rely upon Blossom being sufficiently clever to escape from the clutches of the women who, at the present moment, are probably lionising, idealising and adoring him, and to avoid any possibility of their trying to embark with him on the Southern Glamour in a month's time."

Simpson asked questions, and a few more explanations ensued which decided him, more or less, that Alan Rodmell Armitage was not much interested in women, and was altogether rather a "queer stick." 

Several hours later he decided that Mr. Horatio 

Flower, otherwise Blossom, was a still queerer stick when he came alongside late in the evening in a boat rowed by natives and heaved his balloon-like body up the side of the yacht with surprising agility. "I've borne the heat and burden of the day, Alan," 

Blossom announced. "I've faced the music, and now the tumult and the shouting have died and it is imperative that you come ashore with me forthwith, or, at the latest, before midnight, and meet Mr. Cyrus 

Oke Cole's little girl, who has personally assured me that she will die unless she sees the man who saved her daddy. And I promise you, my dear Alan, that Mr.

Oke's little daughter will be the surprise of your life. 30

Don't have any qualms. It will be no ordeal to meet her. Only a surprise. And I refuse to tell you more. 

Have you any whisky, captain ?" "Sorry, sir, nothing but lager beer, and Mr.

Armitage and I have practically finished my supply of that while we have been waiting for you," replied 

Simpson, surveying Horatio appraisingly. "Incidentally, are you a thought-reader as well as Mr. 

Armitage? I am wondering how you knew, or guessed, that I have been appointed captain." 

"My dear sir, even a landlubber like myself could not mistake you for anything else but a captain,' 

Blossom responded, and shot an enquiring glance at 

Alan. "I hope I have not been guilty of a faux pas?” "My fault for not formally introducing you," said 

Alan quickly. "Let me repair the omission. . . . Captain 

Samuel Simpson-Mr. Horatio Flower." "Delighted to meet you, Captain Simpson, even if you can offer nothing stronger than lager," exclaimed 

Blossom, shaking hands warmly. "However, let us pledge ourselves in beer." "Blossom, what is this mystery regarding Mr. Cole's little daughter?" demanded Alan, regarding Horatio keenly and challengingly as the skipper produced bottles and glasses. "I know by the evil twinkle in your eye that you have been up to some devilment or contemplate some devilment. If you have agreed to my receiving some sort of ovation when I go ashore with you I'll wring your fat neck." "My dear Alan, I assure you that I have arranged you shall be smuggled up to the apartments of Mr. 

Cyrus Cole and his daughter, and that I merely planned what I hope will be a pleasant surprise. Please don't ask me to tell you more, or you may deprive yourself of a thrill." "My idea of a pleasant surprise and a thrill is not 31

visiting a dying—or presumably dying-child at midnight, and I am perfectly sure that you have planned some sort of devilment," commented Alan, still looking hard at Blossom, who shook his head as he gulped down the lager beer. "I am perfectly convinced, my dear Alan, that the little girl will not die after she has seen you," he said. "I have pledged my word of honour that I will take you to the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel to see her before midnight, and I know you will not fail me.

Besides Mr. Cyrus O. Cole's little daughter, Imp is awaiting you at the hotel and is probably wondering if his master is dead. Incidentally, I managed to dodge the quarantine regulations on Imp's behalf by doing some noble lying and explaining that we should be sailing with him in the Southern Glamour almost immediately. The poor brute must be craving to see you." "Humph! that settles it, Blossom, but I like to know just what the surprise is that you seem to have planned for me," Alan retorted. "I've got a suspicion that you may be trying to pay me out for having allowed you to endure the ordeal of being lionised instead of facing it myself." "Not guilty, m'lord," pleaded Blossom with a beaming smile. "I've even engaged a small suite at the hotel to which you can gain access without going through the main door, and you will have to endure nothing more than the thanks of Mr. Cyrus Cole and his little girl. All the same, I think I can promise you a surprise. 

Shall we get ashore now? Remember I promised the little girl faithfully that the Fairy Prince who had rescued her father would appear by midnight." 32

CHAPTER III 

83 

ALAN 

LAN still had an uneasy suspicion as he climbed down into the boat, after arranging a meeting with the captain of the Southern Glamour ashore next day, that Blossom was planning to play some sort of practical joke on him, for Horatio still seemed, as it were, to be chuckling inwardly. "Have you, by any chance, Blossom, since I left you, discovered a liquor which has intoxicating effects upon you?" he enquired. "If I did not know you so well I should imagine that you are tipsy." "What a dreadful idea, my dear Alan!" exclaimed 

Blossom, and almost giggled as he spoke. "I admit I have been imbibing, for the hospitality of Cyrus Oke Cole is boundless, but it is not liquor which has had an exhilarating effect and made me feel that I want to laugh and sing like the jolly natives who are rowing the boat. I think the tropic air has got into my blood already. Do you not feel the glamorous enchantment of it yourself? Is not an almost unbelievably wonderful night?"

It was. The phosphorescent waters of the bay, from which the Kanakas seemed to throw up handfuls of gleaming pearls every time they dipped their oars, were calm almost as the proverbial millpond. Across the still waters floated faintly the sound of melodious music, to which the multi-coloured lights on the shore seemed to be dancing, while the very air itself, even on the water, was heavy with the perfume of tropical flowers. Above, the stars, looking like great electric globes, leered, blinked and laughed, rather than 33

twinkled, in a sky which looked as if it were composed of ebon-black velvet. "It certainly is a glorious night," admitted Alan, "but I'm afraid the tropical glamour has had no particular effect upon me so far-unless that is what is responsible for your inducing me to agree to visit a dying child at midnight. However, you will remember that I have been engaged for hours discussing beer and business with Captain Sam Simpson, who is, I hope, a highly efficient sailorman but does not appear to have any romantic tendencies. Now tell me, Blossom, what exactly is it that I am expected to do when we get to the hotel?" "Nothing much beyond allowing Cyrus Cole and his little daughter to thank you, and say a few nice things, perhaps, to the girl, to take her mind off the fact that her life is in danger. I have had everything laid out in readiness for you in the suite booked for us, and you can slip in there and change as soon as we get to the hotel." "Why should I change?" "Well, I don't think any little girl would expect a fairy prince to be wearing a shabby and ill-fitting uniform, and I thought you had an aversion to wearing another man's clothes. Besides that, you will remember that you promised to return the uniform to the Golden Gate at the earliest possible moment.

Incidentally, that crowd of maniacs from the boat nearly tore my clothes off as they carried me to the hotel, and I was profoundly glad to escape and get into another rig." "Poor old Blossom!" murmured Alan. "When I think of what you must have had to go through I feel quite guilty at grumbling at the thought of having a private reunion with Mr. Cyrus Cole, and accepting 34

the thanks of his little daughter. So I'll go through with it, and for once in a way do exactly as you tell me."

There was a car in waiting on the waterside, and within ten minutes or so Alan had been "smuggled" by a side staircase to his suite in the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel, which looked like a palace transported from fairyland. "Where's Imp?" he demanded, as soon as he entered. "I left him in charge of the little girl, to whom he took an immediate fancy, and who took an immediate fancy to him the moment he was brought ashore and they were introduced," explained Blossom. "Imp definitely refused to leave her and accompany me when I wanted to bring him to you aboard the Southern 

Glamour, so you see, my dear Alan, you would have had to visit the girl in any case in order to retrieve your dog. Incidentally, never before have I seen Imp attach himself to a stranger at first glance, as it were. 

He simply leapt into the girl's arms." "Queer!" commented Alan, as he discarded his borrowed uniform. "Seems almost as if the tropic air had affected Rodmell Homestall King as it has 

Horatio Flower! . . . What do you suggest as the correct garb in which to visit a sick child at midnight, and retrieve one's dog, Blossom?" "I am sure the little girl will expect her Fairy 

Prince to be in full evening dress at least," Blossom answered, so Alan, mindful of his promise to obey instructions, proceeded to don "glad-rags." "This way," said Blossom a few minutes later. "If we hurry I shall be able to redeem my promise to produce you on the stroke of midnight. Mr. Cyrus

Cole's suite is on the floor below." 

They descended the stairs and Horatio tapped on a door, which was almost immediately opened by a 35

lanky, red-headed individual in a dinner-suit, in which he flattered himself he looked a "real gentleman,' but actually he looked more like the "chucker-out" at a Bowery dance saloon. Behind him, seated at a table, from which Alan noticed he picked up a pistol as he rose to his feet, was a dark, thick-set man also in a dinner-suit, and although he wore it with something of an "air" he also did not look "the goods." "This, Alan, is Mr. Connolly," explained Blossom quickly. "The other gentleman behind him is-er 

Mr.-er-Powell. This, gentlemen, is Mr. Alan 

Armitage, whom, as you know, Mr. Cole and his daughter are eagerly awaiting." "Well, I'll take your word for it, but I take no risks," responded Joe Connolly, who had been eyeing

Alan keenly and suspiciously from under his shaggy red brows, and without moving aside from the doorway. "Spike," he added over his shoulder, "tell Mr. 

Cole that Mr.-Flower, ain't it?-has come back and brought a gentleman he says is Mr. Armitage with him." "What sort of reception is this?" Alan enquired frowningly of Blossom as "Spike" obeyed instructions. "Are armed men needed to guard access to the little girl?" "Apparently," Blossom answered, still looking_as if he were inwardly enjoying some rare joke. “You see- 99 He got no further, for at that moment the inner door was flung open and Mr. Cyrus O. Cole dashed out, followed by Imp, yelping with excitement. Both hurled themselves at Alan. Mr. Cole flung his arms around him and might likely have kissed him had he been tall enough. Imp barked joyously, tried to climb up his legs, and "kissed" his hands and his face as

Alan picked him up from the floor. 36

"Mr. Armitage," gasped Cyrus Cole, "how-how can I thank you? I owe you my life, and—oh, I cannot find words. Come in, sir, come in. Perhaps my little girl will be able to find words to thank you and bless you.' 99 He was genuinely moved and tears were streaming from his eyes as he drew Alan through the open door leading to the inner apartments. "My dear Mr. Cole, your thanks are due to 

Blossom-to Mr. Flower-rather than me," said

Coming towards him with hands outstretched and eyes sparkling with unaffected pleasure was one of the most gloriously beautiful women he had ever seen. "Mr. Armitage, this is my little girl," announced 

Cyrus Cole unsteadily. "Gloria, darling"

Before Alan could recover from his astonishment, the "little girl" impulsively threw her arms round his neck and kissed him. "Mr. Armitage, I shall love and thank and bless you all my life for saving my father," she said in a sweet, slightly husky voice, smiling into his eyes. "Thank you, and thank you, and thank you again." 

So unexpected was the salute, and so overcome was

Alan by surprise and embarrassment, that he blushed furiously and had to suppress a desire to curse Blossom as he heard the latter chuckle. Imp relieved the situation by almost leaping out of his arms, licking the girl's face and then barking joyously again, thus giving

Alan an opportunity to recover himself. "I am surely more than repaid by your kiss, Miss

Cole," Alan said, setting down Imp on the floor and giving him an affectionate slap. 37

"Say, Gloria darling, I think it was just sweet of you to kiss Mr. Armitage," chimed in Cyrus Cole. "I wanted to kiss him myself but I guess he'll be better satisfied that you've acted as my deputy. I might have known that my little girl would rise to the occasion. . . . Gloria, dear, persuade Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower to sit down and tell you all about it again while I open up some champagne." "Do please sit down, Mr. Armitage, and make yourself comfortable," begged Gloria, indicating a settee placed by an open window giving access to a small balcony overlooking the bay. "I won't ask you to tell me all about your spendidly brave act, for I know in advance you would say in your modest and delightful English way that it was nothing at all, and that rescuing a man from the shark-infested sea is a thing hardly worth mentioning.' "Thank you, Miss Cole," said Alan, who had now completely recovered his self-possession, and felt somewhat inclined to laugh as Imp jumped up beside him and growled applause. "I have been more than sufficiently thanked already and the joke is on me." "Joke?" repeated Cyrus Cole's "little girl," seating herself at the opposite end of the settee and smiling at him. "I don't understand, and it somehow seems quite strange to be called 'Miss Cole.' Here, at present, everyone knows me as Inez Fernandez." "Say, let me explain this, Gloria," chimed in her father again, handing round champagne and beaming at everyone through his rimless pince-nez, which he wore attached to a long black silk ribbon, which somehow gave him quite a distinguished air despite his lack of inches. "I am sure Mr. Armitage must be puzzled." "I am puzzled, Mr. Cole," said Alan smilingly, "and I suspect some sort of conspiracy. On board the 38

Golden Gate you told me about your little girl's life being in danger, and to-night Mr. Flower gave me the impression that I was coming here to visit a dying child, but at the same time, I must confess, promised me a surprise. If Miss Gloria is the surprise, she is certainly the most charming and delightful one which has ever been sprung on me."

He laughed outright as Imp again growled and barked applause, and then hopped across the settee to "kiss" Gloria's hands. "My dear Alan, I know you will have no difficulty in forgiving me," broke in Blossom, with a beaker of champagne in his hand. "I got the most delightful surprise this afternoon when Mr. Cole introduced me to his little girl, and I thought you were entitled to share the experience. Before we go into explanations 

I propose that we drink to the health of the pleasant surprise and her delightful father." "And Pop and I will sure be delighted to drink to the health of two very gallant gentlemen," said the "little girl" in her rich, slightly husky, attractive voice, as she rose to her feet. "Fill yourself a glass, Pop, and we'll drink 'Aloha Oe' to Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower. 

In case you don't know, 'Aloha Oe' means 'My love to you.'" "Aloha Oe! Aloha Oe! Aloha Oe!" cried Mr.

Cole, hurriedly filling a glass and draining it, before picking up a magnum and insisting upon refilling all the glasses. "Dear, dear gentlemen, I'm so excited and overjoyed that I hardly know what I am saying or doing." "You ought to be in bed, Pop," commented Gloria. "Better sit down, calm yourself and let me do the explaining."

Alan, from the opposite end of the settee, studied her appraisingly as she smiled at him. She was cer- 39

tainly a very, very beautiful creature, perfectly formed and proportioned, indubitably gifted with that undefinable something that we call "charm" or "glamour."

Her chestnut hair had the sheen of a chestnut newly taken from its burr, and she had the most wonderfully expressive eyes, velvety-brown when she was placid, yet seeming to gleam almost like dark rubies when she became animated or excited. 'Actually, I have three names at present, Mr. 

Armitage," she resumed—and she, for her part, was studying the long-limbed, good-looking Englishman, much as he was studying her, and with equal approval. "My real name is Mary Drake Cole, but the world knows me better as Gloria Drake." "Ha!" ejaculated Blossom suddenly. "Now I know why I was so certain I had seen you somewhere before! Gloria Drake, the world-famous film star! Alan, my dear man, you cannot have forgotten that Gloria

Drake played the leading part in the film version of your novel Ashes of Vengeance?" "Of course, I remember, Blossom," said Alan. "It is most interesting to know that we have, in a way, previously been associated. And although Hollywood made a botch of my story, I feel quite sure that Gloria Drake made an even more attractive heroine than my original character." "Do you always hand out compliments like that, Mr. Armitage?" laughed Gloria, still studying him. "Actually, it would not have surprised me if you had said you had never heard of Gloria Drake." "You do me an injustice," retorted Alan, and was annoyed by the knowledge that his face had reddened again. "Of course, all the world knows, or knows of, Gloria Drake." am a good American, and if ever you come to my old home in Virginia I can show you a pedigree that proves that the Coles and the Okes and the Drakes all intermarried. Cole and Oke, as you probably know, are very old Devon family names, although I guess 'Oke' means something different on this side nowadays from what it does in Devon." "Oke, Pop, you said it!" exclaimed Gloria laughingly. "But you needn't worry so long as the name means you're O.K.” "Don't be frivolous, child," rebuked her father, laughing himself. "Let me explain to Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower that we are actually distant relations of Sir Francis Drake, and that is what gave me the notion of calling you Gloria Drake when you made your first appearance at Hollywood nearly seven years ago, when you were only sixteen years, and scored a world-beating success almost at the first shot." Van Rosen," interposed Gloria, with a slight frown. "I don't suppose Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower are really interested, but you can tell them that you followed the example of some of your ancestors and married a Spanish lady, although you met her in New

Hollywood was terrorised. Anna Steed, as you know, was kidnapped from her house in Beverly Hills, although the place was patrolled by armed guards, and was found more dead than alive after her friends had paid a ransom of fifty thousand dollars. The police guessed that Dandy Hagen and his gang were responsible, but they couldn't fix anything on them." "So when I got letters and telephone calls threatening that unless I paid fifty thousand dollars I should be treated like Anna was, and like Lionel Broughton, who was found with his face disfigured, I got panicstricken," Gloria added. arranged her disappearance," supplemented Mr. Cole. Powell is an ex-Pinkerton man, and Joe Connolly had the job of guarding one or two of our leading politicians when their lives were certainly in danger. you, Imp?" chortled Horatio, bending to pat the

CHAPTER IV 

83 ACK in his own suite, Alan flung himself down in a big easy chair with a sigh of relief, stretched out his long legs and lit a cigarette. Imp immediately jumped up beside him and burrowed down into the chair at his hip, resting his chin on Alan's leg and looking up at him with shining, worshipful eyes through untidy white hair. Blossom helped himself to a whisky and soda before depositing himself in another armchair facing Alan and beaming at him with something of the same kind of devotion in his blue eyes as that reflected in the brown eyes of the Sealyham. "Well?" he queried. "Is it well, O master mine?" "I think both of you deserve a darn' good scolding and I have half a mind to wring your fat neck, as I threatened, Blossom, and to spank Imp," responded

Alan. "Rodmell Homestall King and yourself appear to me to have become infatuated with an astonishingly beautiful young woman from Hollywood, and to have conspired together to make me feel-and lookridiculous." "My dear Alan," began Blossom, then made a spluttering noise as he choked over his whisky and soda, whereupon Imp began to bark protestingly, jumped down from Alan's chair and growled in mock menace. "Shut up, both of you," continued Alan. "You are both traitors and have betrayed me. I was led to believe that I was going to do a good deed like a Boy 

Scout by visiting a dying child, and I find Cyrus Oke

Cole's 'little girl' is a film vamp, very much alive." 46

"She kissed you," chortled Blossom. "It would not surprise me to learn that there are many men who would give much to be kissed by the glamorous and beauteous Gloria Drake. She did not kiss me when her father introduced me, but she kissed Imp." "Here, I say, why do you keep dragging my name into this affair?" growled and barked Imp, looking from one to the other, his head cocked amusingly on one side. "Please, I haven't done anything wrong, have I?" "Rodmell Homestall King, you are as bad as

Blossom," said Alan severely, whereupon Imp drooped his ears and tail and cringed. When his master called him Rodmell Homestall King it generally meant he was annoyed with him about something, so Imp thought it politic to lick the toe of Alan's shoe. "Yes, you're quite as bad as Blossom, even although you have more sense than to mix whisky and soda with champagne, but you appeared to have transferred your affections to the film vamp and were obviously reluctant to leave her. You and Blossom both seem to have fallen for the film star, and have become a pair of 

Gloria Drake fans." "Please, master, I never meant any harm," Imp barked, and then, presumably to show that he was sincere and supremely sorry for anything he had done, he took a flying leap onto his master's snowy shirt-front and vigorously licked his face. "Bah! get down you brute," cried Alan, jumping to his feet and spilling the Sealyham back on to the floor. "Your kisses are even worse than those of Gloria 

Drake. You had better go and kiss Blossom, who seems peeved that the film star didn't kiss him." 

Rodmell Homestall King didn't know what to do about the matter at all, so he gave another protesting yelp, crossed the room haughtily and curled himself up 47

on a mat close by the door, closing his eyes firmly to show his disdain. "Well, I confess I have fallen for Cyrus Oke Cole's 'little girl,'" admitted Blossom, and one could almost have sworn that he blushed, although his face was so pink that an extra shade of pale red would have made very little difference. "I think Gloria Drake is the most entrancingly beautiful girl I have ever seen in my life, and your affectation of disgust that she should have kissed you, Alan, astounds me. You are not going to try to maintain that you are totally indifferent to feminine charms ?" "Having reached the age of thirty-four without having allowed myself to be entangled into matrimony, or made a fool of myself over any woman, I'm not in the least likely to fall victim to the charms of a film vamp, if that is what you mean," Alan replied. "I admit she is a beautiful creature, but you should know by this time, Blossom, that I have no time for women, and that the much-boasted 'It' and 'Sex Appeal' make me sick. 

However, you certainly gave me the surprise you promised, and I hope you are pleased with the success of your joke." 

Blossom's smile faded, the twinkle died out of his blue eyes and he regarded Alan bleakly. "I am sorry you are vexed with me, Alan," he said disconsolately. "I did actually think you would enjoy my pleasant surprise, and enjoy meeting Gloria Drake. 

Personally, I think she is the most charming and attractive girl I have ever met. But perhaps I am only an old fool." "My dear Blossom, don't take me too seriously, and don't look so doleful. I am pleased to have met Gloria 

Drake, and quite agree with you as to her beauty and charm-two very necessary aids to success on the screen. I think I shall cultivate her acquaintance while 48

we are here with a view to analysing her personality and using the result in the new play. It strikes me it would be quite a good idea to make one of the leading female characters a film vamp who, in private life, is chaste and virginal, although, I suppose, that would seem far-fetched to Hollywood." "Don't be so damned cynical, and don't talk so callously about 'analysing' the girl as if you were going to put an insect under the microscope and then dissect it. I have it on the authority of her father himself, as well as Max Van Rosen, her agent and manager, that there has never been the faintest breath or suspicion of scandal in connection with the name of Gloria Drake, nor has her name at any time been coupled with that of any man. Van Rosen told me confidentially that although she does not know it, she has been nicknamed in the studios 'The Virgin Vamp.'' 

"Why, that would make quite a good film title," laughed Alan, stretching himself. "Well, well, my dear 

Blossom, you have my permission to sit at the feet of the Virgin Vamp and worship her, if you feel so disposed." "Don't be sarcastic," protested Blossom, still looking unusually solemn. "You know quite well that although I still love romance and adventure I shall never see fifty again, and these days I regard a beautiful woman with only the same emotions as I admire a beautiful picture or piece of sculpture. But somehow Miss 

Gloria reminds me of a girl who once meant everything in life to me the one and only woman. True, she brought disaster, Alan, you had to pick me up from the depths, but somehow"

Horatio waved his plump hands vaguely, rose with a heavy sigh to help himself to another whisky and soda and then resumed his chair. "If Gloria Drake reminds you of the tragedy that 49

we made a compact never to talk about, Blossom, I don't know why there should have been any gladness in the meeting, or why you reproach me for being cynical," remarked Alan. "Just to help you to get back your sense of proportion, let me remind you of a bit of 

Kipling you once quoted to me: "A fool there was and he made his prayer (Even as you and I!)

To a rag and a bone and a hank of hair (We called her the woman who did not care) 

But the fool he called her his lady fair- (Even as you and I!)'"

Blossom sat down for a few minutes twisting his hands together and looking distressed. "I don't think, Alan," he said at last in a thin voice, "that the Virgin Vamp is that sort of creature, and I think-I think-oh, hell! I suppose we'd better go to bed. I've drunk more than usual to-day, and as a result things that I've prayed God to let me forget have come back into my mind-the days that were, the things that might have been, and the ghastly thought of what sort of object I might be to-day if you had not lifted me out of the depths and made me a man again." "I certainly think that perhaps we'd better go to bed, Blossom," responded Alan uncomfortably, after a pause. "You've suffered a lot to-day, partly on my account, and now the reaction has set in, you are not quite yourself." He stepped across and laid his hand on Blossom's shoulder. "Blossom, old man, it still puzzles me occasionally to understand why you are content to be merely my secretary and faithful companion, guide, philosopher and friend." catch "I ask nothing better," Blossom replied with in his voice. "When you restored my manhood I offered 50

to devote my life to your service, and I do it gladly. 

I ask no other life-no better life-and I wish to serve no other master."

He bowed his head in his hands for a few moments, then suddenly sprang to his feet with his usual alacrity, forcing a smile. "Perhaps," he added, "it is just as well that there is unlikely to be any woman in the case as far as you are concerned, my dear Alan, for I have a sort of idea that if you were so ill-advised as to fall in love and get married, your wife certainly would not approve of me, even if you assured her, as you have assured me many times, that I am the 'perfect secretary.'" "My dear Blossom, the very thought of losing my perfect secretary will be sufficient at any time to banish from my mind all thoughts of matrimony," cried Alan kindly, patting Blossom on the shoulder again. "But I think we are both inclined to give way to maudlin sentiment. The obvious antidote is sleep. Let's forget the days that were and the things that might have been, and think, rather, of what the future may have in store for us.' 99 "The right perspective again, Alan," commented 

Blossom. "And so to bed. . . 

He insisted on seeing Alan to his bedroom and assuring himself that Alan was comfortable before retiring to his own apartment. Rodmell Homestall King acted as escort and investigated every corner of his master's bedroom with loud sniffs, while Alan divested himself of his dress clothes, donned pyjamas and a dressing-gown, and sat down by the open window to think and plan and dream as he gazed out over the phosphorescent waters of Honolulu bay. 

The moon had risen and in the garden below he could see waves of brilliant hibiscus, blue jacaranda and royal-red poincianas, while croton bushes and poin- 51

settia foamed over walls and walks, and the fragrance of all wafted up to him on the soft tropical night air. 

He felt as if he were living in a waking dream, a dream in which, somehow, the tragic love story of Horatio Flower became intermingled and entangled with the fact that he, Alan Rodmell Armitage, had been kissed on the lips by a very beautiful woman. "Imp, my lad, this won't do!" he exclaimed suddenly, springing to his feet and shaking himself, whereupon Imp, who had been asleep at his feet, sprang up, uttered one staccato bark, and looked at him enquiringly with his head on one side as usual. "I mean," continued Alan, "that we cannot possibly allow Cyrus O. Cole's little girl to be a disturbing factor in our lives, can we? Let's go to bed, sleep, and return to sanity in the morning. You can have the chair, or you can sleep at the foot of the bed-and remember, my lad, I said at the foot of the bed. None of your nonsense such as trying to get under the clothes and pretending you are freezing on a tropical night. I think you'd better take the chair."

Mr. Rodmell Homestall King considered the matter, twisting his square head from side to side and looking from his master's face to the indicated chair, then he sat down and vigorously scratched his ear, a proceeding which any gentleman dog will tell you is absolutely essential if one has to do any hard thinking. 

The fact that Alan got into bed decided him that the bed was the only place fit for a Sealyham to sleep on, and he took a flying leap just as his beloved master switched off the light.

Alan was dallying with iced grapefruit, mangoes, coffee and biscuits about eight o'clock in the morning when the inter-room telephone on his table rang. "Is that Rodmell Armitage?" queried a sweet, 52

slightly husky voice which he recognised instantly. "Say, Mr. Armitage, would you care to come bathing with me on Waikiki Beach just below us ?" "Thank you, Miss Gloria ; incidentally, I think it will simplify matters if I address you as Miss Gloria, instead of having to decide whether to address you as 

Miss Cole or Miss Fernandez, although I suppose you will prefer to be addressed in company as Miss Fernandez in order to preserve your incognito ?" "You can call me Gloria or Inez, whichever you prefer," came the answer over the wire. "What about bathing with me?" "Thanks all the same, Gloria or Inez, but I bathed nearly an hour ago and am now enjoying breakfast,”

Alan responded in his cool, slightly drawling voice. "Perhaps we might bathe together this evening before dinner, if I happen to be free then. Possibly we may happen to see each other during the course of the day.' 99 "And possibly not," replied Gloria or Inez in an offended voice, and hung up the receiver. It was a new experience for her to invite a man to be her companion for an hour or so and to be coolly and casually informed, in effect, that he had no time for her, and Gloria was piqued. She said as much to Max Van 

Rosen when the latter joined her and her father at breakfast. "The trouble with these Englishmen, Miss Gloria, is that they haven't got any real red warm American blood in their veins," said Van Rosen soothingly and sententiously. "No live man would even think of refusing to bathe with you, even if he'd bathed half a dozen times before receiving your invitation." "I guess, Max, there isn't much wrong with Mr. 

Rodmell Armitage's blood," drawled Mr. Cyrus O.

Cole. "I didn't notice any big rush of red-blooded 

Americans to jump overboard and pull me out of the 53

sea. Say, Gloria, darling, perhaps Mr. Armitage had enough of sea-bathing yesterday, when he rescued your 

Pop, to last him for some time.' "I hadn't thought of that, Pop," admitted Gloria. "Perhaps it is stupid of me to feel as if I had been snubbed." "Say, I've got an idea, darling," said her father, pushing back his chair from the table and lighting one of his long, thin, black cigars. "We'll invite Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower to lunch with us. We'd better arrange for it here in your private suite so that Connolly and Powell need not be on guard at the table, as I suppose they would have to be if we lunched in the restaurant. I dare say the hotel chefs will be able to serve up something very special in the way of a luncheon if we put them on their mettle." "I guess I can fix that, Mr. Cole," said Van Rosen, rising. "If Miss Gloria is agreeable, I'll take the message myself to Mr. Armitage. I want to make his acquaintance." "I'm quite agreeable," Gloria said with a slight pout, "but it will not greatly surprise me if Mr. Armitage declines the invitation, pretending that he has important work to do." "Leave it to me," said Van Rosen, in his selfconfident way.

A few minutes later he tapped at the door of Alan's sitting-room, which was opened by Horatio Flower, looking even pinker and more cherubic than ever, in a tight-fitting, snowy-white suit. Van Rosen shook hands and in a few words explained his mission. "Come in, come in," exclaimed Blossom, and turned to Alan, who had appeared from his own room. "Alan, this is Mr. Max Van Rosen, Miss Gloria's manager and publicity agent.' "Pleased to meet you, Mr. Van Rosen, "said Alan 54

formally, as he shook hands with the sleek, dark, good-looking young Jewish-American. "It must be an onerous business managing a film star, I should imagine, and a great responsibility?" "At present it is a very great responsibility, Mr. 

Armitage, for I am responsible for Miss Gloria's life and safety," responded Van Rosen, studying Alan closely with his bright, beady, black eyes. "But normally my work is a pleasure, for Gloria Drake is not a temperamental genius, ready to quarrel or go into hysterics over trifles, and she doesn't give even those who are jealous of her any opportunity for scandalous gossip nor make a sort of hobby of getting married and divorced twice annually, like some Hollywood stars. She has a lovely nature, as well as a fascinating and beautiful personality, and she is sure the wonder-girl of the screen." "I perceive, Mr. Van Rosen, that you are certainly a good publicity man," commented Alan smilingly. "I guess I know my business better than any other man in Hollywood, or, for that matter, in the whole of the United States," responded Van Rosen modestly. "Talking of publicity, Mr. Armitage, the papers have got the story of your brave rescue of Cyrus Oke Cole, but I want to ask you, when you are interviewed by the reporters to-day-as you certainly will be-not to reveal the fact that Miss Inez Fernandez and Gloria Drake are one and the same person, and that Mr. 

Cole has come here to visit his daughter. You understand the position?" "Quite!" said Alan, still smiling. "I shall have nothing to say to the Pressmen, and am relying upon 

Mr. Flower to save me from them." "You can leave that to me," said Blossom, who had been arranging his typewriter on a table by the window and unpacking papers from a dispatch-case. 55

"Good!" exclaimed Van Rosen, and proceeded to deliver the invitation to luncheon. "Sorry," drawled Alan, shaking his head. "I shall be engaged all morning with Mr. Samuel Simpson, the captain of the yacht I have chartered, and I have invited him to lunch with me, so I must regretfully decline Mr. Cole's kind invitation." "There will be contracts to be made out and legal business transacted, as well as arrears of correspondence to be dealt with," chimed in Blossom, seating himself at his typewriter as a broad hint to Mr. Max 

Van Rosen. "Well, accept an invite to dinner this evening," persisted Van Rosen pressingly. "I understand you and

Miss Gloria have arranged to bathe together before dinner, and it will be nice to spend the rest of the evening in a pleasant social party. Mr. Cole is very keen on having you and Mr. Flower as his guests, and will be sorely disappointed if you refuse. Besides, you will be doing a real kindness to Miss Gloria by accepting, for she has been irked and bored by having had to disguise her identity and not being able to mix with people. Say you'll accept, Mr. Armitage."

Alan felt that it would be more than ungracious to decline; so Max Van Rosen departed a few minutes later to convey to Gloria and her father the glad news that he had induced Rodmell Armitage and Horatio 

Flower to accept an invite to dinner, at the same time promising to interview the management and to ensure that the dinner would be one of the finest ever served in the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel. 56

Taken by surprise, Alan had a sudden inclination to laugh, but he controlled himself. "Tell Miss Fernandez, please, that Mr. Alan Armitage will be honoured and delighted if she will join him and Mr. Flower at tea in the open-air café on the beach before we all go bathing as arranged," he said very politely.
