Alan thanked her and hung up the receiver, then turned to Blossom. "Have you met Miss Janet Gilmour, otherwise 

Bunty, who seems to be Gloria's guardian angel?" he enquired. "Yes, I met her yesterday afternoon," Blossom answered. "Shrewd and highly intelligent Scots-woman, who mixes up colloquial Americanisms with her native Doric, but obviously worships Miss Gloria, to whom she behaves as if she were her mother, although she cannot be more than about thirty-five herself, and a comely wench withal. . . . Do we go bathing?" 58

"We do. Miss Gilmour has promised to have Miss

Gloria fixed and waiting for us on the beach level within a quarter of an hour." "So we'd better strip," remarked Blossom, twinkling, and beginning to unbutton his tight, white tunic, picking up an illustrated advertisement folder from the table as he did so. "This is an advertisement issued by the hotel, Alan, listen: 'Bathers change in their rooms and are carried by elevators direct to the beach level, where they enjoy exclusive privileges: cushions, towels, umbrellas, chairs, and the service of our beach attendant . . . all at no extra cost.' 999 Ten minutes or so later, a tall, lithe young man in bathing costume, accompanied by a rotund, rubicund gentleman who looked like Mr. Pickwick minus his spectacles (for Blossom had remembered to leave his glasses behind), took advantage of the advertised. facilities of the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel and descended by elevator from their suite to Waikiki Beach. 

Just outside the beach porch of the hotel a slim, graceful girl in bathing costume, with a silken wrap flung round her shapely shoulders, and wearing large tinted sun-glasses and a huge Lauhala sun-hat, was standing talking to a dark, rosy-cheeked, bare-headed and jolly-looking woman, who darted a keen glance at the two men as they emerged. "This'll be Mr. Flower and Mr. Armitage, I'm thinking," she exclaimed, and instantly Alan recognised the Scots voice he had heard on the telephone about a quarter of an hour previously, and strode towards her smilingly. "Miss Janet Gilmour, otherwise Bunty, I think?" he said, and held out his hand. "Can it be possible that your companion is Miss Inez Fernandez? The sun-glasses and the Lauhala hat are a most effective disguise." 59

Gloria Drake trilled out a throaty laugh—that laugh that lovers of the "talkies" throughout the civilised world knew and adored, took off her dark glasses for a moment and then quickly replaced them. "Yes, it's Gloria," she laughed. "I told Bunty that even you wouldn't recognise me. Bunty has a key to my bedroom and the door that opens from it on to the corridor, and we slipped out unheard and unseen, leaving Joe Connolly and Spike Powell on guardbut guarding nothing-in the outer apartments. Pop is taking a siesta in his own room, and Max Van Rosen is transacting business somewhere. I feel like an escaped prisoner. This is the first time for weeks and weeks I have been outside the hotel without an armed man on either side of me, yet I don't feel the slightest qualm of fear now that you and Mr. Flower are here, although you are both unarmed." "Say, sweetie, have I no' telt ye that it wis jist nonsense tae hae a pair o' tough guys wi' pistols in their pooches stravaigin' after ye, even when ye come doon here on to Waikiki Beach for tae bathe or get yer bonny body sunburnt?" exclaimed Bunty in her queer mixture of American and Scots. "Och! but it's fine tae feel free!" "It is indeed, Bunty," cried Gloria with another gurgling little laugh, "and if Mr. Armitage will promise to look after me, I'll promise that I'm going to enjoy myself this evening. I'll simply revel in feeling free again, and won't care who recognises me as Gloria 

Drake. .. Step on the gas, you men! Tea on the beach in the midst of the crowd and then-then, Mr. 

Armitage, I'll show you what I can do on the surfboard." This was a new Gloria Drake, a lovely creature pulsating with laughter and joie de vivre, and Alan could not help but find himself both interested and 60

fascinated. He could not help but find himself admiring the lovely grace and shapeliness of her perfect figure, revealed and accentuated by her close-clinging, skimpy silken bathing-suit, as they sat together sipping tea beneath an awning. Nor could Blossom, who found himself wishing, after all, that he had not remembered to leave his glasses behind.

Alan flattered himself that he knew something about surf-board bathing, and Blossom in the water was a sort of human porpoise, but both found themselves mere novices in comparison to Gloria Drake. So skilful and daring was her performance that even the 

Hawaiian boys, who seem to be amphibious, cried out congratulations in their shrill, falsetto voices and most of the sun-bathers forsook their prone positions on the beach to crowd to the water's edge and watch her admiringly.

When at last they emerged from the sea, Alan found himself unwillingly compelled to share in the ovation accorded to Gloria, and also, to his annoyance, found himself recognised by some of the people on 

Waikiki Beach who had been passengers on the Golden

Gate and had witnessed his rescue of Cyrus O. Cole, so he had to endure handshaking and pats on the back. 

Moreover, a squad of Press photographers appeared on the beach, as if from nowhere, and insisted upon his posing with Gloria. "Let's make a dash back to the hotel," cried Alan above the clamour as he found the crowd, mainly in bathing costumes and beach pyjamas, beginning to form a circle hemming him and his companions in. "Miss Gloria, follow me. Blossom, you know your job." "Right!" cried Blossom. "Ready?"

Alan nodded, and Blossom and he immediately lowered their heads and charged through the crowd, 61

much in the same manner as they had done through the crowd on the deck of the Golden Gate, and with the same effect. Gloria, the third member of the party, had recognised what was expected of her, and followed close on Alan's heels as he and Blossom sprinted across the beach to the entrance to the hotel. "Oh, I'll say that was clever!" she cried breathlessly and almost convulsed with laughter as they reached the porch, in which stood Bunty carrying on her arm Gloria's wrap, which she at once threw around her young mistress's shoulders. "Ye're a' behaving like daft things," Bunty exclaimed, "and I dinna mind telling ye that there's hell tae pay here. Watch yer step, Miss Gloria, and dinna blame me. Here they are!" "They" proved to be Mr. Cyrus Oke Cole, Mr. 

Max Van Rosen, Joe Connolly and Spike Powell, all looking white and excited as well as hot and bothered. 

Van Rosen, Connolly and Powell had pistols in their hands, and all cried out in relief at sight of Gloria and her escorts. Cyrus Cole dashed forward and threw his arms around his daughter's wet, lithe form, kissing her repeatedly with tears streaming from his eyes. "Gloria! Gloria, darling, we thought they'd got you!" he sobbed. "I woke up to find you'd vanished, and your guards hadn't seen you go-curse them! 

Say, what's the use of gunmen who let a girl slip out without their knowing?" he added, releasing Gloria from his hysterical embrace and turning furiously to 

Van Rosen. "Here have I been setting the whole hotel alight under the impression that my little girl had been kidnapped in spite of all your precautions, telephoning the police and very nearly asking the authorities to call out the military, and all the time my little girl has only been bathing with Mr. Armitage and Mr. 

Flower. Why didn't any of you boneheads know 62

enough to tell me that Gloria was all right and hadn't been kidnapped? Am I paying big money to be thrown into a state of panic?" "Pop, darling, don't blame Mr. Van Rosen or the guards," interposed Gloria, giving her father an impulsive hug and kissing him on the brow. "Bunty and

I slipped out into the corridor from my bedroom, and Mr. Powell and Mr. Connolly had no chance of seeing us. Oh, Pop, it's been grand to feel free and to be able to enjoy myself without even giving a thought for a moment to the Dandy Hagen gang, or to know that I was being watched by a couple of armed men. I've had my first hour of real freedom for months, thanks to Bunty and to Mr. Armitage and 

Mr. Flower." "Ay, it's been a great relief, Mr. Cole," added 

Bunty, and glanced down as something made a sudden skirmish on the floor. "Is this no' the wee dog ye brought doon with ye, Mr. Armitage?" she enquired.

The question was really unnecessary, for the cause of the sudden skirmish was, of course, Imp, who, like Mr. Cyrus O. Cole, had been in a state of panic, and only with difficulty had made his way through the throng on the beach to rediscover his beloved master, whom he had almost decided was lost to him for ever, together with the female person to whom he had taken a liking on the previous night, and his good friend 

Blossom, his permanent bone purveyor.

Imp, as a matter of fact, had disapproved of the whole proceedings, although he had enjoyed being fed on scraps of cake and biscuit by Gloria while she was taking tea with his master. But Imp did not approve of bathing and was worried when the humans he adored got into deep water. His idea of bathing was to venture into the sea only as far as the tops of his very short legs, and then bark his indignation at 63

any wave which came in and threatened to swamp him, at the same time darting convulsively backwards. So he had spent a thoroughly miserable hour on the fringe of Waikiki Beach, barkingly begging his beloved to come ashore, and welcoming them with excited yelps and warm licks on their bare feet every time they did come ashore on their surf-boards, then protesting until his throat was sore when they took to the sea again. "Why sure it's Mr. Armitage's darling Sealyham," exclaimed Gloria, bending down and picking Imp up. 

Rodmell Homestall King uttered a sort of groan of relief, licked her chin, and then snuggled his squat head against her breast, at the same time, however, cocking one alert eye on Alan. And one would have almost have sworn that he winked. "You've given us all a great fright, Miss Gloria," remarked Max Van Rosen, looking very disgruntled, and both Connolly and Powell nodded assent. "Well, well, well," said Cyrus Cole, wiping his eyes and mopping his brow, "so long as you've enjoyed yourself, Gloria darling, I suppose we didn't ought to complain, but next time you are going to disappear give me a hint to save me losing my hair." "Right, daddy darling," responded Gloria, "and just to show I'm repentent or truly unrepentent, I'll stand -you all cocktails before we go up to change for dinner. 

Incidentally, don't you think that, as Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower will be with us all evening, it would be a good idea to give Mr. Connolly and Mr. Powell a night off?"

She beamed on the two gunmen, and Joe Connolly dropped his pistol back into his pocket, ran his fingers through his rusty hair and smiled, while Powell bowed to her with effective grace as he also put his gun away. "A night off would sure be pleasant, Miss, and I 64

guess Spike and me will be able to find some way of enjoying ourselves," Connolly said. "Still, at the same time, I'd like to think that Mr. Armitage will park a gun in his dress clothes just in case of accidents." "I don't think we need worry about guns, kidnappers, or anything else this evening," remarked Alan in his slightly drawling and "very English" voice, "and I think, for that matter, that the night off for you two gentlemen can start now if Miss Gloria-I mean Miss Fernandez-is agreeable." "Agreeable!" cried Gloria with her attractive, gurgling laugh. "You two men have done your duty splendidly, and I have always felt safe in your charge, but it will be a relief and a pleasure not to be able to see either of you for a few hours. Bunty, give Mr. 

Connolly and Mr. Powell twenty-five dollars each to help them enjoy their evening off." "Imphm! it shouldna be difficult for tae enjoy yersel' on fifty dollars, even in Honolulu," remarked

Bunty dryly, opening the bag she carried and taking out a wad of bills. "I see no reason why we should take such unnecessary risks, Miss Gloria," commented Max Van Rosen, who was still looking peeved and disgruntled. "However, I shall park a gun in my dress clothes to-night even if Mr. Armitage thinks the precaution unnecessary. As a matter of fact as I told you before, I think you should always carry an automatic yourself." "When you've invented some way, Max, for a lady to carry a gun in a backless bathing-suit so that it won't show and won't get wet while she's giving an exhibition of surf-board bathing, I'll park a gun all the time, but not till then," retorted Gloria smilingly. "Now folks, what about those cocktails? Guide the way to the lounge, Max."

Cocktails were served to them in the lounge by a 65

creamy-skinned almond-eyed Japanese girl in Nipponese costume, who bowed low when Bunty, on Gloria's instructions, gave her a tip of five dollars. Incidentally, Bunty was herself generous by nature, but she had a habit of translating dollars into £ s. d., and could never quite understand why Gloria handed out five dollar bills, "the eequivalent o' mair than a pound," as she had reminded her on many occasions, as a tip for any trifling service, although she knew that Gloria's annual income was something almost fabulous. "Well, Alan, what do you think of the fair Gloria now?" enquired Blossom when he and Alan were back in their own suite and were dressing for dinner after enjoying the luxury of a hot bath followed by cold spray. "Personally, I think she is simply marvellous, and I am still lost in admiration of her prowess as a swimmer. If she had been on the Golden Gate when her father fell overboard, there would have been no necessity for either you or me to have gone after him if Miss Gloria was at hand." "She certainly can swim," drawled Alan, “and she certainly knows how to show off. Once again, Blossom, I find myself almost regretting that I went overboard after Mr. Cyrus Oke Cole, for if I had not done so I should have been spared the necessity of making the acquaintance of Gloria Drake, alias Inez Fernandez and actually Mary Drake Cole." "But surely, my dear Alan, you do not regret having made the acquaintance of such an enchanting person?" queried Blossom, looking quite bewildered. "As a type I find her quite interesting, but I do not feel in the least disposed to allow myself to be used by her or her manager, Van Rosen, for publicity purposes," Alan responded. "I should feel flattered, I suppose, by the fact that she has, as it were, appointed me her guardian for to-night in place of a couple of 66

American toughs, but I assure you I do not appreciate the honour, and my personal impression is that all the talk of kidnapping and of the Dandy Hagen gang may be part of a publicity boost for the 'Virgin Vamp,' 

Gloria Drake." "My dear Alan, I feel you are quite wrong," protested Blossom earnestly. "Both Mr. Cole and Mr. 

Van Rosen assured me that the danger was very real, and I think the very fact that a film star gives up contracts worth tens of thousands of pounds and flies in disguise from Hollywood to Honolulu is evidence that she herself was convinced her life was in peril." 

The telephone bell rang at that moment, and Alan picked up the receiver. "Say, Mr. Armitage, Gloria wants to have dinner in the Beach Saloon instead of here in our own suite," said the voice of Mr. Cyrus Cole over the wire. "Do you think it will be safe?" "I foresee no danger, Mr. Cole," Alan answered, "and I don't know of any reason why we should not give the other guests at the hotel the privilege of staring at us, and collect a little incidental publicity for ourselves at the same time." 

"I am glad you take that view of it, Mr. Armitage,' said Mr. Cole, "and I know Gloria will be pleased. 

Mr. Van Rosen was opposed to the idea of dining in public, but what you and Gloria say goes." "Surprising that Mr. Van Rosen should shun publicity," commented Alan.

And although Mr. Cyrus Cole did not detect the satiric and sardonic note in his voice, Blossom did, and made a grimace. 67

CHAPTER VI 

83 MR

R. CYRUS O. COLE had given the management of the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel carte blanche, instructing them to serve a banquet for six, regardless of expense, and to include every sort of delicacy available, accompanied by the choicest of vintage wines. The result was a gastronomic triumph. which was highly appreciated and enjoyed by Mr. 

Horatio Flower, at least, but elaborate meals always bored Alan, who was glad when at last the coffee and liqueur stage was reached, although, with characteristic courtesy, he added his compliments to those of Blossom to his host on the excellence of the dinner. "Let's get outdoors," suggested Gloria, a superbly beautiful figure in a sheath dinner-gown of shimmering green satin, with which she wore a necklace and bracelets of emeralds and a superb rope of pearls. "I want to listen to the Hawaiian natives singing, and watch them dancing, and then I want to dance myself.

As I told you before, I feel like an escaped prisoner, and I intend to enjoy my freedom to the full. One little hour of rapture and freedom will be compensation for being watched and guarded by armed men for weeks and weeks. It will be heavenly to dance out of doors in the moonlight." "I don't think you should take unnecessary risks, Miss Inez," protested Max Van Rosen, glancing around apprehensively; but "Miss Inez's" father, who, like Blossom, had mightily enjoyed his dinner and the wines, and was flushed of face and in great good humour, slapped him on the back. 68

"My dear Max, we've nothing to fear with Mr. 

Rodmell Armitage and Mr. Horatio Flower in charge of the party," he exclaimed. "Gloria shall have her hour of rapture. What say you, Mr. Armitage?" "Why, certainly, Mr. Cole, and I am all in favour of falling in with Miss Gloria's suggestion to get out of doors and dance in the moonlight." And thenwhy, he could not have explained even to himself-he found himself quoting: "We shall have one hour of rapture, Oh, give to God the praise! 

Then we'll say good-bye for ever 

At the parting of the ways." "Isn't that rather a melancholy idea, Mr. Armitage?" Gloria commented, regarding him with a quizzical expression in her starry eyes. "Possibly, Miss Inez, possibly," drawled Alan, and laughed to hide his own embarrassment; "but I have a habit of trying to face facts. Within a month I am leaving Honolulu on a long cruise, and it is highly unlikely that our paths will ever cross again." "Don't talk like that!" cried Cyrus Cole, seizing 

Alan's hand and shaking it vigorously. "My dear Mr. 

Armitage, I hope we are going to be friends for life.

Meanwhile- 99 "Meanwhile, on with the dance!" interrupted Alan, and bowed gallantly and smilingly as he offered his arm to Gloria, around whom Bunty, herself a demure figure in a black evening frock, draped a magnificent chinchilla cloak. "Ye'll need it," she said, as Gloria protested that she required no wrap. "It's no' safe, even in the tropics, for tae go out intae the night air frae a hot room withoot something round ye. I'm feeling like dancing 69

mysel' to-night, but I'll be on hand, sweetie, to take charge of your cloak when you want to dance." "I guess I shall feel more at home and happier in the lounge than outdoors, and so will Mr. Van Rosen," remarked Cyrus Cole. "No, sir," said Van Rosen. "As the guards are off duty I don't propose to let Miss Inez out of my sight until she is safely back in her own suite." "Really!" Gloria's voice was icy and had in it a mocking note. "I don't propose to allow Mr. Max

Van Rosen to interfere with my liberties and enjoyment to-night, and I suggest that his part of the programme will be to look after my father instead of me. 

I suggest also, my dear Max, that you are paying Mr.

Armitage no sort of compliment by insinuating that he isn't capable of looking after me for an hour or two." "I tell you that you are running unnecessary risks, and someone should be on hand to keep watch over you," insisted Van Rosen scowlingly. "Someone will be on watch-meaning me," interposed Bunty. "And it might interest you to know, Mr.

Van Rosen, that I carry a gun, amongst other things, in my vanity-bag, and ken how to use it, although I hope I'll never have ony occasion to shoot." "As for me," chimed in Blossom, "I fear my dancing days are over, and I should have difficulty in finding partners in any case, so I think the best thing to do will be to accompany Mr. Cole to the lounge, there to continue my researches into the effect of whisky and soda on the human organism." 

Everyone laughed (although Van Rosen did so with obvious effort) and Gloria and Alan, the cynosure of all eyes, strolled from the Beach Saloon, followed by Bunty, into the open and stood for a time in the 

Coconut Grove listening to a troupe of Hawaiian 70

natives singing their lilting, tuneful songs, which included, of course, the inevitable "Aloha Oe," written by Liliuokalani, once Queen of Hawaii, and more tuneful and civilised than many songs crooned to-day, although it was written before "civilisation" came to Oahu. 

Then they danced in the silver moonlight filtering through the palms, to music played by the Royal Hawaiian Orchestra, and once again found themselves the centre of attraction, for both were graceful and highly accomplished dancers and their steps matched perfectly. "I think I can tell you quite sincerely that you are the best dancing partner I have ever had," said Gloria later as they "sat out" in the silver moonlight amid bowers of rainbow-coloured, heavily-scented flowers. "Even if you don't seem to want to talk to me much," she added. "I doubt if we have very much in common, Miss Gloria, and I have never been very good at making conversation," responded Alan calmly, after lighting a cigarette. “As a matter of fact, I am quite grateful to you for not asking me to tell you all about my work and pretending you have read everything I have written." "As a matter of fact, Mr. Armitage," Gloria responded, mimicking his voice and manner, "I have read everything you have written under the name of Rodmell Armitage, and possibly some of the things you have written under other names." "I am flattered," said Alan, but still in a detached tone. "It is rather fun having two or three names," Gloria went on, "but that is where you score over me. You can produce work under a nom de plume, but I could not appear on the screen as Inez Fernandez or Mary 71

Cole without being instantly identified as Gloria 

Drake. You can alter your style, but I cannot alter my voice, face, and figure sufficiently to go unrecognised, and the film directors seem to have conspired to prevent me from playing any part other than that of a vamp or a seductive siren. You cannot tell me that I look quite different from what you expected if you have seen me on the screen, but I can tell you that you are quite different from what I expected Rodmell

Armitage, the author of Ashes of Vengeance, to be." "I don't know if I should feel flattered by that as well, Miss Gloria," drawled Alan smilingly. "What, may I ask, did you picture Rodmell Armitage to be?" "Well, I think I expected you to be older and more fiery and romantic," answered Gloria slowly. "You seem almost too-too cold and cynical to have written the tender and beautiful love scenes I remember in your novels." "As a matter of fact, Mr. Flower helps me with the love scenes," explained Alan, and it seemed to Gloria that inwardly he was laughing at her, although his expression had again become serious. "Blossom is an incorrigible romantic, and I may tell you in confidence that he writes hectically passionate love stories under a pseudonym." "How very funny," laughed Gloria. "I cannot imagine Mr. Flower-Blossom, as you call him—in the part of Don Juan. Which just proves, I suppose, that authors in real life are quite different from what we expect them to be, judged by their books." 

There came an interruption. A chubby little 

Hawaiian gentleman in evening dress, not unlike 

Blossom in appearance, although decidedly different in colour, appeared before Gloria, followed by a tall, square-shouldered, fair and exceedingly handsome young man, also in full evening dress. 72

"I am the Master of Ceremonies," announced the 

Hawaiian, bowing low. "Miss Fernandez, permit me to present Mr. Earl Winter." 

Earl Winter, in turn, bowed from the hips, and

Alan judged him to be a German who had received military training. The rays of a spotlight which someone was operating to show up the beauties of the brilliant hibiscus, blood-red poincianas and blue jacaranda, and the many beautiful flowering shrubs surrounding the dancing space, fell direct on him for a few moments, revealing him to be of waxily pallid complexion, pale, steely-grey eyes, and with a neat little golden moustache which did not conceal his thinlipped mouth. "I may have the pleasure of a dance, Miss Fernandez ?" he asked with a certain degree of assurance. "Sorry, Mr. Winter, but there's nothing doing," said Gloria coldly. "I'm engaged for the whole evening to one and only one dancing partner.' 99 "Since when has Miss Gloria Drake found it necessary to engage the services of a gigolo ?" enquired the newcomer. 

Next minute he was wondering if there had been a sudden earthquake in Honolulu, for he found himself on his back, vainly trying to struggle to his feet and with the idea in his head that something had hit him -and hit him hard. He was right. The "something" that had hit him were the fists of Alan Rodmell 

Armitage, one shooting out like a piston-rod, straight to his solar plexus, and the other, at the same instant, swinging upwards to hit him on the point of the jaw. 

It happened so swiftly that Gloria had scarce time to gasp, and as she turned and grabbed Alan's arm his other arm encircled her. "I object to being called a gigolo, Miss Gloria," he drawled, "but let's dance this," and before Gloria 73

quite realised what was happening, he had whirled her out into the moonlit and spotlit dancing space, leaving

Mr. Earl Winter sprawled beneath a fragrant clump of flowering shrubs, with the little, tubby Hawaiian M.C. waving his arms and making strange noises beside him, or, rather, above him. "That was swift work," breathed Gloria, and then she laughed rather breathlessly. "Now I do feel that

I am safe with you, Mr. Armitage, and that Pop was right." "My inclination is to shepherd you back into the hotel, Miss Gloria," responded Alan in a cool, calm voice, and Gloria noticed that he was not looking at her. "I wonder," he added, "if you have the nerve to go on dancing and await developments? I wish 

Blossom were here. I anticipate trouble. Make up your mind quickly. Decide whether to get back at once to the hotel and safety or take the risk of going on dancing. I scent danger." "Why, of course, I'll go on dancing, and if Mr. 

Earl Winter tries to make any trouble for you I'll tell the officials he insulted you by calling you a gigolo,' exclaimed Gloria, still rather breathless. 

"Right!" said Alan, "Go on dancing as if nothing had happened, but don't complain if I steer you around in an erratic sort of manner. I want to keep my eye on 

Mr. Earl Winter. Smile, Miss Gloria, and try to appear quite unconcerned."

Gloria obeyed, but she found that he still was not looking at her, and found herself puzzled and wondering if, in spite of his apparent composure, he was scared of what the results might be of his having laid out Mr. Earl Winter, wondering, also, why Alan was making her steer such a zigzag course as they danced. 

She did not realise that her partner was, actually, "keeping an eye on" Earl Winter, and had scarcely 74

let him out of his sight for a moment since his right fist had come into such sudden and violent contact with the blond fellow's jaw. He had seen Winter being assisted to his feet, and had seen, also, three men appear mysteriously from the shadows of the bushes and cluster around him. He had not failed to notice, incidentally, that a moment after he had laid Winter prone among the bushes, Bunty had bolted back into the hotel as if panic-stricken. "This is a queer sort of dance, Mr. Armitage,' commented Gloria as she found herself whirled round again and steered through a little crowd of other dancers into the rays of the spotlights. 99 "We are not dancing, we are only marking time,'

Alan told her, his eyes still roving. "We'll mark time here, pretending to dance until the something I anticipate begins to happen. I hope you are still enjoying yourself, Miss Gloria.' 

"Well, I always enjoy mystery, and you seem to me to be behaving mysteriously, but you are certainly making things interesting," Gloria answered, inwardly annoyed because she knew, or thought she knew, he was paying no attention to her. "Perhaps you will explain why you are behaving like this." "Glance over to your left and tell me if I am mistaken in thinking that I saw your Bunty, Miss Gilmour,

I mean, dash out from the hotel just now, accompanied by my Blossom, otherwise Horatio Flower, and your 

Mr. Van Rosen," responded Alan, his eyes still roving.

Again Gloria obeyed, and in a few seconds was able to assure him that he was not mistaken, for she saw Bunty, Blossom and Max standing together only a little way off and gazing at them anxiously. "Right!" said Alan again. “Now we can dance."

He steered her out of the bright light towards the little group, humming the air that the orchestra was 75

playing, and smiling, but when, for a moment, his glance did rest on Gloria, she saw that his eyes were glinting and there was no mirth in them. "We dance past Blossom and Bunty and I give instructions," he said, scarcely above a whisper. "Prepare yourself to be ready to make a dash for the porch of the hotel as soon as I bid you do so. 

Be on the alert and obey orders, but meanwhile keep smiling."

Yet again Gloria obeyed and kept on smiling up at him, wondering why he was being, so it seemed to her, so dramatic and mysterious. They danced past and then alongside Blossom, Bunty and Van Rosen, and she heard Alan say, as if talking to no one in particular: "Blossom, be ready in a few minutes for some deadly tackling. There's a fair man with three other fellows preparing, I think, to pounce on me and Miss Gloria. They're stalking us round the bushes now.... Get your gun out of your vanity-bag, Miss 

Bunty. You may need it. . . . Van Rosen, have your gun ready, and when I push Miss Gloria towards you presently fire into the air first and swiftly, and then rush Gloria into the hotel, keeping your body between her and anyone who starts shooting. ... Dance on, Miss Gloria."

He whirled Gloria round again so that he could glance towards the spot on which he had last seen 

Earl Winter and his three companions, and then danced around until he again caught sight of them and was able, so to speak, to take his bearings. He knew instinctively that the danger could not be avoided, and he fervently hoped that Bunty and Van Rosen would rise to the occasion. He had not the slightest fear or doubt about Blossom. "If you think you're scaring me, Rodmell Armitage, you're wrong," exclaimed Gloria, breathless again. 76

"I hope I'm wrong," said Alan, and she saw that his expression was tense and felt his arm tighten involuntarily around her; "but when I thrust you away from me in a minute or so I beg you to sprint as fast as you can into the hotel.... Now!"

He hurled her away from him as he spoke, and at the same moment bent double and seized round the legs one of the two men who had dashed forward with the idea of seizing Gloria, who was sent hurtling almost into the arms of Bunty. Almost simultaneously, Van Rosen fired his pistol, and as he did so, Blossom emitted a bellow that would have been a credit to a prize bull and charged head down at the second man who had tried to grab Gloria. 

The first man had been sent crashing to the ground by Alan's rugby tackle, but as Alan straightened himself and wheeled round he found himself confronted by the fair, waxen-faced individual who had been introduced as Earl Winter. The thin lips of Earl 

Winter were parted in a sort of snarling grin that showed a double row of white teeth, and he had in his hand an automatic which he levelled at Alan's breast. "Put 'em up!" he snarled, and Alan immediately raised his hands above his head; but as he did so, he raised his foot-a trick he had learned in Mexicoand kicked hard and sure at the hand that held the pistol.

It is quite a good trick, for the man with the gun does not expect the man with his hands above his head to use his feet, and if the kick is sure, the pistol flies from his hand. Earl Winter, who had been so sure of himself, suddenly found himself disarmed, and next moment Alan had him by the throat, and both went crashing to the ground. They were well matched, and practically of equal physique, and there followed 77

a tense few minutes of "all-in" wrestling and struggling on the ground. Alan was trying to strangle his opponent, but the other man was no mean antagonist, and he got a grip―a ju-jitsu grip-on one of Alans' wrists, compelling him to release his hold on his throat, and at the same time, struck him in the face, partly dazing him.

The fourth man of Earl Winter's party had been standing with a pistol in his hand irresolute, afraid to fire at either of the two twisting and writhing bodies on the ground in case he hit the wrong man, and suddenly he found himself out of action, for, as he watched the struggle on the ground, the bald head of 

Mr. Horatio Flower hit him full in the pit of the stomach, sending him staggering backwards, winded and bewildered, and before he could recover himself, Blossom had knocked his pistol from his hand. "Shoot him, Kruger," snarled Earl Winter breathlessly as he sprang to his feet. "Shoot, damn you!"

But Kruger was the man Blossom had butted and disarmed, and as he dived for his gun, Blossom hit him neatly on the side of the jaw, sending him staggering again, and picked up the pistol, which he levelled at Winter, realising that he was the leader of the raiders. "I think," he panted, "that you swine had better make yourselves scarce. Hop it, or I'll do the shooting!" His hand, his eyes and his gun were steady as he confronted Earl Winter. "Shall I shoot him,

Alan?" he cried without relaxing his tension or attention for an instant. "Shout for help, Blossom," gasped Alan, gathering himself up from the ground, and alertly avoiding a blow aimed at him by one of the fellows who had tried to seize Gloria, and who, with his companion, had struggled to his feet. 78

Blossom drew a deep breath without relaxing his vigilance for a moment. "Help! Help! Help! Police!" he bellowed, and as he did so Earl Winter swung round and started to run. "Beat it, all of you," he yelled, barging his way through a group of excited and agitated men and women who had been watching the struggle, too scared even to attempt to intervene, and, incidentally, sending the little, tubby Hawaiian M.C. sprawling in his flight. 

His henchmen heard his shout and bolted after him, two of them diving through the bushes as they saw men in uniform appear in answer to Blossom's bellow for help. All four disappeared before anyone, save

Blossom and Alan, quite realised what had happened, or had any idea of pursuit. "Well done, Blossom!" cried Alan, and laid his hand affectionately on his tubby little secretary's shoulder. 

Blossom turned and twinkled at him through his big, gold-rimmed glasses, which, somehow, he had miraculously preserved intact. "Quite an interesting evening, my dear Alan!" he remarked, trying to appear quite casual, but then dissolving into laughter in which Alan joined. "But now, Alan," he added quickly, "I think we'd better follow the example of those thugs and beat it-beat it back to the hotel, for I see that the crowd is gathering and instinct warns me that they may try to make us heroes again. Beat it!"

He grabbed Alan by the arm and together they sprinted, laughing and breathless, back into the hotel and along a corridor to an elevator which shot them up to the floor on which was their own private suite. 79

88 66 "I 

THINK, my dear Alan," said Blossom as he closed the door, "that the occasion calls for two large whiskies and sodas, and I think that, for once in a way, you may be in agreement with me?" No, at the moment I am afraid Mr. Armitage cannot speak to you, as he is having a bath.”

He twinkled at Alan again through his glasses as he put down the receiver and waited for comment, but Alan merely shrugged his shoulders and began to fling off his clothes. 99 "I find myself wondering, Blossom, if we are being victimised and used as pawns in a publicity game,' he drawled, some little time later, when he had bathed and Blossom had treated his and his own scars with iodine and arnica. "I find myself wondering now whether to-night's 'bonny fecht' may not have been a publicity stunt.' 99 "A publicity stunt?" repeated Blossom. "Surely that could hardly be possible, my dear Alan. Miss Gilmour -Bunty, as Miss Gloria calls her-dashed panicstricken into the lounge and informed us, meaning Mr. 

Cole, Mr. Van Rosen and myself, that you had knocked out a man whom she recognised as Dandy Hagen, the chief of a band of kidnappers. You will remember that it was because of the threats and her fear of the Dandy Hagen gang that Miss Gloria fled here from Hollywood." "Really! That makes the matter more interesting," drawled Alan. "If the blond individual who was introduced as Mr. Earl Winter is actually Dandy Hagen, as Bunty asserts, I am glad to have had the oppor- 81

tunity of handing him a slosh on the jaw and then of doing my best to strangle him. But I am puzzled, Blossom. Why should the fellow, who, by the way, looks like a Boche, have insulted me, unless he was looking for trouble? True, he seemed to have three thugs waiting to help him, but still I find myself baffled. 

Bring your great brain to bear on the problem, Blossom, and tell me what you think is the explanation."

Before answering, Blossom crossed to the sideboard and helped himself to another whisky and soda, presumably with the idea of stimulating his imagination and his deductive faculties. "I think, my dear Alan," he said at last, seating himself and lighting a cigarette, "that Earl Winter, otherwise Dandy Hagen, did not expect to be rebuffed by Gloria at the outset, when he was introduced by the M.C. and asked for the pleasure of a dance. I imagine his simple plan was to partner Miss Gloria in a dance, guide her outside the lighted area and then, with the aid of his three henchmen, carry her off. 

I may be wrong, but, drawing a bow at venture, I should say that when he insulted you he probably only anticipated starting a wordy row with an inoffensive English gentleman, during which he would lay you out while his accomplices seized Miss Gloria and spirited her away." "Humph! sounds feasible enough, assuming the

Boche is actually Dandy Hagen, the notorious kidnapper," commented Alan. "Proceed, my dear Blossom." "I think he could hardly have anticipated that you would so promptly put him down for the count, and I was surprised to find you and Miss Gloria dancing when I appeared on the scene," proceeded Blossom. 82

"But tell me, why did you anticipate trouble or suspect that he had a gang with him?" "Well, I disliked him at first sight, Blossom, and after I had laid him out it occurred to me that he might have some private motive for insulting me although, as far as I know, he had never seen me before," answered Alan. "So I kept my eye on him, and when I saw him engaged, apparently, in giving instructions to three toughs, I danced Miss Gloria around while awaiting events. The events duly eventuated and the rest you know. If the Bochelooking individual is actually Dandy Hagen, the kidnapper, I think, my dear Sherlock, that your theory is quite a plausible one."

As he spoke there was a discreet knock at the door, which Alan opened to find himself confronted by a man in evening dress and a uniformed police officer. "Mr. Armitage, I think?" said the man in evening dress, and Alan nodded. "I am the assistant manager of the hotel, Mr. Armitage; this is Captain Smith of the Honolulu police who wishes to ask you a few questions about the affair outside a little while since." "Come in," said Alan, and waved his hand towards a couple of vacant chairs. "What is it you wish to know, captain?" "First of all, who the man was that you laid out twice, and who the other fellows were," answered

Captain Smith promptly and crisply, eyeing Alan and 

Blossom appraisingly as he sat down. "I haven't the remotest idea, captain, but my secretary here, Mr. Flower, is under the impression that the man who was introduced as Earl Winter, is actually Dandy Hagen, chief of a gang of kidnappers." "What! Otto Hagenberg here in Honolulu !" exclaimed Captain Smith, his steely eyes lighting up. "Gee! this is interesting." 83

He began to ask questions, and both Alan and Blossom explained matters frankly as far as their knowledge served them. "You two gentlemen certainly seem determined to get yourselves into the news," commented the police captain smilingly, after listening attentively and making copious notes. "First of all you save Mr. Cyrus

Oke Cole from drowning, and now it appears you have saved his daughter from being kidnapped. Not a bad performance for two successive days, I'll say!" "I suppose I can hardly expect you to believe me,

Captain Smith, but my desire is to keep out of the news," retorted Alan a trifle testily. "Publicity may please film stars, but I neither want it nor need it. 

What happened to-night was just one of the adventures which seem to come my way. . . Now answer me some questions, please. Have you arrested the men who were responsible for the trouble?" "No, Mr. Armitage; the officer who chased them report that five of them got away in a speed-boat, and they have no knowledge of what has become of them. 

We have sent out natives in their canoes, as well as the police motor-launch, to conduct a search, but the men had a long start on us and may have reached one of the other islands." "Do you think Mr. Flower is right in believing that the man introduced as Earl Winter is Dandy Hagen, the kidnapper ?" "What happened seems to me to confirm Mr. 

Flower's impression," answered the police captain. "Describe the man again as accurately as you can, sir. Yes, that is a description of the man we know as 

Otto Hagenberg, commonly known as Dandy Hagen, and to-night calling himself Earl Winter, which is only one of a dozen aliases he uses," he added when Alan had complied. "Thank you very much, gentlemen, and 84

accept my congratulations on getting away unhurt from that tough gang. If you will excuse me, I'll get along now and interview Miss Fernandez, who, I am surprised to learn, is Gloria Drake, the film star. And 

I think Miss Gilmour may possibly be able to supply me with some useful information." "I think Mr. Flower and I might as well come along with you, captain," remarked Alan. "I am anxious to know if Mr. Flower's theory is correct, and both of us would like to know just what the fight was about." 

Led by the assistant manager of the hotel, they all trooped down to "Miss Inez Fernandez's" suite on the floor below. The hotel official knocked on the outer door, which was opened, after a considerable interval, by Max Van Rosen, looking white and scared and with a pistol in his hand. He gave vent to a gasp of relief at sight of the police officer with Alan and 

Blossom behind him, and threw the door wide. "Come in, gentlemen, come in," he exclaimed, rather breathlessly. "I confess I was half afraid to unlock and unbolt the door, not knowing whether I might find myself confronted by the Hagen gang. We are all rather scared by what has happened, and as the guards are off duty, I am Miss Gloria's sole protector.' 99 "This is Mr. Van Rosen, Captain Smith, the secretary to Miss Inez Fernandez," explained the assistant manager to the police captain. "I think you will be able to assure him and Miss Fernandez that it would be utterly impossible for any gang to enter the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel and kidnap anyone from it." "I can assure them, anyhow, that extra police guards will be posted," replied Captain Smith. "Put your gun away, Mr. Van Rosen. Your fingers are twitching and the gun might go off." 

Van Rosen obeyed, and drew another breath of relief as he led the way into the inner lounge, in which 85

Bunty, also with a pistol in her hand, and Gloria and her father, all looking pale and apprehensive, were standing.

At sight of Alan and Blossom all three cried out even more relievedly than Max Van Rosen had done, and Gloria ran forward and clasped Alan's hands in her own. "Thank God you have come," she almost sobbed. "I have been so afraid-so afraid, not only for myself, but for you, since Bunty told me the man was Dandy 

Hagen. Oh, how can I thank you? You scared me, but you were wonderful." "I can assure you, dear lady, that you have nothing to fear now, and our friend, Captain Smith, the police chief, will likewise assure you that you are now quite safe," said the suave assistant manager of the hotel reassuringly. "Such a regrettable and distressing incident has never previously occurred here, where the comfort and safety of our guests is our first consideration."

He glanced expectantly and significantly at Captain Smith, who realised what was expected of him and played up. "From what I have heard, lady, I guess you are right in saying that Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower behaved wonderfully," he said. "Mr. Malloy, here, is right in assuring you that you have nothing to fear now. My men are in pursuit of the gang who tried to attack you, and I am having extra guards posted at the hotel entrances and in the grounds. . . Now, if you will be good enough to answer a few questions. 99 Gloria could give him little or no information, but Bunty assured him she was practically certain that the man Alan had knocked out was the individual who had been pointed out to her months previously at a 86

Hollywood "first night" as Otto Hagenberg, otherwise Dandy Hagen, the man of whom all the rich people in the film capital went in fear, and who contrived his coups so cleverly that the police had never been able to get a conviction against him for anything except minor misdemeanours. "I kent the evil face o' him as soon as I set een on him, and I've been blamin' mysel' ever since for not shooting him when Mr. Armitage knocked him doon, instead o' running for help," Bunty explained. "That would certainly have simplified matters for me, Miss Gilmour, but might have complicated things for you," commented the police captain.

He asked a few more questions, politely declined Mr. Cyrus Cole's offer of a drink and a cigar, and took his leave, accompanied by the suave and smiling hotel official. Van Rosen saw them out and was careful to lock and bolt the outer door when they departed. "No need to take any unnecessary risks," he remarked with forced lightness to Alan. "You will remember that earlier in the evening I pointed out to 

Miss Gloria that she was taking unnecessary risks, first in giving the guards the night off, and then in refusing to allow me to be in attendance with my gun handy." "You were on the spot when you were needed, Mr. 

Van Rosen, thanks to Bunty," responded Alan dryly. "I rather fancy if you or the guards had been on duty earlier the incident might have developed into a shooting affray in which there might have been several casualties. As it is, none of us is hurt." "You are hurt," exclaimed Gloria, and to his embarrassment, she clasped Alan's hands again. "You have a cut on your cheek and another on your brow, and this poor hand is bleeding. Let me bandage it 

Bunty, fetch a bandage, please." "It is nothing," protested Alan. "Blossom treated for you. 87

my knuckles with iodine and arnica before I came down, and they will be all right."

But Gloria would not be gainsaid, insisted upon bandaging his hand (and did it very skilfully, somewhat to his surprise), then "kissed it to make it better," remarking, as she smiled into his eyes, that was what her old nurse used to do. "You shed blood for me, Rodmell-you don't mind if I call you Rodmell instead of Mr. Armitage?and-oh, I don't know how to thank you," she said. unsteadily. "You may have saved me to-night from something worse than death." "Please spare your thanks, Miss Gloria," responded 

Alan, stiffly and unsmilingly. "I assure you Blossom and I quite enjoy an occasional scrap, and we would have enjoyed it just as much if the lady in the case had been a decrepit charwoman, instead of a film star of international reputation."

Gloria dropped his hands and recoiled from him with a hurt look in her eyes, but as she did so her father dashed forward and seized Alan's bandaged hand. "I can believe that, my dear Mr. Armitage," he exclaimed tremulously. "I know that you and Mr. 

Flower are very brave and gallant gentlemen. You saved my life and now you have saved my little daughter. Bless you and bless you both! I only wish you would allow me to reward you as you deserve." "There can be no question of reward," said Alan sharply. "If you imagine that I am a sort of Sir

Galahad, making a hobby of rescuing people, or that my friend Blossom and I are reincarnations of Don 

Quixote and Sancho Panza, you are completely wrong. 

And I should like to add that we neither seek nor desire publicity for ourselves, nor do we wish to be used as pawns in a publicity game for a film star." 88

"My dear sir, I-I don't know what you mean,' stammered Cyrus Cole, looking quite dazed. “My gratitude is sincere and I am sorry if I have offended you by mentioning my wish that I could reward you." "I am more bored and puzzled than offended, Mr.

Cole," Alan responded. "To be quite frank with you, I am wondering if to-night's affair was not staged by

Mr. Van Rosen, and if the Dandy Hagen kidnapping gang are part of a Hollywood publicity scheme, into which I have got myself entangled." "No, no, no, Mr. Armitage!" cried Van Rosen. "I assure you Dandy Hagen is a real danger." "Don't you understand that I came here to escape from him and his gang?" interposed Gloria, her voice tremulous. "It is cruel of you to suggest that you are being used for publicity on my behalf-which I suppose is what you actually are suggesting." "I shall be glad to find that I am completely mistaken," said Alan, “and I am sorry if I have offended. 

Now you must excuse me. I have just remembered that I arranged to meet Captain Samuel Simpson at the bar to-night, and he must have been waiting for me for some time. Good night to you all." 

He bowed formally, darting a glance at Blossom, who was looking and feeling worried. "Of course you arranged to see Captain Simpson," said Blossom, "and I expect you want to have a confidential talk with him. If you don't mind, Alan, I'll stop here." "As you will," responded Alan. "See you later. 

Again good night, Miss Gloria. Good night, Mr. Cole. 

Good night, Mr. Van Rosen." 

As Van Rosen let him out, carefully re-bolting and re-locking the outer door after him, Gloria suddenly threw herself down in the settee, covered her face with her hands and burst into tears. 89

"He may be brave, but he hasn't any heart, and he hurt me intentionally," she sobbed. "You heard him say that he thought no more of me than of any decrepit charwoman. I thought"-sobs stifled her voice for a few moments—"Oh, men have told me they would give their lives for me." "I can quite believe that, my dear Miss Gloria," exclaimed Blossom, greatly concerned, seating himself on the settee beside her and gently patting her quivering shoulders. "Please don't cry. Mr. Armitage has misunderstood the situation, and you don't understand him." "I understand that he evidently meant what he said when he told me that I mean no more to him than any decrepit charwoman-and that hurts," said Gloria, raising her head and struggling to control her agitation. "It isn't only my vanity that is hurt. My gratitude was sincere and I felt as if he had struck me a blow in the face. This evening he was so charming, and I thought he really liked and admired me, but now- 

Oh, Mr. Flower, what put the idea into his head that to-night's attempt to kidnap me was some sort of frame-up for publicity purposes? Is he quite heartless and cynical all the time?" "My dear Miss Gloria, I assure you that Alan

Rodmell Armitage, although at times he may appear cynical and blasé, is quite one of the warmest-hearted men I have ever met in my life," answered Blossom, much relieved to note that Gloria was recovering her composure, for women's tears always distressed him. "But he is a queer fellow, for whom women, no matter how beautiful or alluring, appear to have no particular attraction. If you realise that, you will understand that his remark that he would have done as much for a decrepit charwoman to-night was merely a statement of fact, and was not meant to hurt you." 90

"I feel sure, Mr. Armitage, would never say or do anything to hurt any woman's feeling, darling, and that you are upsetting yourself needlessly," chimed in Cyrus

Cole, putting his arm affectionately around Gloria's shoulders. "He's proud, and I guess I offended him by mentioning reward." "I'll put matters right, Miss Gloria, by assuring him he is all wrong in thinking there was any sort of conspiracy to-night, or that I ever had even a thought of using him in any publicity stunt," added Van Rosen soothingly. "I'll see him first thing in the morning if 

Mr. Flower can fix things for me." "I assure you I shall do everything in my power to adjust matters," said Blossom.

All three men were anxiously regarding Gloria, who, after a few moments, rose to her feet and smiled at them through her tears. "So no woman, no matter how beautiful or alluring, has any attraction for him, and I mean no more to 

Rodmell Armitage than any decrepit charwoman!" she exclaimed, gazing at Blossom with shining eyes. "That seems to me a challenge. I'll make him care. 

Yes, I'll play the part in real life that I play on the screen, and vamp Mr. Alan Rodmell Armitage! I mean it!"

She laughed, just a trifle hysterically, and the three men again regarded her in silent concern for a few moments, then Blossom also laughed and jumped to his feet. "My dear Miss Gloria, I warn you that you will be playing with fire, and that Alan Armitage is not a man with whom you can trifle," he said. "Still,

I shall watch the experiment with a considerable amount of interest. Meantime, if your father cares to suggest it, I shall be pleased to drink to your success." 91

"Mr. Flower, I'm real sorry!" exclaimed Cyrus

Cole, stirred to action and bustling to the sideboard. "It isn't like me to forget the drinks. Will you have champagne or whisky?" 92

CHAPTER VIII 

8 

HERE had been several speed-boats racing about

TH vos aut off Waikiki Beach as well as a considerable number of motor-fitted out-board canoes, so it was hardly surprising that no one had paid any particular attention to the speed-boat in which the Dandy Hagen gang had made their escape, and that neither the police launch nor the native canoes had managed to locate it. As a matter of fact, almost before the hue and cry started, the speed-boat and its passengers were safely aboard a yacht anchored in the shelter of Diamond Head. 

The boat had been slung on davits ready for use again at almost a moment's notice, and the passengers it had carried were in the cabin, used by day as a dining saloon.

The police captain had been correct in his information that there were five of them, the fifth man having been left on the beach in charge of the speed-boat while the others attempted their daring raid. They were not a pretty lot, although two of them-Otto Hagenberg and Al Casey-under different circumstances might have been described as good-looking. 

But no man is good-looking when his face is contorted with passion, and all the men in the cabin were angry, to say the least of it. "You lousy useless lot of white-livered boobs,' 

Dandy Hagen raved at them, his waxy face livid with rage. "Three of you, all with guns, and you let yourselves be laid out by a couple of damned English swine, without even laying hands on the girl!" "You bungled the business from the start, Dandy, 93

damn you, and I didn't notice that you came off top dog with the tall Englishman yourself, although you had a gun," snarled one of the men at him. "He's left marks on you that'll stop your chances of being a lady-killer for a week or two." "Don't dare to sneer at me, Kruger, or I'll leave marks on you that you'll carry to the grave-and the grave'll be waitin' as soon as I have finished with you," Hagenberg snarled back at him. "If I thought you had worked the double-cross on me I'd make you food for the sharks here and now." "If I'd worked the double-cross on you, Dandy

Hagen, you'd be in Sing Sing now, and you might have gone to the electric chair," retorted Joe Kruger, his lips curling back from his uneven yellow teeth, and his deep-set, beady eyes glaring. 

He was a villainous-looking fellow whom any judge might have been excused for convicting on appearance alone-swarthy, with a drink-sodden, deeply-lined face, a broken nose, beetling eyebrows, a mop of untidy black hair and a spiky black moustache. "Say, Joe, what's the idea of starting a row with the boss?" exclaimed Al Casey angrily, pushing Kruger aside and interposing between him and Hagenberg. "I dunno why he didn't shoot the tall Englishman to start with, and I dunno why he didn't shoot him when he had a second chance." "He let the blasted Englishman kick the gun out of his hand," sneered Kruger, "and then he blames us." "Curse you! I'll—I'll-" yelled Dandy Hagen, and again Al Casey interposed. "See here, boys, you're doing wrong in using hard words and threatening to treat us rough," he said. "You've got to remember that there were three men against us the lanky fellow who downed you, the little round man who charged like a buffalo, and the 94

Jew man who fired the shot, besides crowds of men who might have come to their rescue. I'll say you're not giving us a fair deal, and you're asking for trouble by suggesting that any of us worked the double-cross on you.' 99 He was a totally different type from Kruger, being fair, young, clean-shaven and upstanding, but he ran no danger of being mistaken for a gentleman, for he wore very "loud" clothes, with a coat much padded at the shoulders, spoke with an accent compared with which the sound of a man sharpening a sword would be sweet music, carried himself with a swaggering, defiant air, and looked, in short, what he was-a 

Chicago gangster. But, unlike Joe Kruger, ready to turn traitor if any profitable opportunity presented itself, he was loyal to Otto Hagenberg, and actually admired his boss, whose exploits he had hopes of emulating, although he knew full well that in all probability his career would be cut short. "You're right, Al, in saying the boss isn't giving us a fair deal," chimed in Bill Jones, a nondescript-looking individual with sandy hair, a bronzed and much befreckled face and a foxy expression. "We were playing against the odds, and Dandy Hagen should have shot the lot up as he's done before, and let us get a make-away with the girl." "You'll tell me what I should have done, Ginger!" yelled Hagenberg, almost foaming at the mouth with rage. "I'll show you who's boss. I'll- 99 "Otto, my dear friend, this vill not do !" interrupted a harsh, croaking voice, and a claw-like hand clutched

Hagenberg's arm. "I'll tell zee boys this is bad business for to quarrel with their chief. Come to be reasonable, my dear Otto, and consider not how you failed tonight, but how you make the success next time."

The interrupter and would-be peacemaker was even 95

uglier than Joe Kruger, and considerably older. He was a ferrety-faced, wizened, hook-nosed, bald-headed man with a scrubby beard, hollow cheeks and thin claw-like hands. Dandy Hagen turned on him with an oath, shaking off his hand from his arm, and then, as he met the glance of the old man's ferrety eyes, he shrugged his shoulders and sat down. "Perhaps you're right, Seth," he said after a pause; "but I still think the gang let me down to-night. 

What's your opinion?" "I think you have the bad luck, and you run against unexpected an Englishman who is a fighting man," said

Seth Sneyd in tones that were meant to be soothing. "That's so, Spider," cried Al Casey. "Spider" was the gang's nickname for Mr. Seth Sneyd, a rascally lawyer who had served several terms of imprisonment before becoming Otto Hagenberg's "man of affairs" and chief adviser, particularly his chief adviser on the best means of escaping the law and avoiding the consequences of his misdeeds.) "It was sheer bad luck, and we made a bad break, so it's no use us blaming the boss or the boss blaming us.' "Perhaps you're right," growled Dandy Hagen in his guttural voice, smoothing his sleek, golden hair with his carefully manicured hands. "I guess we'd better have some liquor and discuss how we stand.” "That is ze idea," agreed Spider Sneyd, his wizened face wrinkling into some sort of semblance of a smile. "Always it is a mistake to quarrel over a failure, as much as it is a mistake to quarrel over the share-out of the spoils after the success. To me it seems nobody vas to blame." "You think so, do you, Mister Sneyd?" interposed a high-pitched female voice, and a woman who had been sitting on a divan in the shadows at the end of the saloon, watching and listening, came forward 96

swiftly to the table. "I'll say that Dandy Otto was to blame first, second, and all the time." She faced Hagenberg across the table, her china-blue eyes gleaming mockingly. "You thought, I suppose, dear Otto, that you were so handsome that even Gloria Drake would fall for you at first sight?" she continued. "You wouldn't take my advice and let me go ashore first to see or find out how things stood. You butted in, expecting her to fall for you, and expecting to dance her off under the very noses of her guards, but you got laid out yourself instead. A pretty mess your darn conceit has made you make of things! Now you can't expect to land again at Honolulu without being recognised and arrested." "Damn you, Sybil!" Hagenberg snarled at her, livid with passion again. "Are you going to sneer at me and call me down in front of the whole gang? What do you think you could have done?" "I couldn't have done worse than fail, and get the whole of Honolulu talking, and the police excited," retorted Sybil Laval.

She was a woman of perhaps twenty-five, artificial in appearance and manner, artificial outwardly and at heart. She had natural good looks and a pink and white complexion, but her hair was of an unbelievably platinum blonde, her lips were, as the poet says, too, too incarnadine, and her long, dark eyelashes, fringing her china-blue eyes, were obviously artificial, while her frock was, although obviously expensive, just a trifle too bizarre. "If you think I am going to be called down by you, shouted Hagenberg, jumping to his feet in a fury; but again Spider Sneyd intervened. you- "Please, please, please," he pleaded. Let me ring for the steward to bring ze liquor, and afterwards perhaps we see things the better." A steward appeared at that moment, drinks were ordered, and Sneyd handed round his cigar-case to the men and laid a gold cigarette-case in front of Sybil, for whom he set a chair at the table. "Miss Laval, perhaps, will tell us what is in her mind."

"Say, Sybil, I'll trust you," he added, "and if you bring off what you have in mind, I'll make everything all right with you. I'll be honest and own up now that I'm sorry I was impetuous this evening and didn't take your advice. Gee! if only you can manage to decoy the famous

English author, Armitage, as well as Gloria Drake, aboard this boat, it will be a great scoop, and after the ransoms have been paid, I guess we'll be able to retire from business." "It is a great idea, and I know that Miss Laval is clever enough to bring it off," agreed Spider Sneyd, glancing at his watch. "We had better go ashore as soon as possible, and in one of the ship's boats and not the speed-boat, which might possibly be identified. I'll get back as quickly as I can, chief, to report, so that if there seems to be any danger threatening we can sail to-night and be out somewhere in the Pacific before the police begin their search." "I can finish packing while you give orders and have the boat got ready," said Sybil, jumping to her feet. "You can leave it to me, Otto, to help you bring off the biggest thing of your life. Kiss me, darling, and wish me luck." "It isn't only the prospect of getting held of Gloria 

Drake that makes me trust you and agree to your plan," remarked Dandy Hagen, as he complied, "but the hope of getting into my power that damned Englishman who laid me out to-night. By God, I promise you I'll make the English swine sorry that he ever ran up against Otto Hagenberg!" 104

CHAPTER IX 

8 

LAN found Captain Samuel Simpson waiting for

ALA 

him at the lounge bar of the hotel, but within a few minutes of ordering drinks he begged the skipper of the Southern Glamour to take him to some other place, for he was recognised as the hero of the night's sensation, and men and women came flocking from the lounge, eager to congratulate him. "For the love of Mike, Simpson, let's get out of this!" he exclaimed, forcing his way through the crowd and making a bolt for the door. "Take me to some place where we can drink and talk in peace, where there are no confounded women, and where no one will want to slobber over me," he added, as the captain joined him in the open air. "I reckon I can sort of guess how you are feeling, Mr. Armitage," said the captain with a booming laugh; "but if you will make the doing of heroic deeds your daily hobby, you must expect to be fussed over, particularly by women." "Damn the women!" snapped Alan irritably, and then he drew a deep breath and laughed-partly at himself. "Let's be off, captain, or some of the would-be hero-worshippers will be after me. I hope you know some quiet little pub where we can drink beer in peace." "There's a quiet little pub on the waterfront, but it's frequented chiefly by sailormen, and is the sort of place where the appearance of a gentleman in evening dress will cause something of a sensation," responded Simpson. 105

"I'll chance that, and I can apologise for the evening dress."

Ten minutes or so later they were seated in a corner of a clean, old-fashioned, but badly lighted saloon, each with a big glass of beer in front of him. The other customers were mainly seafaring men, as Simpson had led Alan to expect, and to them Alan, in his self-composed and attractive way had apologised for his "glad rags," requesting the bartenders at the same time to serve drinks round at his expense, to celebrate the fact that he had become nominal owner of the Southern Glamour, and the fact, also, that he had had the luck to secure the services of Captain Samuel 

Simpson as his skipper. Which, of course, made everyone, and particularly Simpson, feel well disposed towards him, and decide he was a "he-man" in spite of his boiled shirt.

He and Simpson talked of many things, but mainly of the Southern Glamour, which the captain had arranged to take into dry dock on the following morning, promising to superintend the refitting personally. Then, over a third tall glass of beer, Simpson began to ask questions about the events of the eveing. "What sort of girl is this Gloria Drake, Mr. Armitage?" he enquired. "I've seen her on the movies, of course, and she looks as if she could vamp the heart of any man. I've got a wife in 'Frisco, and I'm not the sort of sailorman who has a wife in every port, but I guess I'd fall for Gloria Drake, and I'm wondering how you feel about it." "I don't think I'm likely to fall for her, captain, if that's what you mean," responded Alan smilingly. "Why? Isn't she as beautiful in real life as she is in the pictures?" asked Simpson. "I think she is the most gloriously beautiful female creature of any nationality that I have ever seen in my 106

life," Alan answered. "Women don't interest me much except as types, but it is almost impossible not to be interested in Miss Gloria Drake. I am assured that her name—unlike that of many of the female stars of Hollywood-has never been associated with that of any man, that her vamping is confined entirely to film plays, and she is known in the studios, although she is unaware of the fact, as the 'Virgin Vamp.' I should like to cultivate her acquaintance and make a study of her during my stay here, but I abhor the idea of my name being linked with hers in any sort of publicity stunt, as it seems likely to be after what happened this evening.'

Sam Simpson nodded, took a swig at his glass and began to refill his pipe. "I think I understand how you feel about it, Mr. 

Armitage," he said after a pause. "If it had become public knowledge that Miss Inez Fernandez is Gloria 

Drake, I guess you'll find your name in the headlines in the newspapers to-morrow, coupled with hers, and all Honolulu will be crowding along to try to see you as well as Gloria Drake." "I realise that, captain, and that is why I am feeling so sick about the whole affair. That is why I am glad to be here now, drinking beer among men to whom film stars don't matter two hoots, and who don't want to slobber over a fellow just because he happens to have come out on top after having accidentally found himself involved in a scrap. I don't want to find my name coupled with that of any damn' film star, no matter how beautiful or interesting she may be, so tomorrow morning Blossom and I will find new quarters." "The Imperial Hawaiian is great, Mr. Armitage, but if that's how you're feeling, I guess it is a good place to get away from if you want to avoid publicity." 107

"Do you know of any hotel, some little way outside the city and yet within easy reach of it, where I might find peace and quiet until the Southern Glamour is ready for sea?" enquired Alan.

Captain Simpson lit his pipe and puffed at it thoughtfully. "There's a little hotel down at Haleiwa that I think might suit you," he answered after much consideration. "You get to it by a sort of toy railway that runs round the coast, mainly for the benefit of tourists who want to admire the scenery, but very few tourists make a stay at the Haleiwa Hotel, although in my opinion it is situated in the most beautiful spot of all the beautiful places in Oahu. It's right at the north of the island, but within easy reach of Honolulu, and we should have no difficulty in keeping in touch with each other." "Good!" exclaimed Alan, "to-morrow Blossom and 

I go to Haleiwa and make it our headquarters until the 

Southern Glamour is refitted. Have some more beer, captain ?" "No more, thank you, sir," said Simpson, shaking his head and rising to his feet. "I've got to get aboard now to get everything in readiness for getting into dock to-morrow morning. There's a boat manned by Kanakas waiting to take me out to the Southern Glamour. 

Would you care to come with me and spend the night aboard as you are so anxious to avoid publicity? I can fix you up with pyjamas and shaving tackle, and you could send ashore for a suit of mufti first thing in the morning." "A splendid idea, captain!" exclaimed Alan, finishing his beer and getting up from the table; and then he shook his head. "No, that might only complicate matters further as far as I am concerned, captain," he added after a few moments of consideration. "If I don't return to the hotel within a reasonable period 108

Blossom will become alarmed, report me missing and the police might jump to the conclusion that I had fallen into the hands of the Dandy Hagen gang, thereby creating another sensation, which would make things more awkward than ever for me when I turned up to-morrow morning.' 99 "Humph! I hadn't thought of that," said Simpson. "Incidentally, I wonder how the Dandy Hagen gang managed to make a successful get-away in their speedboat, and whether they have boarded a vessel in the harbour or made for Molokai?" "I think we can leave that to the police to discover," Alan responded as they went out together into the cool, fragrant night. "If you don't mind, captain,

I'll stroll along with you to where your boat is waiting, and then wander back to the hotel round about midnight, by which time I hope the excitement will have subsided and most of the people will have gone to bed." 

They strolled along together in silence. The moon was waning, but its light was still silvery bright and the stars of the Southern Cross seemed to hang out like great glittering crystals from a sky of blue-black velvet. From the grounds of the Imperial Hawaiian 

Hotel in the distance the faint sound of languorous dance music floated through the still air, while clearer came the sound of the singing of the natives who were waiting in their boats to take visitors who had elected to spend the night ashore back to their various vessels. 

To Alan it seemed as if there was some sort of soothing magic in the scented night air, and the lapping sound of the phosphorescent water against the quay. 

He found his irritation evaporating, and by the time he reached the place where Captain Simpson's boat, manned by smiling, singing Kanakas, was awaiting him, peace had enveloped him like a mantle. 

As Simpson's boat left the waterfront it almost col- 109

lided with another which was approaching the quayside, and there was a sudden exchange of nautical "compliments," in high-pitched voices for the one part and deep-toned English oaths on the other, which made 

Alan smile.

He paused idly to watch the incoming boat made fast, and saw a fair woman, whose face gleamed white in the moonlight, assisted from it on to the slippery steps that led up to the quayside, and then he noticed that two or three sailors were struggling to land a cabin trunk, a suit-case and a couple of hat boxes. It struck him as rather curious that a woman with a considerable amount of luggage should land at such an inconvenient spot on the waterfront at such a time of night, and he dallied out of idle but instinctive curiosity.

There was an old-fashioned hurricane lamp, which shed a yellow glow, fixed in an iron standard at the top of the steps, and by its light, and the silvery light of the waning moon, Alan was able to see the woman quite clearly as she came up to the quayside, and she could see him equally distinctly. He saw a fair, fluffy sort of woman with big, goo-goo eyes and very red lips, wearing a fur coat over what seemed to be a skimpy dinnerfrock, and Sybil Laval saw a tall man in evening dress, and somehow guessed at once from his appearance that he was English. It was passing strange that the first man upon whom she set eyes on landing at Honolulu should be Alan Rodmell Armitage, but coincidences do actually play a part in real life, as well as materially assisting novelists. "Excuse me, sir," said Sybil in her high-pitched voice, laying her hand lightly on Alan's arm as he was about to turn away, "I wonder if you can help me.

I want to get to the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel, and there doesn't seem to be a taxi in sight. Can you tell

The dancers were thinning out, but Alan was conscious of the fact that he and his fair companion attracted a considerable amount of attention and that they were scrutinised closely by the police officers posted in the grounds and at the entrance of the hotel. As soon 114

as he had introduced Miss Sybil Laval to the reception clerks, Alan made a bolt for the lift without waiting to be thanked, and was whisked up to his own suite, where he found Blossom pacing up and down the sitting-room, puffing at one of Mr. Cyrus O.

And then suddenly it dawned upon him. "Why, Blossom, where's Imp?" he demanded.

Blossom turned from the sideboard with a drink in either hand and blinked apologetically through his glasses. burst into tears, Imp jumped up on her lap and licked her face, evidently with the idea of consoling her. 

When I took my leave, some time ago, Imp was curled up peacefully in Miss Gloria's lap, and he definitely refused to accompany me, snarled at me when I attempted to pick him up, despite the fact that I sternly addressed him as Rodmell Homestall King." "Most extraordinary!" remarked Alan, accepting the glass Blossom held out to him and sipping from it with ruminative deliberation. "Do you mean to tell me that Miss Gloria Drake actually burst into tears?" "Tears seems but a weak description, my dear

Alan," responded Blossom. "She sobbed as if her heart was broken, and I found my position horribly distressing. You see, you had told her that she meant no more to you than any decrepit old charwoman, and that hurt her sorely, and wounded her to the quick.

I must say, Alan, that you were unusually brusque and sardonic in your manner, and seemed to have made up your mind to say something biting to Miss 

Gloria and her father as well as Van Rosen. You left them all in a distressed state, and it was only with difficulty that I persuaded them afterwards that you had not intended to be deliberately sarcastic or insulting." "Good Lord!" exclaimed Alan in some consternation, and feeling twinges of conscience. "Do you mean to say, Blossom, that I was as sarcastic as all that?

I jumped to the conclusion that I had been used as a sort of pawn in a publicity stunt, and I confess I was angry, but I had no intention of hurting the feelings of Miss Gloria Drake or her father."

He emptied his glass, lit a cigarette, heaved himself out of his chair and paced up and down the room for a few minutes, his brows drawn together in a troubled frown. Blossom, accustomed to all his moods, and 116

knowing or guessing what was in his mind, sat down and watched and waited patiently and in silence, sucking his black cigar.

Sorry to disturb you, Miss Gloria, but I find that 

Rodmell Homestall King, otherwise Imp, has appointed himself your guard of honour, so to speak, and I wish to relieve you of the responsibility of looking after him, as you may find him troublesome during the night, and he will certainly demand to be taken for a walk first thing in the morning. . . . "You're a queer beggar, Alan."

As he went slowly down the stairs to Gloria's suite on the floor below, he found himself rehearsing what he intended to say to her, and deciding-as Sybil Laval had decided a little time before-that he must be "nice."
