and then, after a pause, the door was opened to him by Bunty. The alert and always capable Miss Gilmour had her small automatic pistol in her hand, but she threw the door wide and gave Alan a welcoming smile as she recognised him. "Come inbye, Mr. Armitage," she said. "Miss

Gloria's alone, except for me, and I'll no' be in the road. Connolly and Powell turned up tipsy no' sae long ago, and Miss Gloria and me decided we'd be safer by oursel's than wi' a couple o' tipsy men.

Forbye that, Miss Gloria decided that she wanted to be alone, and when she maks up her mind aboot onything, gee, there's nae mule could be mair stubborn, and I've said so tae her bonny face. Come in,

Mr. Armitage. Miss Gloria's waiting for ye, and I'm sure she'll forgie whit ye said tae her aboot her bein' nae better than ony auld charwoman, but she'll be laith for to part wi' yer wee dog."

She locked and bolted the outer door as Alan smilingly entered, then escorted him into the inner lounge, where Gloria, now in filmy negligee, was seated on a divan with Imp snuggled against her knees. The dog uttered a yelp of welcome and leapt to the floor, instantly assuming a sort of apologetic air at sight of his master and wagging his stumpy tail jerkily. "Rodmell Homestall King, you're a traitor," exclaimed Alan with mock severity, and the Sealyham cowered, still wagging his tail, however, and turned his square head to glance out of the corner of his eye at Gloria, who had risen to her feet. 8 LAN seated himself in one corner of the divan, ALAN

herself involved in some sort of scandal a year or so ago and dropped out." Alan's lean face flushed, and he looked, and felt, abashed. "I am afraid that expression offended you, Miss

Please forgive me, and let me assure you, now I am convinced that to-night's affair was not a pre-arranged publicity stunt, that I am truly glad to have been of service to you."

For answer Gloria impulsively held out her hand, and as Alan clasped it, Imp sprang up excitedly and licked both hands, which made Alan and Gloria laugh and relieved the situation. "Rodmell Homestall King, you are making a nuisance of yourself," exclaimed Alan with mock severity, setting the dog down on the floor. "Lie down, sir! Homestall King (or at least that is what he seemed to be saying in doggy language). "I was here before you, master, looking after her, and I don't see why I should be pushed off the couch and called Rodmell

He changed his mind, however, when his master 122

again addressed him sternly by his full name and mentioned something about "whip" and lay down growling and grumbling under his breath, and watching the couple on the divan out of the corner of his eye. 

Within a few minutes he seemed to realise that he had ceased to interest the couple on the divan, and as Gloria moved closer to his beloved master he emitted a snort, closed his eyes, rolled over on his side and went to sleep. "So you don't really dislike me, Rodmell?" queried 

Gloria softly. "My dear Miss Gloria, I don't think I could dislike you even if I tried," responded Alan, conscious of a slight and unusual quickening of his heart as he met her smiling, shining, appealing eyes. "I merely dislike the idea of publicity and self-advertisement, and it seemed to me that you and your father, to say nothing of Mr. Van Rosen, were losing your sense of proportion and overrating anything I had happened to be able to do, hence my reference to decrepit charwoman. 

I hope you understand." "I understand that to-night you risked your life for me and possibly saved me from something worse than death, and I want to thank you and bless you with all my heart and with every fibre of my being," Gloria said, and took one of his hands in both her own. two different personalities. Now tell me you are glad,

Alan Rodmell, and tell me how best I can show my gratitude to you."

She had moved still closer to him and Alan found himself conscious of a heart-thrill which no other girl had ever succeeded in giving him, found himself gazing half fascinated into Gloria's limpid eyes, and he tried to pull himself together and recover his usual sang-froid. "My dear Gloria—yes, I think I prefer to call you 

Gloria of course I am glad to have saved you from being molested by a gang of American thugs, and to know that you are grateful and think kindly of me is sufficient reward," replied Alan, trying to speak lightly, and rather despising himself because he found his voice faltering. "I assure you it is a pleasure to me to have been able, with the assistance of my trusty 

Blossom, to save from danger the most beautiful woman in the world."

Gloria drew a long breath, her unusual eyes, which seemed to possess some magnetic quality, became still more luminous, and her clasp on Alan's hand tightened again. "Is that a formal compliment, or do you really and truly mean that you think me the most beautiful woman in the world?" she enquired. "Are you quite sure you didn't say the same thing to the platinum blonde, Miss Sybil Laval, when she thanked you for conducting her to the hotel?" "I do not pay compliments to women I meet casually," retorted Alan, almost brusquely. "I spoke sincerely, but as you have been accustomed to living in an artificial world perhaps sincerity does not often enter into your calculations." "Oh, Rodmell, dear, I am so deeply sorry!" gasped

Gloria, acutely conscious of the fact that she had 124

blundered badly. "I know-I realise now-I should not have said that, and I hate myself for having hurt you. Please, please, don't be cross, don't talk in that hard voice, but be your own lovable self. Forgive me, dear Rodmell Alan." 

She raised his hand to her lips and kissed it as she spoke, and Alan's pique died at that instant and he was conscious again that his heart was thrilling strangely. Perhaps it was because of that very fact, and because for once in a way he was unsure of himself, that he forced a laugh and again tried to assume a light and casual tone. "Perhaps it will further reassure you if I tell you that I do not inform decrepit charwomen, with a pretence at sincerity, that they are the most beautiful women in the world," he said. "You can believe me,

Gloria, when I say again, as I said to Blossom a little while since, that you are quite the most beautiful female creature of any nationality that it has ever been my good fortune to see." "Thank you, Rodmell, dear," said Gloria, scarcely above a whisper, after a few moments of silence. "I am so very, very glad I am beautiful in your eyes. 

You have made me very proud and happy. Let me tell you again that I think you the most wonderful man in the whole world, and I shall always love and bless you for saving my darling Pop and me."

She would have raised Alan's hand to her lips again but he restrained her. "No, please don't kiss my hand again, Gloria." "You mean you mean that kisses are wasted on the hands? I understand, Rodmell, darling, what you mean, and now I know how I can reward you in part." 

She slid her bare, shapely arms around Alan's neck as she spoke, and next moment her rich lips were pressed on his own, kissing him as no woman had ever 125

kissed him before, and in a fashion that Gloria Drake had never kissed any man before. Her kisses were a new, thrilling and intoxicating experience that set his heart and his pulses throbbing and racing and made him forget all else except the nearness and loveliness and the beauty of her, and he crushed her close and returned her kisses with a burning fervour which almost frightened her, although at heart, as an actress, she was glorying in what she believed to be a speedy triumph and new proof of her power and ascendancy over even such a man as Rodmell Armitage, who had professed not to be interested in women. "Stop, Rodmell, stop!" she gasped at last, breaking from his embrace, flushed and breathless. "You hug like a bear, and you must be terribly hungry for kisses. 

You took far more than I meant to give.'

Alan rose to his feet, feeling like a man suddenly awakened from sleep, and breathing quickly and unsteadily. 99 "Your kisses went to my head like a drug, or drugged wine, and I did not realise what I was doing,' he said shakily after a pause. "I have never been kissed like that before, and it is an experience that will live in my heart for ever. I could not have believed that any girl could have kissed a man with such fervour unless she loved him distractedly. You are a great artist, Gloria." "A great artist ?" repeated Gloria, her husky breathless voice quivering. "I don't understand, Rodmell. If I kissed you as you say, with a fervour of a girl who loves to distraction, you returned my kisses, so it seems to me, as only a man could do who loved the girl with a passion equal to her own or surpassing it. Do you love me, Rodmell? Have my kisses stirred your heart, which Blossom told me to-night had never 126

been stirred by any woman? Look into my eyes, dear 

Rodmell, and answer me truly.' "I am not quite sure that I want to look into your lovely eyes, Gloria, for they seem to have some sort of hypnotic quality," responded Alan, still a trifle breathlessly and unsteadily. "You certainly affect me as no other woman has ever done before, but as for loving you-well, I confess my brain is in a turmoil and I cannot answer you truly."

He paced quickly up and down the room for a few. minutes, trying to master the unusual emotions which seemed to be seething within him, then seated himself again beside Gloria and took her hand. "Do you mean me to understand, Gloria, that you have fallen desperately in love with me?" he resumed in calmer tones. "Remember that we met for the first time only last night, and that to-night's adventure has shaken and excited us both and we are not at present our normal selves. I have asked the question, but I do not ask you to answer definitely to-night if you love me and wish to marry me." "I know I want you to love me, Rodmell," Gloria answered after a hesitant pause. "I-I can't even analyse my own feelings, but-but I wanted to kiss. you, and I wanted you to kiss me, and if any other man on earth had kissed me as you did just now I should have wanted to kill him. Darling, I think- 

She broke off, for at that moment there came a discreet knock on one of the inner doors and Bunty appeared, wearing an apologetic air, whereupon Imp was suddenly galvanised into action and uttered two or three staccato barks, just to prove that he was a really good watch-dog as well as a pedigree Sealyham. "Say, you folk, I'm awfu' sorry to disturb you, but mebbe ye havna noticed that it's nearly two in the mornin'," said Bunty. "We've got to think o' reputa- 127

tions, and if a handsome young man was seen leaving the apartments o' Miss Inez Fernandez, wha's very likely kent by this time tae be Gloria Drake by most of the folk in the hotel, Gloria Drake's reputation micht get the first smudge that it's ever had on it." "By Jove, you're right, Miss Gilmour," exclaimed

Alan, springing to his feet after glancing at his watch, and secretly relieved to grasp the opportunity to escape from a situation which seemed to be getting beyond his control. "Come along, Imp, we'll say good night to Gloria." He picked up Imp from the floor, tucked him under his left arm and held out his right hand to Gloria. "Good-bye, Gloria." "Until to-morrow-which is already to-day," Gloria responded softly as she clasped his hand. "To-morrow? Why, To-morrow I may be myself with Yesterday's Sev'n Thousand Years," quoted

Alan, with the uncomfortable feeling that he was talking banal nonsense. "Good night, my dear Gloria." "Good night, Alan darling," Gloria replied, and heedless of the presence of Bunty, she kissed him full on the lips again. "I shall dream of you to-night, and already I am looking forward to to-morrow. Good night, dear, brave man."

Alan's heart was still beating unsteadily as he bade 

Bunty good night and she saw him out, carefully locking and bolting the outer door behind him. Imp, perplexed by the whole proceedings, squirmed under his arm as he walked along the deserted corridor, and tried to lick his face. "No thanks, Imp," said Alan with an involuntary laugh. "I've been kissed enough—or overmuch—for one night. You can walk the rest."

He set the Sealyham down on the floor, and Imp, still obviously puzzled, stood still for a minute glancing 128

back at the doorway from which they had emerged, then evidently decided he had better follow his master and pattered after him on his short, stumpy legs. 

Blossom, coatless, collarless, with his shirt sleeves rolled up, busily engaged at his typewriter (often he started work round about midnight on one of his own stories, and continued until the early hours), glanced round as Alan opened the door, and was immediately aware by Alan's expression that something unusual had again happened. "Well?" he queried, swinging round. "You look excited."

Without answering Alan crossed to the sideboard, mixed himself a whisky and soda which he swallowed at a gulp, then lit a cigarette and flung himself down in a big armchair. "Blossom, my lad, I've been vamped!" he announced with mock solemnity. "I assure you it is a most disturbing experience, and I am trying to decide whether it is pleasant or otherwise." "Tell me about it," exclaimed Blossom eagerly, his eyes sparkling. "I think this is one of the occasions when one can kiss and tell. What happened?"

Armitage related in part what had occurred, and Imp, almost appearing to understand what he was saying, jumped up on his knees and grinned at Blossom, who was listening with most intent interest. "Gloria Drake is certainly a wonderful actress,"

Alan concluded. "She almost convinced me that she was in love with me, but when I challenged her directly on that point and asked if she wanted to marry me, she hedged, and I realised she had been acting." "But did you not find yourself in love with her, Alan?" enquired Blossom, deciding not to say anything about Gloria's half-hysterical declaration that she would "vamp" in real life as she did on the films. 129

Alan lit another cigarette and inhaled the smoke of it several times before replying. "My dear Blossom, no one has yet been able definitely to define what love is," he answered at last. "I admit Gloria Drake, or whatever she likes to call herself, stirred me for a time as no other woman has ever done before, but—but I doubt if I am capable of loving or if I want to love-any one woman in the manner that the characters in my novels and plays love, and particularly in the hectic and passionate manner in which the characters in your stories love, Blossom. I think that even if I could believe Gloria was perfectly sincere this evening, I should still be glad that we are leaving for Haleiwa to-morrow morning -or rather this morning." "Humph! So that's that!" was Blossom's only comment as he rose to help himself to another whisky and soda.

Alan also rose, displacing the sleepy Imp from his knee, and stretched himself. "I am going to bed, Blossom," he announced with a yawn. "We will make an early start in the morning and clear out before most of the rest of the guests here are astir." "I have arranged that," said Blossom, and Alan nodded his thanks and went to his own room, followed by Imp giving an imitation of a Sealyham in the last stages of exhaustion.

On the floor below Gloria and Bunty were discussing affairs much in the same way as Alan and Blossom had been doing. "D'ye think he's fallen for ye, Miss Gloria ?" Bunty asked. "I can't make up my mind, Bunty," Gloria replied. "He intrigues me. I thought I had won him, and then, quite suddenly, he began to talk about to-morrow and 130

to-morrow in a way that I couldn't quite understand, and seemed to change back almost into the cynical individual who said that he thought no more of me than any decrepit charwoman." "Och! that's nonsense," exclaimed Bunty. "When 

I interrupted for tae remind ye what time it was, he wisna lookin' at ye as if ye wis a charwoman-decrepit or otherwise-but lookin' at ye with the eyes o' a lover. I'm thinkin' he's fallen for ye a' richt, but I'm wonderin' if you havna fallen for him." "There isn't any question of falling for him, Bunty," said Gloria quickly, flushing in spite of herself. “He's the first man, bar my father, that I've ever kissed or allowed to kiss me except when I've been acting. 

I'm not in love with any man, and I doubt if I ever shall be, but I'll admit that Rodmell Armitage interests me more than any man I've ever met, and I mean to make him fall for me. To satisfy my pride and selfrespect, I want to make him love me and to tell me that he'd do more for me than any other woman on earth, to cancel out his remark about the decrepit charwoman." "I should think without flattering you that it would be almost impossible for any man wi' red bluid in his veins not to fall in love wi' you," remarked Bunty; "but mebbe Mr. Rodmell Armitage is cauld-blooded, and ye'll mind that Mr. Flower warned ye that he's no' the sort o' man tae be trifled with. We'd better get to oor beds." "To-night I know I stirred his heart, and to-morrow 

I shall win him, make him love me and afterwards make him my slave," said Gloria. "Wake me about nine, Bunty, and I'll invite Rodmell to come and bathe with me, and protect me from any risk of being kidnapped. And while we're bathing I'll make love to him again." 131

"Imphmn! Gloria Drake, in her bathing costume and making love, should be irresistable and a'-conquering, but we'll see what we shall see," commented 

Bunty. "Guid night. I'll bring yer tea before nine."

She did, and at nine o'clock found Gloria sleeping as peacefully as any healthy infant, and had to shake her into wakefulness. On the floor above, although neither was aware of the fact, Alan Armitage had spent a restless and almost sleepless night, and finally had betaken himself to the veranda in his dressinggown and pyjamas to smoke and reflect and to wait for dawn. "Hand me the telephone," commanded Gloria, after leisurely consuming the cup of tea which Bunty specially brewed for her every morning. "Put me through to Mr. Armitage," she ordered as Bunty handed her the receiver from the table by her bedside. "What? Are you sure?"

The receiver dropped from her hand, her lovely face flushed, then paled, and she turned to gaze wideeyed at the watchful Bunty. "Bunty, he's gone!" she gasped. "They tell me that he and Mr. Flower left early this morning without saying where they were going. .. Don't you understand, Bunty? He's gone-and last night I thought—

I thought he cared! Now I know he meant it when he said I meant no more to him than any decrepit charwoman. Oh, the humiliation of it! Bunty, don't you realise and understand? I made love to him, and now-now he has gone without even a word of farewell, so showing his contempt and disdain. I've never been so humiliated in my life!" 

She buried her face in her hands and burst into tears and Bunty put her arm around her shoulders and gave her an affectionate hug. "Mebbe he has run away from love, Miss Gloria," 132

she suggested soothingly. "Dinna greet, my dearie! 

D'ye hear what I'm telling ye? I'm sayin' that mebbe he's run away from love." 

Maybe she was right. 133

CHAPTER XI 

I' T would not have lessened Gloria's distress had she known that Alan, despite his troubled night, had succeeded in dismissing her entirely from his thoughts. 

It was an opal and silver morning, cool and invigorating, and the scented air seemed to have in it something of the qualities of champagne. He and Blossom were travelling on the little toy train that runs round the island and both were too greatly enchanted by the beauties of the varied scenery to think of anything else. 

They passed along a rugged coast of lava rock, at the foot of which great waves thundered, sending spindrift high into the air; they passed alongside fish ponds fringed with saffron grasses, alongside rice paddies and saw Japanese coolies with conical straw hats driving water-buffaloes through liquid mud that came up to their knees; they passed through sugar-cane fields where the spear-like leaves of the purplestemmed trees seemed to be dancing in unison in the breeze; and they passed through coconut groves which ran down to a white beach on which the surf was breaking, and came at last to Haleiwa.

Captain Samuel Simpson certainly had not exaggerated. Haleiwa seemed to be a sort of earthly paradise and was almost unbelievably beautiful. The little hotel, reached by a Japanese bridge that crossed a placid little river meandering from the hills to the sea, was almost hidden by flowering trees, and set in grounds with gardens the like of which an English gardener sees only in his dreams.

The proprietor greeted them with enthusiasm, for 134

visitors were few, and Blossom had no difficulty in securing what the host called his "luxury suite" at remarkably reasonable terms. They breakfasted on luscious fresh fruit, eggs and fried chicken, then went out to explore and to discover new interests, beauties and delights.

Everyone they encountered seemed to be happy, smiling, and unhurried. They watched old Hawaiians plaiting leis, and as they watched a comely Oahu maid picked up two of the completed wreaths and smilingly insisted upon hanging one round the neck and shoulders of each of them, half murmuring, half singing “Aloha 

Oe" as she did so, and turning to dart away laughing, proving that her action had been spontaneous and without any thought of reward. 

Flowers everywhere, delighting the eye and perfuming the air, masses of lei, hibiscus, ilima, and scores of other multi-coloured flowers to which they could not put names, and fruit in abundance of great variety to be had for the picking. It seemed a veritable place of plenty and sweet content. Why should anyone worry or hurry or work, if he did not feel so inclined, when he could pluck a meal from the branches of the trees, or enjoy the unbounded hospitality of the Hawaiians who had taken the trouble to cook? "This lei custom is quite charming in its way, Blossom, but you look a fool, and I feel a fool wandering about with a garland of sweet-smelling blossoms round my neck," remarked Alan after a time. "There is, I believe, a superstition that it is unlucky to throw the leis wreath away unless you cast it into the sea, so I think we'd better ramble down to the beach in order to rid ourselves of our fragrant encumbrances." "I quite agree," laughed Blossom. "I feel like a blooming bride who has had her wedding bouquet slung around her neck, and I shall be glad to get rid 135

of the thing. However, we seem at last to have discovered a place where we can have the peace and quiet for which we longed." "Yes, Blossom, Haleiwa seems a sort of earthly paradise, and I hope no new Eve will try to intrude," responded Alan after laughing at the idea of Blossom feeling like a blooming bride.

They wandered down through palm groves to the little sandy beach, sprinkled with tiny pink cowrie shells and enclosed by high rocks, and laughingly cast their lei wreaths into the turquoise and green sea, only to watch them being washed back again to them by the surf. A native boatman came along and explained in a sort of bêche-de-mer English that he had for hire a glass-bottomed boat in which they could sail over the coral beds and watch fishes of many colours darting through the rainbow waters, so, for lack of anything better to do, they chartered the queer craft with the glass bottom and for an hour or so were as interested as if they were in the aquarium of the London Zoo, where most of the lighted fish tanks look like the windows of an expensive jeweller's shop in which the jewels have come to life. "This seems to me a veritable lotus land in which I shall be able to lounge and loaf and dream dreams without feeling any disposition to work," drawled Alan as they meandered back to the hotel for lunch. "Already I feel soothed and rested, and I propose to spend the afternoon dozing in some shady spot in the hotel grounds to compensate myself for last night's loss of sleep." "Quite a good idea, my dear Alan," Blossom responded. "I shall try to sit and dream myself, but, as you know, I consider sleep rather a waste of time, and I feel a sort of urge to finish the chapter that I began at the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel last night-or, rather, 136

this morning. I wonder, incidentally, if Miss Gloria has discovered that we have disappeared." "What does it matter?" said Alan with a shrug. "I seem to be dropping into the habit of quoting Omar Khayyám, but I think the old fellow hit the mark when he wrote: 'Unborn To-morrow and dead Yesterday, Why fret about them if To-day be sweet!'"

Although both were men of imagination, neither Blossom nor Alan bothered to wonder if their sudden disappearance had had any particular effect on the people with whom they had come in contact in Honolulu, and it was of the Southern Glamour and future plans that they talked as they lazily enjoyed an excellent luncheon.

Possibly Alan might not have been able to slumber so blissfully and comfortably during the afternoon in a long cane chair set under the shade of a banyan tree, and Horatio Flower might have had some little difficulty in finishing the chapter of his passionate love story, as he did before devoting the rest of the afternoon to the consumption of whiskies and sodas, had they known that Gloria Drake, as a result of their disappearance, was in a state almost verging on hysteria, with the natural result that everyone immediately associated with her was troubled and upset. 

Gloria had alternately wept, stormed and raved, and even her father had found it difficult to soothe and pacify her. Poor Mr. Cyrus Oke Cole found himself wishing he had never undertaken the journey to Honolulu, particularly as the Chief of Police had informed him that he had probably been shadowed by the Dandy Hagen gang and was thus indirectly responsible, in a way, for their attempt to kidnap Gloria. "Dear, darling child, listen to your old Pop," he pleaded to Gloria. "I'm sure you're all wrong about 

Mr. Rodmell Armitage. I guess he never had the 137

slightest intention of hurting or insulting you, but he's one of those modest Englishmen who hate any sort of fuss or publicity. I've read that lots of heroes were shy men at heart, and although they'd fight like lions, they'd go and hide when it came to being praised and complimented and thanked. I reckon that it very likely occurred to Mr. Armitage last night that he'd have swarms of Pressmen and photographers and detectives bothering him this morning, so he suddenly decided to slip away to some unknown destination." "I'm quite sure ye're richt, Mr. Cole," chimed in 

Bunty. "I've been tryin' tae tell Miss Gloria something the same mysel'. I've minded her o' what Mr.

Armitage said about publicity, and I'm thinkin' he's a shy man although he's sae brave, and didna feel inclined for tae face the music, as ye micht say." "I tell you again, that he'd made his plans to leave before he saw me late last night," stormed Gloria with a passion that almost scared them. "He left as he did to show his contempt for me, although I tried to Ishow him that I loved him. I've never been so humiliated in my life, but I'll get even with him yet, somehow or other." "Darling, do try to be reasonable and sensible," protested her father, greatly distressed. "Remember that Rodmell Armitage saved your old Pop's life, and saved you from being kidnapped last night. I guess, dearie, you've no cause to think so badly of him even if he has hurt your pride by running away without even a word of farewell."

Gloria scarcely heard him. Her pride was so badly wounded that she had temporarily lost all sense of proportion. "I'll get even with him yet, somehow or other," she repeated, pacing agitatedly to and fro. "I hate himhate him! He did it deliberately to hurt and humiliate 138

me! Perhaps he's eloped with that platinum blonde

Sybil Laval he picked up on the waterfront last night and brought to the hotel." "You're haverin', Miss Gloria !" interjected Bunty again. "For the love o' Mike, sit down and compose yersel'."

"Last night's meeting may not have been the accidental encounter he pretended it was, and she may know where he has gone, even if she hasn't gone with him."

She had no difficulty in finding Miss Sybil Laval. 

One of the hotel servants pointed out to her a showily dressed platinum blonde little woman, seated in a corner of the hotel lounge, engaged in earnest conversation with an elderly, partly bald, vulture-faced man with a scraggy beard. as also did "Spider" Sneyd, for there are such things as lady detectives. "I'm Janet Gilmour, secretary and companion to

Miss Inez Fernandez, who's better known to ye by another name," explained Bunty. "Meaning Miss Gloria Drake?" enquired Sybil, and held out her be-ringed hand as Bunty nodded assent. Miss Gloria to remember me and wish to see me.

When I heard rumours this morning that the lady calling herself Miss Inez Fernandez was actually Gloria Drake I wondered if I might venture to ask her to meet me, if only that I might be allowed to congratulate her on her escape last night. This is Mr.

Sneyd, who looks after some of my business affairs," she added, and Sneyd rose and bowed, twisting his face into some sort of semblance of a smile. "I am sure you won't mind waiting for me, Mr. Sneyd, while I meet

Spider Sneyd was accustomed to playing a waiting game, but he had nearly finished a third cigar before

Sybil reappeared, her expression inscrutable, but with a gleam which he judged to be due to inward excitement in her china-blue eyes. "Vell?" he queried as Sybil slumped into a chair facing him and groped in her bag for her cigarette-case. "I want a large high-ball before we do any talking," said Sybil abruptly. "Call the waiter."

Sneyd obeyed, and Sybil neither spoke nor looked at him until the drinks were served, but puffed at her cigarette with a sort of feverish impatience. That means detectives might board the Clarissa if she remains here, and might recognise Otto or some of the gang.'

My plan, as I have said, may take two or three weeks to carry through.' 99 "Ve vill have another drink, and then you must tell me your plan exactly, because Otto vill want to know all the details," said Sneyd. "The plan must depend on circumstances," continued Sybil when the drinks had been served by a dainty 

Japanese waitress in native costume. "If I can discover 143

where Rodmell Armitage has hidden himself I shall seek him out and try to attract him, although I guess that will not be easy, for the man who hasn't fallen for Gloria Drake isn't likely to go goo-goo about Baby

Sybil. Still, you never know. Meantime, I am going to fawn on Miss Gloria and try to win her confidence, and help her to get the man she seems to want. As soon as I have got either or both of them in tow, I cable to the Clarissa to return here."

She paused as if she had explained everything, but 

Spider Sneyd was not satisfied. "And then-vat then?" he queried. "Why, bonehead, I should be able to persuade one or both of them to accept an invitation to lunch or dine with me aboard the Clarissa, and—well, when I get them on board, the rest is up to Otto, you, and the boys. The Englishman, if I find him, will surely not refuse an invitation just to allow me to show how grateful I am to him for his kindness when I was landed at dead of night, and if Gloria Drake is reluctant, the news that he is going to be my guest will surely induce her to accept my invitation. Now do you get the idea?" 

Spider Sneyd nodded his head several times and once again clawed his beard in thought. "Ja! I get you, Sybil, and I think you should be able to bring it off," he said after a pause. “The 

Clarissa will leave Honolulu as soon as I get aboard, and I will cable to you where the yacht is to be found and where to send your message when it is wanted. 

Himmel! if you could get both the man and the woman on board, the fortunes of all of us will be made, and 

Otto will love you for ever. I wish you luck." 144

CHAPTER XII 

8 As

Sybil and Sneyd were discussing final arrangements the suave under-manager of the hotel approached the table at which they were seated, and bowed. "Forgive me if I am interrupting, Miss Laval, but 

Inspector Harrigan wishes to have a few words with you," he said apologetically. "Inspector Harrigan is nominal chief of the detective force of Honolulu, and I think he merely wishes to enquire why you did not pass through the Customs or show your papers to any of the port officials when you landed last night. Inspector Harrigan is in my office, Miss Laval." "I'll see him in a few minutes," responded Sybil calmly. "Please ask the inspector to wait. I'm just giving my attorney some instructions about important legal and business matters."

The under-manager bowed again and withdrew some little distance to await Miss Laval's pleasure. 

Spider Sneyd was again clawing at his beard, but he also had assumed an attitude of indifference. "If the chief detective wants to ask questions, Spider, you'd better clear out quick and tell Otto to get the 

Clarissa away from Honolulu as fast as possible," said 

Sybil quickly, scarcely above a whisper. "Make yourself scarce and be slick about it, for the detective might possibly recognise you and ask some awkward questions. You can trust me not to spill any information that will endanger Otto, even if Mr. Harrigan tries some sort of third degree on me-which I don't think he will." 145

"Yes, yes, I think it would be advisable for me to do ze fade-out quick," Sneyd whispered back to her. "I will get back to the yacht as fast as I can and explain to Otto." "That's the idea. Now get up and shake hands with me, and I'll tell you in a voice that can be heard by anybody who happens to be listening, or trying to listen, at any of the tables round about, that I agree to the terms and you can either mail the contract to me here or bring it personally for my signature." 

Spider Sneyd nodded understanding and faded away after obeying instructions, and after they had both acted a part for the benefit of anyone who happened to be watching them or trying to overhear their conversation. "This way, Miss Laval," said the under-manager, when Sybil had strolled, perfectly self-possessed and almost arrogantly, across the lounge to where he was waiting. "Sorry to trouble you, but you know what officials are."

He escorted her along a corridor to his office and as he opened the door, and stood aside with a courteous gesture inviting her to enter, a stalwart, elderly, well-groomed and soldier-like man sprang to his feet from the armchair in which he had been lounging, laid aside the cigar he was smoking and nodded curtly, regarding her keenly.

Sybil met his searching glance with an unmoved expression, and decided that her role should be that of a rather helpless poor little rich girl, puzzled rather than scared to find herself called upon to answer questions by a detective. "Miss Sybil Laval-Detective-Inspector Harrigan," said the under-manager by way of introduction, and Sybil curtsied and favoured the inspector with her most bewitching smile. 146

"Pleased to meet you, Inspector Harrigan," she exclaimed, and realised instinctively that she had, so to speak, struck the right note, for she read admiration in the detective's eyes. "I suppose I should be scared, as you represent the majesty of the law, but I don't understand what poor little me has done wrong, and hope you are not going to cast me into a dungeon or something of the sort. 99 

Inspector Harrigan smiled, waved his big hand invitingly towards a chair facing him, picked up his cigar and resumed his seat. "I understand, Miss Laval, that you landed at a remote part of the waterfront late last night without reporting to the Customs or to any official, and walked here escorted by a gentleman who then was staying in the hotel," he said in his brusque, incisive fashion. "It is my duty to ask you to explain why you landed at such an hour, where you came from, and what is the purpose of your visit to Honolulu. I must also ask you to allow me to inspect your passport and papers.' 

"Papers?" repeated Sybil, her china-blue eyes opening wide. "I don't understand, Inspector. I'm an 

American citizen, and I was told that I shouldn't need any passport for visiting the Hawaiian Islands or cruising in the South Seas, and there weren't any officials of any kind on duty when I landed from my yacht last night. Why, there wasn't even a taxi, and that was why, feeling rather scared, I appealed to a gentleman in evening dress, whom I had never seen in my life before, to escort me here."

She proceeded to tell her carefully-arranged story about having become tired of cruising after a spell of rough weather and seasickness, and Inspector Harrigan listened in intense silence, his keen grey eyes considering her, and his keen brain wondering whether 147

she was quite as helpless and innocent as she evidently wished him to believe. "The hotel people sent an auto for my baggage, and it was taken right up to my rooms, where the Customs people can inspect everything if they suspect me of being a smuggler, or something of the sort. I am dreadfully sorry if I've committed some sort of technical offence against the laws of Honolulu, but how was I to know where I should have landed and where to find officials when I couldn't even find a taxi?” "The gentleman who escorted you from the waterfront to this hotel was, I understand, Mr. Alan Rodmell Armitage," responded the inspector after a pause. "You say you had never seen him in your life before?" "Never before, but I was so pleased to meet him when we introduced ourselves to each other, and I found out who he was, because I'd read his books and liked them." "How did Mr. Armitage happen to be waiting on the waterfront at the place where you landed, Miss Laval?" "He'd walked down there to see off the captain of the yacht he has chartered," replied Sybil and launched into explanations. "I am so sorry to find he has left this morning without even giving me an opportunity of thanking him for having been so kind and helpful last night."

Inwardly she was fidgeting uneasily under the unflinching scrutiny of Harrigan's keen grey eyes, but she maintained her pose of helpless innocence. "I suppose, Miss Laval, although you have no passport or papers, you can supply me with proofs of your identity?" queried the inspector. "Do you known anyone at Honolulu ?" "Oh, dear! Oh, dear!" sighed Sybil with a pathetic smile. "What a shock to my vanity! Why, only a year 148

ago, Mr. Harrigan, I thought I was quite famous as a film star, and that all the movie fans knew Sybil 

Laval. But I suppose the public forgets frightfully quickly. Miss Gloria Drake-oh, I wonder if I am doing right in revealing the fact that the lady who is known in this hotel as Miss Inez Fernandez is really 

Gloria Drake?-she knows me, and I was talking to her only a little while ago. Incidentally, she, like you, seemed to think that I must have met Mr. Armitage before, and asked me if I knew where he had gone and why he had disappeared." "Do you think that the disappearance of Mr. Rodmell Armitage had anything to do with your arrival here ?" "Say, Inspector Harrigan, are you suggesting that 

I am such a fright that the poor man fled at daybreak in case he'd have to meet me again?" 

She trilled out a high-toned laugh as she asked the question, and the hotel official laughed in sympathy, but Inspector Harrigan only smiled slightly. "Why should Miss Gloria Drake have been under the impression that you could give her information as to the whereabouts of Mr. Armitage, and why he had left the hotel so suddenly this morning?" he demanded. "It is all very puzzling, Mr. Harrigan," replied

Sybil, shaking her platinum blonde head as if in complete bewilderment. "It seems he told her last night that he'd met me, and she didn't believe it was an accident, or that we'd never met before. But Gloria 

Drake and her secretary can assure you I am actually Sybil Laval, if you have any doubt about my identity. 

I'm beginning to feel all hot and bothered, Inspector, and almost wish I'd stayed on my yacht after all, instead of deciding to spend a week or two at the 

Imperial Hawaiian Hotel. I never dreamt that I might be running into some sort of trouble, and of course I 149

hadn't heard about the trouble there was here last night in connection with Miss Gloria Drake. Is there anything else I can tell you?" "My dear Miss Laval, I think I am right in assuring you that Inspector Harrigan is merely carrying out formal official duties in asking you questions, and I can confidently assure you that your stay at the Imperial

Hawaiian Hotel will be made as pleasant and enjoyable as is humanly possible from the point of view of the management," interposed the under-manager in his suave, soothing fashion, and glanced at the inspector as if for confirmation. "I don't think you need worry, Miss Laval," said the inspector with a smile, as he heaved himself out of his chair and deposited the butt of his cigar in an ashtray. "You may have a formal call from the Customs official, but meanwhile all I have to do is to have a word with Miss Gloria Drake and corroborate your statement that she and her secretary know you as Miss Sybil Laval, and the matter will be O.K. as far as I am concerned. Sorry to have troubled you, Miss Laval, but I'm glad to have met you. By the way," he added, as he shook hands, "who was the gentleman you were talking to in the lounge when I arrived?" "Oh, that was my attorney who looks after my business and legal affairs," explained Sybil with an air of frank unconcern. "I invited him on the cruise and he came ashore this morning to make sure before the yacht left that I was properly fixed and to supply me with money to pay bills and so forth. He seemed quite worried about leaving me here without a chaperon.' "Well, you will be able to assure him now that you will have police protection," smiled the hotel official. "If you have any little difficulty, Miss Laval, please remember that we have two lady hostesses and 99 150

chaperons on our staff who will be at your disposal at any time." "Sure, Miss Laval, you may rely on the police looking after you while you are here," added Inspector

Sybil out. "I think I can personally remember Miss

Laval appearing on the films." "Yes, seems O.K.," responded Inspector Harrigan, thoughtfully tugging at his iron-grey moustache, "but

I seem to remember the ugly-looking individual she said was her attorney, and have an idea that we've got him on our records. You might put a message through to Miss Gloria Drake telling her that I wish to interview her. Meantime, with your permission I'll use your telephone."

He found that the yacht Clarissa was in the harbour, and that the registered owner was Sybil Laval, yet he was not altogether satisfied, and gave instructions to the Harbour Police to pay a visit to the yacht and verify Sybil's story. Next he rang up police headquarters, gave further instructions and received reports. "Miss Inez Fernandez will see you at once," announced the hotel manager as the inspector finished telephoning. "I shall be pleased to escort you to her apartments and introduce you."

Ruthless to crooks and criminals and normally brusque, masterful and incisive of manner, Detective- Inspector Harrigan nevertheless had an eye for a pretty woman and a soft heart for the gentler sex in general. Small wonder then, that he found himself lost in admiration of the loveliness of Gloria when he 151

was introduced, and there was no trace of brusqueness in his manner as he questioned her about the events of the night before. He noticed, incidentally, that

Gloria was agitated and seemed to have been crying, and he determined, if possible, to ascertain the cause. "If, as we suspect, the men who tried to kidnap you are the Dandy Hagen gang, can you suggest how it was possible they discovered that your guards were off duty?" he enquired of Gloria. "I don't understand how the gang could have known in advance that Powell and Connolly would be off duty," Gloria answered. "The guards didn't know themselves that they were going to have the evening off." "Huh! that seems to clear them, and I'm not suggesting the possibility that they may be in the pay of the gang, but I'll have a word with both of them later, just to satisfy myself," resumed the inspector. "Tell me, please, Miss Drake, what you know about Miss 

Sybil Laval." "I don't really know her at all, Inspector," Gloria replied. "I seem to remember meeting her at some reception or première in Hollywood a year or so ago, and I think I have seen her in the studios, but I am not sure that I recognised her when I sent for her to-day." "May I ask why you sent for her to-day, Miss Drake?" "I wanted to know if she could offer any explanation of the sudden disappearance of Mr. Rodmell Armitage, and if she could tell me where and why he had gone. It was Mr. Armitage who told me, late last night, that he had met her and escorted her to the hotel." "Had you met Mr. Armitage before-I mean before he arrived here? It is part of my business to be suspicious of everyone when anything mysterious happens, or a crime is attempted, and it strikes me as 152

distinctly queer that the gang should have known in advance that you and Mr. Armitage would be dancing together in the hotel grounds, and that they apparently made no attempt to shoot Mr. Armitage. They're a desperate bunch who never hesitate to use their guns, and how a couple of men put the four or five of them to flight without shooting, is beyond me.” "For the love of Mike, Inspector!" broke in Bunty impulsively. "You're no' suggestin' that Mr. Armitage is in league with the gang? If ye'd been there ye'd understand how two brave Englishmen put the scoundrels tae flight and prevented ony shootin'."

She proceeded to give a vivid and dramatic account of the fight as she had seen it, and Harrigan listened with interest, his eyes twinkling, then thanked her politely. "You haven't told me, Miss Drake, if you had met

Mr. Armitage before," he continued, turning to Gloria again. "It will be my duty to ask him for explanations when I interview him during the course of the next hour or two, Miss Drake," responded Inspector Harrigan, and was almost startled by the effect of his words. 

Gloria sprang to her feet, her hand pressed to her heart, her hazel eyes glowing with sudden excitement. "You know where he is ?" she gasped breathlessly. "Why, of course we know where he is," answered 

Harrigan. "Didn't Police Captain Smith tell you last 153

night that we posted extra guards around the hotel, and don't you realise that I have had detectives mixing with the guests here, as well as a force of detectives and police trying to discover what became of the Dandy Hagen gang when they made their get-away in the speed-boat? When Mr. Rodmell Armitage and his secretary, Mr. Horatio Flower, left the hotel early this morning, they were shadowed by one of my men, who has reported they are now located at an hotel at 

Haleiwa, where Mr. Armitage has apparently announced his decision to stay for at least three weeks."

He was watching Gloria keenly as he spoke, still admiring the glamorous beauty of her, but wondering, at the same time, what was the cause of her agitation and excitement, wondering if she were in love with 

Rodmell Armitage and wondering, if that were the case, why Armitage had disappeared. He guessed, he told himself mentally, as he watched and waited, that he would have had no inclination to run away if a girl like Gloria Drake had fallen for him. "Where is Haleiwa?" enquired Gloria after a pause.

Harrigan told her, describing the place in fact, much in the same way as Sam Simpson had described it to 

Alan on the previous night. "I shall be going to Haleiwa this afternoon after lunch, Miss Drake, just to interview Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower and get their version of last night's affair, and if you care to accompany me I shall be honoured and pleased," he concluded. "I guess you'll feel quite safe with me in a police car, and I can tell you the run to Haleiwa is very beautiful and well worth making." "I'll come with pleasure, Mr. Harrigan," exclaimed

Gloria, impulsively holding out her hand. "We shall both have quite a lot of questions to ask Rodmell Armitage." 154

"Ye're not going without me, Miss Gloria," interposed Bunty again, and everyone laughed, including Mr. Cyrus O. Cole, who had sat like a mute after shaking hands with Inspector Harrigan at the beginning of the interview.

Cyrus Cole, although he had not the slightest reason in the world to be afraid of them, always felt nervous in the presence of police officers, whether they were in uniform or not, and he had been badly shaken by the events of the previous night. But now he pulled himself together and rose from his chair, in which he had been sitting as if he were numb from the waist downward. "Say, that's real good of you, Mr. Harrigan," he exclaimed. "Try one of my cigars and do me the pleasure of having something to drink with me. My little girl has been quite worried about Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower having disappeared this morning, but 

I can guess the reason of their disappearance even if you can't. I guess a little champagne wouldn't do any of us any harm, Mr. Harrigan." "I guess you've guessed right, Mr. Cole," responded the inspector with a deep-throated laugh. "But tell me what you think is the reason for Mr. Armitage and 

Mr. Flower levanting so suddenly early this morning from the Imperial Hawaiian?” "Well, it's like this, you see, Inspector," explained

Mr. Cole, producing glasses from the sideboard and a bottle of champagne from a portable refrigerator; "Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower are modest Englishmen who don't want to pose as heroes, and they were worried about the reception that was given them when they arrived here on the Golden Gate after having saved my life. Mr. Armitage, I'm told, bolted out of the hotel last night to avoid congratulations on saving little girl from the kidnapping gang, and I guess he my 155

and his delightfully amusing secretary, Mr. Flower, decided to make their escape this morning before anyone was up in order to avoid Pressmen and photographers and police enquiries-if you'll forgive me for bringing in the police. I can assure you that you can banish from your mind any suspicions concerning Mr. 

Armitage or Mr. Flower, Inspector. They are both real English gentlemen, and you can judge from what

Bunty-meaning Miss Gilmour-told you that they are brave as well as gentle." 

Harrigan nodded his iron-grey head as he accepted a large glass of champagne and one of Mr. Cole's villainous-looking long black cigars. "Congratulations on your escape, Miss Drake," he said, smiling at Gloria over the rim of his glass before drinking. “You've got nothing to worry about now that Captain Smith and myself have taken your case in hand, and I think none of the Dandy Hagen gang will be able to show his face in Honolulu, or, for that matter, anywhere in Oahu, without being arrested within five minutes. . Yes, you are right, Mr.

Cole, any two men who could put the Dandy Hagen gang on the run without any shooting must sure have nerve. I shall look forward to meeting Mr. Rodmell 

Armitage and Mr. Horatio Flower this afternoon. 

Your good health, sir!" He drank his champagne, accepted a light for his cigar from Mr. Cole, arranged to call in his auto for 

Gloria and Bunty after luncheon, and took his departure after shaking hands all around, to interrogate 

Joe Connolly and Spike Powell, both of whom protested vehemently and with a certain degree of profanity that they had merely been on the spree the previous night, and if they had had the slightest suspicion that Miss Gloria would have been in danger 156

they'd have come back and shot the Dandy Hagen gang to pieces. "Perhaps one of you will tell me who told the

Hagen gang you were going off duty?" suggested the inspector in his most ferocious manner. "Perhaps you met one of the gang in a saloon as soon as you went off duty and started spilling information? Answer that!"

Powell and Connolly both swore by all that was holy and unholy that they were innocent as new-born babes, as loyal to Miss Gloria Drake as the most loyal subject of King George of England, and luridly asserted that if anyone whom they had suspected of being a member of the Dandy Hagen gang had questioned them they'd have shot him without compunction, and hoped he would die without benefit of clergy -or words to that effect.

Detective-Inspector Harrigan left, looking dissatisfied and leaving them worried, and departed after a word or two with the manager of the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel for police headquarters where he gave, Before leaving for lunch, a variety of orders, including one to the effect that Miss Sybil Laval was to be shadowed, and any letters addressed to her or posted by her, and any cables she sent out or received from the yacht Clarissa, were to be opened and reported to him forthwith. He also ordered the best car in the police fleet to be in readiness to take him, Miss Inez Fernandez, and Miss Gilmour to Haleiwa on his return from lunch. 157

CHAPTER XIII 

83 LOSSOM, despite his bulk, usually moved about 

BLO 

as noiselessly as a cat, but Alan was a very light sleeper, and the sound of Blossom brushing against one of the branches of the banyan tree under which he was dozing was sufficient to make him sit up in his long cane chair, instantly fully awake. "Well?" he enquired, immediately aware from Blossom's expression that something disturbing had happened. "Eve has invaded Eden," announced Blossom, "and with her she has brought Detective-Inspector Harrigan, who seems to be chief boss of the Honolulu police, and both of them seem to want to ask you questions. 

I am afraid there is no hope of escape, my dear Alan, and you'll have to decide whether to see Miss Gloria and Inspector Harrigan out here or in the hotel, at which they arrived a few minutes ago by motor-car." 

Alan scowled at him as he groped in his pocket for his cigarette-case, and then suddenly he laughed. "I suppose you are breaking it gently, Blossom?” he commented. "You are going to tell me that the motor-car in which the lovely lady arrived with the inspector also carried Mr. Van Rosen, her publicity agent, her poppa, a couple of reporters and a Press photographer." "No, no, Alan, it isn't as bad as all that," responded 

Blossom, laughing in turn. "Miss Gloria is accompanied by Bunty and the detective by an armed police officer. The chauffeur, incidentally, is also an armed police officer. What are we going to do about it?" 158

Alan shook his head, slung his long legs over the side of the chair in which he had been reclining, rose to his feet and then sat down again and lit a cigarette. "Better bring them out here, Blossom," he said at last. "I don't wish to see them, and if they wish to see me—well, let the mountain come to Mahomet." "Right oh!" said Blossom after nodding his pink head several times, and he trotted away in his noiseless fashion, to reappear after an interval of a few minutes, accompanied by Gloria and Harrigan. "Glad to know you, Mr. Armitage," said the inspector after Blossom had performed a perfunctory sort of introduction. "Perhaps I should apologise for intruding, but I am as anxious to know why you and Mr. 

Flower suddenly disappeared from Honolulu early this morning as Miss Gloria Drake seems to be." "Rodmell, why did you suddenly vanish without leaving any sort of message for me?" broke in Gloria. "You must already have made up your mind to leave before we parted in the early hours of this morning, and yet you said nothing about your intentions. I have been terribly distressed and upset." "So upset that you have apparently engaged the police to track me down to this beautiful haven of refuge," drawled Alan, meeting her reproving glance with a cold and inscrutable expression. "I have already endeavoured to explain to you that I object to being used as a sort of pawn in any publicity stunt, and even if last night's affair was not a publicity stunt, I would remind both you and Inspector Harrigan that I am, so to speak, a free agent, at liberty to go where I will without offering any explanations to anyone in advance. 

Am I to understand, Inspector, that I should have asked for permission to leave Honolulu ?"

Inspector Harrigan shook his head and tugged at his iron-grey moustache as his keen eyes glanced from Alan 159

to Gloria, who looked as if she were on the verge of tears and back again. "Say, Mr. Armitage, I think you've got this thing all wrong," he said abruptly. "Miss Drake did not engage me to find you. As you know, following last night's affair extra police were put on duty in and around the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel with orders to report any suspicious movements on the part of any of the guests, and you will admit, I think, that the officers were entitled to believe that you and Mr. 

Flower leaving the hotel early in the morning was a suspicious circumstance. Perhaps you will be good enough to explain just why you decided to leave the hotel early this morning and came here?" "You already heard me explain, Inspector, that I do not want publicity," replied Alan. "Mr. Flower and I came to Honolulu expecting peace and quiet, but found ourselves unwittingly and unwillingly forced into the limelight for which people in the film world seem constantly to crave." "Don't be so brutally cruel!" exclaimed Gloria with a break in her voice. "I don't crave for publicity, and -and it hurts me to hear you talk like that. It isn't fair!" "I'm sorry if you don't understand my point of view, but-well, there it is," said Alan, his face still expressionless. "I'm quite sure Mr. Armitage doesna mean tae hurt ye, Miss Gloria," chimed in Bunty. "Why, of course not," added Blossom quickly. "He would not hurt anyone intentionally except anyone who tried to do you or Miss Gloria any harm. What about having some tea? I expect the resources of the Haleiwa 

Hotel run to tea. Let's go and enquire, and then I'll show you round the grounds while Inspector Harrigan 160

and Mr. Armitage discuss whatever it is that the inspector wants to discuss."

His tactful suggestion relieved the tension, and as he led Gloria and Bunty away Alan resumed his seat on the side of the long cane chair, and Harrigan sat down beside him and accepted the cigarette. "Tell me, Inspector, why have you brought Miss

Gloria Drake here?" enquired Alan, taking the initiative. "Well, she seemed worried about what had become of you and why you had suddenly disappeared, so I thought I'd invite her to come along," answered the inspector. "I guess there are very few men who would object to being called on by Miss Gloria Drake, and who wouldn't feel flattered by the fact that she was worried about them. You must be rather an exceptional sort of man, Mr. Armitage.' 

"If being an exceptional sort of man, Inspector, means that I don't want to be bothered by women, Pressmen, Press photographers, police officials and the plaudits of a crowd of overfed holiday makers and excitement seekers, then you can put me down as exceptional," retorted Alan. "Will you believe me when I tell you that I'm almost sorry I rescued Mr. Cyrus

Cole from drowning and found myself mixed up in last night's affair as a result?" "You sure are an exceptional man, Mr. Armitage," commented Harrigan, surveying him with a perplexed expression. "Tell me your version of what happened last night when Dandy Hagen came on the scene. 

Alan told him, briefly and concisely, and Harrigan asked many questions, not only about the affray but also about Miss Sybil Laval, and rose at last to his feet quite convinced that the Englishman had answered him truthfully and sincerely, and that prior to the previous night he had never seen either Sybil Laval or 161

Otto Hagen or any of the latter's gang, and that his sudden departure from the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel had simply been due to his desire to be "left alone." 

He told Armitage so in his curt, incisive fashion at the end of his cross-examination, and Alan smiled. "Thanks, Inspector," he drawled. "So that't that!

Now I suppose we had better join the ladies and try to have a nice little tea party. I notice that my most excellent secretary and friend is showing them the beauties of the gardens, but that every now and again they cast anxious glances in this direction, probably wondering if you are going to arrest me on suspicion of having thrown Mr. Cyrus Oke Cole overboard from the Golden Gate and organised last night's kidnapping attempt." 99 The inspector laughed heartily and slapped him on the back. "Say, Mr. Armitage, I guess I was right when I said you're an exceptional man," he exclaimed. "Sure, let's join the ladies, and I'll tell them in a manner of speaking, that you leave the court without a stain, or a suspicion of a stain, upon your character, and highly commended for your grit and pluck, plus your modesty." "Spare my blushes, Inspector, and merely reward me by promising not to tell the Pressmen where I am hiding myself," said Alan laughingly.

They wandered across to where Blossom (so Alan guessed) was drawing upon his imagination to answer the questions of the ladies as to the names of the tropical plants they were admiring. Gloria's eyes were very bright and her lovely face was slightly flushed as she looked quickly and enquiringly from the inspector to Alan, relieved to find that both were smiling. "Everything O.K., lady," announced the inspector. "I'll confess any suspicions I had have been banished, 162

and that I agree with what Miss Gilmour said. Yes, I guess verything's O.K." "Yes, I guess everything is O.K. here, Mr. Harrigan," said Gloria, trying to mimic his voice and manner. With the result that everyone laughed. “I think this is quite the loveliest place I have ever been in, or even imagined in all my life," she added, with change of manner, turning to Alan. "I wonder-I wonder if you would very much mind if I came to stay here?"

Taken aback, Alan stared at her for a few moments in surprised silence, then recovered himself, uncomfortably aware of the fact that everyone was gazing at him expectantly. "I'm afraid I don't quite understand, Miss Gloria," he answered at last, and his voice was colder and his manner more formal than he intended. "It is not in my power to prevent you from staying in Haleiwa if you wish to do so." "I know that, but-but will you disappear again if I tell you that I have decided to stay here?" faltered

Gloria, and looked so appealing and wistful that Alan felt he had been behaving like a boor.

He was granted a few minutes to think things over, for at that juncture a Japanese servant appeared to announce that tea was served on the hotel veranda. "Good!" cried Blossom, rubbing his plump hands together and beaming on everyone through his big glasses. "Next to a whisky and soda I know of nothing better than a cup of tea as an aid to amicable discussion of any problem.' 99 Again the tension was eased, but all were conscious of a certain air of constraint as they seated themselves around the table on the veranda, and Bunty, talking at random, began to pour out tea. "So you've fallen in love with Haleiwa, Miss 163

Drake?" enquired Inspector Harrigan, his eyes twinkling. "Yes, I think it's the loveliest place beyond compare that I have ever seen in my life," said Gloria, drawing a deep breath. "If Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower will not object to my presence I should love to stay here." "But would it be safe, Miss Gloria ?" interposed 

Bunty. "What do you think, Inspector?" "Wait! Hear me out," continued Gloria quickly. "No one in Honolulu knows where I am at present, and no one here in Haleiwa-except you, of courseknows who I am. Do believe me, Rodmell," she added, turning to Alan, "when I tell you that I am as anxious to avoid publicity as you are, and to find a haven of refuge. I could stay on here and feel safe, particularly if I knew that you and Mr. Flower were near, and no one need know what has become of me. I feel sure 

Inspector Harrigan could pledge the people at the hotel to secrecy. I could keep on my suite there and none of the other guests need know that I have left. The hotel people could explain that I am so much upset by the events of the last couple of days that I have been ordered to remain quietly indoors for a few weeks and receive no visitors." "Now, I call that a real brainwave !" cried the inspector, regarding Gloria admiringly, and still with a twinkle in his grey eyes. "I can fix things with the people at the Imperial Hawaiian, and I'd suggest that you leave your guards, Connolly and Powell there, to give everyone not in the know the impression that you are still at the hotel. I can also arrange for a couple of detective officers to be on duty here, posing as tourists or holiday makers, which would, I think, add to your feeling of security." regard Alan enquiringly and appealingly. "What do you say, Rodmell?" "You could, of course, register here under another assumed name, and perhaps disguise yourself in some way to avoid the possibility of recognition by anyone who knows you." San Francisco. You can choose whatever name you like for yourself." "Imphmn! I'm wonderin' what your father will say to this arrangement," remarked Bunty. "I'm thinkin' he'll be awfu' nervous about ye.'

Hotel to Haleiwa without giving anyone any clue to its destination or the whereabouts of Miss Inez Fernandez, who is still supposed to be in the hotel." Do you get me?" 166

"Aye, I get ye, Inspector," replied Bunty after a pause; “and I'm beginnin' tae think it's a guid idea after a', and that yer no' blate at the uptak'.' 99 "Say, what sort of language is that, Miss Gilmour ?" enquired the inspector. "No' blate at the uptak'?' 

What does that mean? I speak a bit of Japanese, a bit of Hawaiian, some Portuguese and Filipino, and quite a lot of good English and American, but you've got me beat on Scotch." "It means, Inspector," explained Blossom, as Bunty dissolved into laughter, "that you are not slow of understanding. I know, because I am half Scotch and half soda, myself. Left alone on the veranda, Alan and Gloria regarded each other half apprehensively and with mingled feelings. Alan, for his part, found himself for 167

once in a way feeling shy and nervous, while Gloria was feeling that she had behaved with almost brazen audacity, and was wondering if Alan was of the same opinion. I regard you as a nuisance?" responded Alan. "If you feel safer and happier here than you did at Honolulu 

I shall feel glad for your sake. As far as I can judge at the moment, we both seem to have possibilities of enjoying a real holiday, thanks to Detective-Inspector Harrigan.' "Promise me, Rodmell, that you won't run away from me again," pleaded Gloria, moving her chair a little closer to him and laying her hand on his arm. How could you be so cruel, Rodmell? Don't tell me that you despise me and that you were really in earnest when you said that

I mean no more to you than any charwoman." "I thought I had already explained fully to you that that expression was used merely with the idea of restoring your sense of proportion, and you will remember I asked you to accept my apologies if I had offended you, my dear Gloria," exclaimed Alan with a sigh, running his fingers through his hair with a sort of distracted gesture. "I am sorry if I hurt you again this morning by disappearing without leaving any message, but I did not want to be again molested by a mob of Pressmen, and thought it best just to disappear and drop out of your life." "I-I am trying to understand, Rodmell, but-but 168

do I mean as little to you as that?" asked Gloria with a break in her husky, musical voice, after a tense pause. "Were you running away from the horror of publicity this morning, or were you, perhaps, running away from love?" "Put it down to publicity, Miss Gloria," answered

Alan with forced calmness. "I hate being fussed over, and I loathe the idea of being hailed and advertised as a hero, simply because I sloshed a man on the jaw. 

Besides that, the methods of your American Pressmen scare and disgust me. Put me down as a coward, if you like, for running away from publicity without informing you in advance that I intended to do so, in case you made another scene. As for running away from love, although I have written stories and plays concerned with the chief emotion of life, I confess I do not know what love means." "And you have no desire to learn?" "Not if love is the mere sensual, sexual stuff that

I see presented on the films, and the sloppy sentiment 

I encounter occasionally in real life. I have my own ideal of love, and when-if ever-it comes into my life, I think it will be a master passion from which I shall make no endeavour to escape. You, perhaps, know the meaning of love?"

Gloria's eyes met his direct gaze half defiantly, and she shook her chestnut head. "I don't know or understand what people mean when they talk of love being a master passion, but—but I think

"What need to make any formal promise, Gloria ?" responded Alan. “I have already explained that I have decided to stay here until my yacht is ready. I hope nothing will occur during our stay here to cause you any further distress or apprehension."

Gloria looked at him, her head slightly tilted aside, her lovely face dimpling into smiles and her wonderful eyes very bright. "I wonder if that means that I mean more than a decrepit charwoman after all?" she said softly. "And I wonder if perhaps we both may discover in this set of earthly paradise that is called Haleiwa what love means-real love. Rodmell, dear"

She broke off, for at that moment there came the sound of excited yelping and Rodmell Homestall King, otherwise Imp, hurled himself out of the hotel doorway as fast as his little stumpy legs could carry him, made one leap on to Gloria's lap and proceeded vigorously to lick her face with a wet, pink tongue. Following him appeared Bunty, laughing. "Ye'd shut him in, Mr. Armitage, but he made sic a din as I was passing your door to inspect our rooms that I couldna resist openin' it on the way back, and he was so glad to see me that he nearly tore the clothes off my back and then bolted doon the stairs," she explained as Alan was trying to rescue Gloria from 

Imp's onslaught of affection. "I don't mind at all," Gloria protested, choking with laughter and cuddling the dog close to her. "It's something to be loved by someone really and truly, even if the someone is only a pedigree Sealyham." 170

"Garrrran! What do you think of that, master?" demanded Rodmell Homestall King, as Alan threatened him. "Woof, woof, woof! Garrrran, you great bully! What d'yer mean by shuttin' me up in a strange room with people here waiting to be licked? Hoi! 

What was that funny thing that went scuttling under the bushes? 'Scuse me! Grrrr! Hooray! Woof!" 

He leapt from Gloria's arms and dashed pell-mell and headlong into a flowering shrub, yelping excitedly, and the three humans could do nothing but laugh and laugh. The two other humans, to wit, Inspector Harrigan and Horatio Flower, appeared on the veranda to discover the cause of the commotion, and they joined in the laughter as they watched Imp's comic antics as he chased some real or imaginary animal or insect round and round the gardens. "I fixed up rooms, Mis Gloria, and I think you'll like them, although there's naething elaborate aboot them," announced Bunty, when the laughter subsided.
