CHAPTER XIV 

8 “H 

ERE, Miss Gloria, I canna quite get the hang of things, and it seems tae me that ye're no' playing fair," remarked Bunty suddenly to Gloria, as the latter was about to retire two nights later. “Are ye in love wi' the man or are ye no'?" 

Gloria opened her sleepy eyes wide and gazed at her smilingly as she picked up her nightdress. "My dear Bunty, he said he didn't know the meaning of love, and I'm just beginning to teach him," she responded. "Dear old Pop really helped matters forward when he came to say good-bye this afternoon, accompanied by Inspector Harrigan, and told Rodmell he was sure I'd be safe in his charge; but you probably noticed that Rodmell, although he said to Pop that he would do his best to make me feel happy and carefree, declined to accept any responsibility for me after his yacht is ready to sail. That, according to what he has been told by Captain Simpson, will be in about three weeks' time. Would you like to bet me, Bunty, that at the end of three weeks Rodmell Armitage is not hopelessly in love with me?" 99 "No, Miss Gloria, and that's what I meant when I said that I dinna think you're playin' fair unless you're in love wi' the man,' answered Bunty. "I'll ask ye again-are ye in love with Rodmell Armitage, or are ye only acting as if ye were playin' for a film? If I hadna watched ye acting I'd swear that ye're head ower heels in love with him, and so would anybody else that's been watching you since we came here.' "Thanks for the compliment, Bunty," laughed 99 173

Gloria. "That seems to prove that I really am an excellent actress. I rather think that in less than three weeks dear Rodmell will be hopelessly in love with me, and that when he departs for his cruise in the 

South Seas he will be heartbroken. . . . Why stare at me, Bunty, as if you were a Calvinistic minister and 

I a heretic? Remember Rodmell told me I meant no more to him than any decrepit charwoman, and then when I first made love to him, left without a word, showing that he despised me, or, at least, thought me of little account." "He saved your father's life, and he saved you frae the Dandy Hagen gang," remarked Bunty in a hard voice. "Mebbe it's because I had some sort o' Calvinistic upbringing that it doesna seem fair tae me for tae break his heart in jest." "For the love of Mike, Bunty, don't glower at me in that ghoulishly reproving fashion!" cried Gloria, lighting a cigarette. "I don't actually mean that I want, literally, to break Rodmell Armitage's heart, but I certainly intend to try to make him fall in love with me and then to tell him that I only did it to restore his sense of proportion. You will remember he told me that women did not interest him, and that his reference to my meaning no more to him than any decrepit charwoman was intended to restore my sense of proportion as far as he was concerned. I am grateful to him, of course, for saving Pop and me, but he hurt and offended me afterwards, and I feel that if he's not interested in women I've got to show him that I'm not interested in men. Have a cigarette, Bunty, and don't look so glum, or I'll begin to suspect you of being in love with Rodmell Armitage yourself.' . Bunty accepted a cigarette, lit it and smoked in thoughtful silence for a minute or two. "I think Mr. Alan Rodmell Armitage is one of the 174

finest gentlemen I have ever met in my life, although he's mebbe no' sae amusing as Mr. Flower, who has gi'en me permission tae call him Blossom since he took tae callin' me Bunty," she said deliberately at last, "and I hate the thought o' your breaking Mr. Armitage's heart after a' he's done, if that's yer intention. 

And I'd remind you, Miss Gloria, that Mr. Flower warned ye that ye'd be playing with fire, or words tae that effect, if ye tried tae vamp Mr. Armitage. Blossom, like me, thinks that Mr. Armitage is one of the finest gentlemen in the world. Mebbe ye don't know that he saved his life as well." "How did that happen?" enquired Gloria, suddenly interested. "It's a long story," explained Bunty, "but a very great and beautiful lady in England led Blossom to believe she was in love with him, and then broke his heart by laughing at him-as I suppose you mean to do to 

Rodmell Armitage-and marrying somebody else. Mr. 

Flower at that time was a professor in an English university, but he disappeared, and Mr. Armitage found him somewhere in Arabia. He'd taken to drugs, and he'd very nearly 'gone native,' but Mr. Armitage rescued him and made a man of him again. Miss 

Gloria, my dearie, I'd be glad if I thought you were falling in love wi' Alan Armitage, but I beg of you not to break his heart just to satisfy your ain vanity. I'm thinking Mr. Armitage would be even more dangerous as an enemy than he is guid as a friend." "I don't know what I should do without you, Bunty, but when you assume your Calvinistic air and start lecturing me I find you boresome," responded Gloria with a shrug. "You talk of my conceit. What about the conceit of Rodmell Armitage, who professes to have almost contempt for women? Wasn't his remark to me that no girl had ever affected him and that he did not 175

know the meaning of love almost a direct challenge?

He talked about restoring my sense of proportion. It may do him good if I restore his sense of proportion by teaching him what unrequited love means. Now I am going to bed." 

Given glamorously beautiful tropical surroundings and a gloriously and glamorously beautiful girl exerting all her powers of fascination to win the love of a man, there could be but one result. Within ten days 

Alan, for the first time in his life, had succumbed to the wiles of a woman and confessed himself wholeheartedly in love.

At first he had suspected that he was being "vamped," but Gloria seemed so absolutely sincere that he began almost to hate himself for his suspicions, and gradually, almost in spite of himself, the spark of love kindled to flame in his heart and became a passionate emotion.

The days passed almost like a dream in the night, a dream of loveliness. By day they had bathed together in the azure, milk-warm waters, sailed together in the glass-bottomed boat owned by the odd-looking native, sailed also in queer native outrigged canoes to watch the flying-fishes darting over their heads in a way that reminded Rodmell of the swallows in summer in his native Sussex, and they had wandered through what seemed oceans of tropical flowers which fringed every lane and roadway, almost overcome at times by the heavy perfume.

Gloria knew instinctively that Alan's resistanceif that be the word-to her powers of attraction, was weakening, and that he was beginning to find real pleasure in her companionship and in her close proximity. But the climax for which she had been hoping and acting came with unexpected suddenness. 176

Both were music lovers, and as they enjoyed their coffee and cigarettes after dinner they had listened by radio to a master musician playing Chopin, listened in tense silence with their minds and hearts attuned to the music which was stirring their deepest emotions. When the recital was over they were like people awakened from a dream, and Alan drew a long breath and gazed deep into Gloria's wonderful eyes. "Let's go out?" he suggested after a pause. "To listen to anything else would be a sort of anticlimax." 

Gloria nodded assent without speaking, and slipped her hand through his arm as together they walked slowly and silently out into the perfume-laden gardens and took the path that led down to the little moonlit beach on which the surf seemed to be breaking with musical rhythm.

At a turn of the path where the palm trees meeting overhead made a velvety darkness, intershot by silvery spears, Gloria stumbled, clutched hard at Alan's elbow, and next moment she was in his arms, strained close to his breast. "Gloria, I love you, I love you," he breathed. "I love you, darling heart, love you as I never hoped, expected, or desired to love any woman. Make me happy, my Gloria, by telling me that you love me." "Rodmell, darling, I—I have been trying to show you that I loved you," responded Gloria breathlessly and scarcely above a whisper, "and now that I know you love me I-oh, I am to proud, so proud and happy."

There was triumph in her heart as their lips met in a kiss that seemed almost to make the world stand still, and yet she was conscious of an emotion the like of which she had never before experienced. Alan's kisses seemed to be drinking the very heart out of her, and his arms around her were like iron bands which seemed 177

to be crushing her so close as almost as if he wished to make her part of himself. "Please, please, darling bear, let me go," she gasped at last. "You are crushing the life out of me." "Sorry, sweetheart," exclaimed Alan with a breathless laugh, as he released her. "It is such a joyous delight to hug you that I almost wish I were an octopus instead of a bear, because then I could hug you with eight arms instead of two. Kiss me, again, belovedest, and tell me again that you love me and are all mine."

Gloria hesitated only for a few moments, her breath coming and going unsteadily, her heart racing and throbbing as it had never done before, and then her arms encircled Alan's neck and she kissed him. "I do love you, Rodmell mine," she whispered between kisses. "I have never loved any other man, and never will. Oh, darling, I am so glad and proud to have won your love, my hero, and you have made me so happy. . . . Oh, darlingest, don't crush me so close, and don't be so greedy for kisses. I can hardly breathe." "Sorry, sweetheart," said Alan again. "I hardly know what I am doing. I am drunk with love and delight and happiness at the thought of having won the love of the most beautiful and enchanting woman in the whole world." "Darling! It is so sweet and wonderful to know that you think of me like that now," breathed Gloria as he released her again. "I think you the most wonderful man in the world, and I am so proud to know I have won your love and admiration, and that no other girl has ever before succeeded in doing so. Let's go down to the beach and sit in the moonlight and talk. I want you to tell me all about yourself, my darling hero."

She slipped her hand around his arm again as she spoke, and gave it an affectionate little squeeze, and together they went down the path, on the sides of 178

which, at places, there were cascades of flowers perfuming the air but looking, somehow, unreal in the moonlight.

On the beach, which the moon had turned into a bed of silver, and on the fringe of which the phosphorescent sea was murmuring and surging, they sat close together and talked. Thrilled as he had never been before, Alan talked about himself and opened his heart to Gloria and revealed his real self. He told her about the little estate in Rodmell, that dear, delectable spot, enclosed in a fold of the Sussex Downs, from which he took his middle name, and which had been the heritage of the Armitages for generations. "This tropical beauty is wonderful, but England is home, and I am sure you will love 

Sussex. My father and mother have been dead for years, but Rodmell Court is mine and is being looked after by two dear old aunts, who, every time I go home, urge and implore me to marry and settle down in my ancestral home. Yes, I feel sure, Gloria, you will love 

Sussex, and you will love Rodmell best of all."

Gloria was conscious of a sudden pang in her heart and had a sense of guilt. She realised, as Alan went on to picture her as the mistress of Rodmell Court, that she had done something bigger and perhaps more dangerous than she had anticipated in winning Alan's love and breaking down his natural reserve, but she told herself that she had no occasion for fear, and no reason to reproach herself for what she had achieved, and what she intended doing. Rodmell Armitage, the lovely wife of the young squire, is assured of a great reception." "Mrs. Alan Rodmell Armitage!" repeated Gloria, and trilled out a laugh. He shook hands again, a litle perplexed, incidentally, by Bunty's seeming lack of enthusiasm, and just then Imp, who had been sleeping in a most inartistic attitude with his short legs directed heavenwards in a remote corner of the lounge, suddenly came to life and intruded. He realised, when he saw his beloved master and Blossom shaking hands that something special must have happened while he was fast asleep, and that the lovely woman he had consented to "adopt" must be concerned in the affair, since she was standing there smiling. So he barged in as fast as his short legs would permit, tried to climb up the long legs of everyone concerned with the idea of licking their faces, and then, finding that nobody seemed to want to be licked, stood in the middle of the group of humans and barked, growled, and yelped in a fashion that set all the humans laughing. "Hoi!" he shouted in his doggy language. "What's this all about? I'm Rodmell Homestall King, I am, and

I've taken prizes at any number of dog shows, but now even my beloved master tells me to keep quiet, and only looks at the female human who smells so nice and has such fine cuddley arms. Woof! woof! woof! I'm not going to be neglected. Hoi, woman, what about it ?"

He stood up on his hind legs as he spoke, in the attitude known to humans as "begging," waved his front paws in the air and gazed up at Gloria appeal- 182

ingly, with the result that she stooped down, still laughing, and gathered him up in her arms, to his great content. "The occasion, I was about to say before I was interrupted by Rodmell Homestall King, now engaged in licking Miss Gloria's face-and putting his tongue out at me and his master when he is not otherwise engaged-seems to call for champagne," announced 

Blossom, still laughing, and waving his podgy arms. "What do you think, Bunty?" "Och! I'm just thinking, Blossom, and just wondering," responded Bunty, dropping into a chair. "There's times when I just sit and think, and there's times when I just sit. Still, I wouldnae wonder but what champagne is a guid idea on your part, you queer guy, and if I knew how to call the Japanese waiter in his own language I'd shout for him."

There was no occasion to shout for the Japanese waiter, who, together with a Japanese waitress in her national costume, was hovering about at a respectful distance, for as soon as Blossom looked round and raised his finger, both darted forward smilingly.

Champagne was duly served, toasts were drunk, and 

Blossom, looking and behaving more than ever like 

Mr. Pickwick, made several little complimentary speeches and beamed on everyone and everything. "Here, Blossom, it's no' fair of us tae sit here playing gooseberry, as ye micht say," exclaimed Bunty after a time, jumping to her feet. "Take me for a walk round the gardens, and I'll see whether I canna feel sentimental and romantic." "A great pleasure, Bunty," said Blossom readily, and bowed as gallantly as his figure permitted as he proffered his arm. "I am feeling quite romantic myself to-night."

They went out together, but it was not until they 183

were some little distance from the hotel, and out of earshot of the two or three people seated in the grounds, that Bunty spoke. Then she asked suddenly: "Are you really happy about this affair, Blossom, or are you just acting?" "Acting, my dear girl?" Blossom responded in a surprised tone. "I assure you I am truly and sincerely pleased and happy that Alan has at last fallen in love and found a mate worthy of him. I have felt all along that if only he learned the real and vital meaning of love, and his heart and soul were stirred to their depths, he might produce a masterpiece. His love scenes, to my mind, although beautifully written, have always lacked fire, and have hardly rung true. Often I have had to beg of him to introduce more sentiment and to write, as it were, from his heart rather than his keen, logical brain. Now that he is in love I prophesy that his next book will be the greatest of his career.' "Imphmn! And do you think it'll improve his work if he breaks his heart?" enquired Bunty dryly. "Do you imagine that Miss Gloria is really in love with him? Have ye forgotten that night in the Imperial 

Hawaiian when she swore that she'd vamp him, and have ye forgotten what I told you about the way she carried on when she found you and him had disappeared?" "My God!" Blossom stopped short with a gasp, his blue eyes goggling. "You don't mean that you think

Gloria has merely carried out her threat, and is only acting? Great Heaven! if she is only trifling with Alan and makes him suffer as a woman once made me suffer years ago, I shall feel like poisoning her. Bunty, I can't believe it possible that so sweet and lovely a creature would deliberately ruin Alan Armitage's life merely to gratify her own vanity. I can't believe she is not genuinely in love." 184

"I am hoping and praying that you're right, Blossom," Bunty responded with a sigh. "It certainly looks like the real thing-I mean she looks to-night as if she were deeply in love with him and radiantly happy, but I've seen her look like that many a time when she's been acting for the screen, and the man she was acting opposite to meant no more to her than the negro hall porter. I love her, and she has always been very, very good to me, but, like yourself, Blossom, I think I'll feel like killing her if she plays Mr. Armitage false. 

I'll have a heart-to-heart talk with her to-night before we go to bed, and gie ye my considered opinion when I see you in the morning." "Thanks, Bunty," said Blossom with most profound and unusual solemnity. "God grant that she is not merely acting!" 185

CHAPTER XV 

B 

UNTY went to Honolulu next morning, accompanied by Alan Armitage, who was looking radiantly happy, and both were bound for the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel, Bunty to play the part directed to her by Inspector Harrigan, to give anyone interested the idea that Miss Gloria Drake was still in Honolulu, and Alan to collect letters which he had ordered to be forwarded to the Imperial Hawaiian and to keep an appointment with Captain Sam Simpson.

They talked quite gaily during the journey, but Bunty was not in high spirits, for in the course of her "heart-to-heart" talk with Gloria the latter had been evasive and elusive when asked any direct questions as to whether she was really in love with Alan, and had only laughed half derisively when Bunty enquired how soon she intended to get married. "I am afraid, Bunty dear, that the Southern

Glamour will sail without me, but it is lovely to be loved and to have conquered a man like Rodmell Armitage," she had answered, as she said good night.

So Bunty at heart was uneasy, but tried to conceal the fact and did so successfully as she chatted in her amusing mixture of Scots-English and colloquial 

American. They made the mistake of entering the

Imperial Hawaiian Hotel together, and Miss Sybil Laval, who happened to be seated at a table in the lounge commanding a view of the entrance, saw them and immediately jumped to conclusions. It happened, however, that at the moment she was being entertained to cocktails by a susceptible young Californian who 183 186

had been attracted by her platinum-blond and pinkand-white beauty, and before she could find an excuse to leave him Alan had collected his letters and left to keep his appointment with Captain Simpson, and Bunty had entered the lift and been shot up to the suite of "Miss Inez Fernandez." 

Daily Miss Laval had made enquiries about "dear

Miss Gloria," and had been answered gruffly, curtly, evasively or politely in turn by Joe Connolly, Spike 

Powell and Max Van Rosen. On two or three occasions she had intercepted Bunty, who had told her that her mistress was still unable to see anyone, and given her fictitious reports as to Gloria's condition in a manner which she hoped was convincing, but was not, for Bunty was so instinctively and naturally honest that she found pretence difficult.

Sybil had found herself completely baffled and had spent rather a miserable week or so, although she had enjoyed posing as a film star and spending money freely, for she knew that Otto Hagenberg and Seth Sneyd were waiting for news and both might be suspecting that they had been "double-crossed" by her. 

So she did some hard thinking after seeing Alan and Bunty together, and after having got rid of her Californian admirer, who was only one of a party of tourists spending one or two days in Honolulu, and didn't seem likely to run to anything more than cocktails. All along she had suspected that Gloria's advertised "collapse" was merely a blind, but she had found herself baffled at every turn. Now she began to see light. "Rodmell Armitage and that Gilmour girl arriving here together must mean, I guess that Gilmour knows where Armitage is now located, but the fact that she didn't seem to invite him up to Gloria Drake's suite, and that I haven't seen her about for three days at 187

least, seems to indicate that Gloria Drake may not be in the hotel at all," she soliloquised. "I guess I'd better go up and ask some questions and try to get the hang of things."

Three minutes later she was knocking at the outer door of Gloria's suite, which was opened by Max Van 

Rosen, whom she had done her best to fascinate. 

Behind him she saw Connolly and Powell seated at a table, looking very bored and evidently engaged in an attempt to cheat each other at cards, and she was quick to note that none of the men seemed to be on the alert, which one would have expected of guards of a famous film star threatened by kidnapping. "I am sorry, Miss Laval, but Miss Gloria is still unable to see anyone," Van Rosen lied glibly. "However, I am very pleased to see you, and it is good of you to call." "I do so want to have real news about dear Miss 

Gloria, Mr. Van Rosen," said Sybil with her most bewitching smile. "I wonder if I could have a word with Miss Gilmour, who, I suppose, is in constant attendance on her. Do please ask Miss Gilmour to speak to me. I saw her come into the hotel only a few minutes ago, but I happened to be engaged at the time so could not stop her to make enquiries.' 

"I'll see what I can do, Miss Laval," replied Van

Rosen, and departed to find Bunty, who was carefully packing a few frocks and other garments which Gloria specially desired into a small suit-case. "I don't like the woman, but I suppose I'd better see her," said Bunty. "God forgive me for the lies I'll have to tell her."

She allowed Sybil to cool her heels at the door of the outer room for some minutes, and then went out and greeted her with an affected politeness and cordiality which would have deceived nobody. 188

"Miss Gloria is awfu' grateful to you for calling so often and asks me to convey her thanks," she said. "She is gradually improving in health and hopes to be about again in a week or two, but at present the doctors have forbidden her to receive any visitors, and she must be kept perfectly quiet with nothing and nobody to disturb her." "Thank you so much, Miss Gilmour," Sybil responded with a smile that was as false as Bunty's affectation of friendliness. "Do please give my love and kindest regards to dear Miss Gloria, and tell her I am looking forward to seeing her completely restored to health in the near future." "I'll do that, Miss Laval," Bunty assured her, and

Sybil withdrew to do some more hard thinking at her seat in the lounge commanding a view of the main entrance of the hotel.

She was still feeling baffled and at a loss to know how to act when, some time later, she suddenly caught sight of Bunty leaving the hotel, accompanied by a coloured porter carrying a suit-case, and immediately she stiffened to attention and rose to her feet. She wanted to run, but forced herself to walk out of the lounge without any appearance of haste lest she should attract attention, and she was relieved to find that Miss Gilmour and her porter had not disappeared from view when she reached the pavement. Outwardly calm but inwardly excited, she followed them down to the terminus of the toy railway which runs round the island (Bunty with characteristic economy had decided to return to Haleiwa by rail instead of engaging a motor-car), and she saw the porter deposit Bunty and her suit-case in one of the coaches of the miniature train.

Luck was with her, inasmuch as there was quite a crowd of tourists booking the trip, so she was able to 189

enter the coach next to that into which the porter had ushered Bunty without fear of being recognised by the later. She had no eyes for the wonderful scenery as the train meandered round the coast, but at every stop she peered out eagerly. As it happened, quite a number of tourists alighted at Haleiwa and Sybil kept at the back of the little crowd as she saw Bunty, suit-case in hand, step from the next coach, but did not lose sight of her quarry, and followed her at a safe distance along the winding path and across the little Japanese bridge, to see her enter the grounds of the hotel, and at last disappear into the hotel itself. 

White-coated waiters, mostly Japanese, were flitting about the grounds, smilingly welcoming the trippers, and taking orders for refreshments, and Sybil, after pausing for thought, seated herself in an arbour from which she had a view of the hotel, and ordered a cocktail. "Can one have lunch here?" she enquired of the little Japanese who served the drink. "Certainly, madam, and we shall be most greatly honoured to serve you the most beautiful lunch that so beautiful a lady deserves," was the elaborately polite reply. "You will lunch, perhaps, in the garden or in the hotel, please?" "I'll think it over and decide later," replied Sybil, tipping him a dollar bill. "I wonder if there happen to be any friends of mine staying here? I know some people who'd be glad to meet me are making a tour of the island, and I seem to remember they said something about putting up here." "What name, please?" asked the little yellow man, and Sybil found herself momentarily at a loss, unable to decide whether to give the actual names or not. "Don't worry," she said hastily. "I'll stroll along 190

presently when I have decided about lunch, and have a look at the visitors' book. Thanks."

She did more hard thinking as she sipped her cocktail, and then, when the coast seemed to be clear, she wandered up to the hotel and languidly inspected the visitors' book. Her heart almost seemed to miss a beat as she saw the signatures "A. Rodmell Armitage" and "Horatio Flower," and a little further down the page that of "Janet Gilmour," immediately following that of "Mary Smith, San Francisco," and in the same handwriting. Sybil glanced up to find an alert clerk regarding her enquiringly. "Say, this is verry interesting and exciting," she exclaimed in her high-pitched voice. "I find there are friends of mine here at least, I hope they are still here. Tell me, are Miss Gilmour and Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower here?" "Yes, madam, and I understand they intend stopping for some time, as they have fallen in love with Haleiwa," answered the polite clerk, with an ingratiating smile. “Everyone falls in love with Haleiwa, and everyone who can do so stays as long as possible. 

May I have the pleasure of booking rooms for you?

We have one or two apartments vacant.' 99 "I'm fixed at present at the Imperial Hawaiian 

Hotel," Sybil explained glibly, "but I guess I'll give my friends here a surprise by fixing up here to-morrow or the next day. Gee! they will be surprised and pleased!" "I think Miss Smith and Miss Gilmour are in the hotel now, madam," said the obliging clerk. "If you wish, I'll send a servant to—" "Say, I won't bother them now, thanks," interrupted Sybil. "Don't mention to them that anyone was making enquiries about them. I am going back to 

Honolulu to arrange to leave there and come here 191

to-morrow if I can manage it. Meantime, you can book an apartment for me, but don't put my name on the book. I want to give my friends a real surprise. 

Say, do you often go to the movies?" "As often as I can, madam," replied the polite clerk. "Then perhaps you may remember seeing me in some picture, although I retired from Hollywood over a year ago," said Sybil. "My name is Sybil Laval, but, as I said before, I don't want you to mention my name until I get here. Understand?" "Yes, madam, perfectly, and you can rely upon me to be discreet," answered the clerk, and then did a bit of polite lying on his own account. "I remember you perfectly well on the films, Miss Laval, and always admired you, and it is a great honour to meet you in real life." "You've seen Gloria Drake on the films, I suppose?" queried Sybil. "Many times, madam." "Don't you think Miss Mary Smith, one of my friends from San Francisco, resembles her?” "Now you've mentioned the matter, madam, I think the resemblance is quite extraordinary," responded the clerk. “I have been wondering who it was that the beautiful Miss Smith reminded me of, and now I realise that she is the very image of Gloria Drake. 

You mean perhaps—" "I don't mean anything really," interposed Sybil again, "but to-morrow you may have two film stars patronising your establishment."

She asked a few questions, the replies to which satisfied her that "Miss Mary Smith" was Gloria, and took a hurried departure from the hotel, after again pledging the clerk to secrecy, to catch the first train back to Honolulu. She was in a state of inward excitement and exultation, but she had not quite decided 192

how to deal with the situation, or how to act, and it was late in the afternoon before she sent off a cable to Sneyd, who had cabled her a few days before that the Clarissa had put in at Hilo, the chief port of the island of Hawaii, and was awaiting instructions. 

To that cable she had replied cryptically, but knowing that Sneyd would understand: "Birds flown. Making enquiries. Wait." Now she cabled: "Found bird's nest. Difficult. Wait, but be ready to cruise." 

Inspector Harrigan saw copies of all three cables, did some hard thinking on his own account and sent a code message to the inspector in charge at Hilo, after having received a report from the detective he had appointed to report on Sybil's movements that she had paid a visit to Haleiwa that morning. Next forenoon he received another report to the effect that Miss Sybil Laval had left the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel and taken up quarters at the Haleiwa Hotel, with the result that the detectives posing as guests at Haleiwa received further instructions. Incidentally, Harrigan saw no particular reason to suspect that either Sybil Laval or Rodmell Armitage were in any way connected with the Dandy Hagen gang, but there seemed to him to be something suspiciously significant in the fact that Sybil and Alan had met late at night after the kidnapping attempt, and that Sybil should now have gone to Haleiwa. 

If Sybil's arrival at the Haleiwa Hotel had been arranged in advance by a film director it could not have been more admirably staged. Gloria, Alan, Blossom and Bunty had all been bathing together, and were seated round a table on the hotel veranda, sunning themselves and enjoying cocktails and cigarettes, when Sybil stepped from the car which had brought her from Honolulu. All four gasped at sight of her, 193

and Sybil at sight of them did quite an admirable little piece of acting. "Why-why-oh, am I dreaming?" she cried, stopping dead and putting up her hands to her head as she gazed wide-eyed at the quartet. "I can't believe my own eyes, but-but surely-surely you must be real?"

Still wide-eyed and with every appearance of complete bewilderment she came up on to the veranda and timorously held out her hand. "Tell me that you are real, and that I am not dreaming or suffering from delusions," she pleaded. "Oh, this is so wonderful! Dear Miss Gloria, I thought you were lying sick in the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel, and I have been feeling so worried on your account and making enquiries after you every day, and now I find you here looking lovely and well. And

I hardly even hoped to see Mr. Armitage again, and here he is with you. Oh, it seems like a wonderful dream, but-but I don't understand." "For my part, Miss Laval, I don't understand how and why you happen to be here," drawled Alan, who had risen to his feet with instinctive courtesy. "Oh, that's easily explained, Mr. Armitage," lied 

Sybil glibly. "I told you I came ashore from my yacht, for a rest and quiet time, and I found the Imperial Hawaiian so-so disturbing in a way and decided to find some quieter place. Yesterday I took a trip round the island and fell in love with Haleiwa at first sight, so decided to stay here for a week or so. But I never expected to find you and your friend and Miss Gloria and Miss Gilmour here. I feel quite bewildered. It all seems so wonderful—and yet so mysterious." "It was Cowper, I think, who wrote 'God moves in a mysterious way, His wonders to perform,' re- 999 194

marked Alan half ironically. "Won't you sit down, Miss Laval, and have a cocktail or something?"

Miss Laval sat down and chattered about herself, told them how lonely she had been at the Imperial Hawaiian, although there had been such crowds of smart people there, and how worried she had felt about dear Miss Gloria. "Say, it was real cute of you, Miss Gloria, to give everyone the idea that you were prostrated in the hotel. when all the time you were safe here with Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower, and I guess you've put any would-be kidnappers completely off the scent," she went on smilingly in her high-pitched voice, which Alan found himself comparing mentally to hail falling on a corrugated iron roof. "It sure was clever, but when I think of the sympathy I have been wasting I feel almost inclined to weep. Why, only yesterday morning naughty Miss Gilmour told me you were too ill to see anybody." She chattered on, apparently oblivoius to the fact that she received only indifferent or monosyllabic answers to her questions, and that her advent did not appear to have aroused any particular enthusiasm. At length she fluttered away into the hotel, after lamenting the fact that she had not brought her maid with 195

her, and that she would probably have to do her own unpacking. "Rodmell, why has that woman come here, and how did she know you were here?" demanded Gloria as soon as Sybil had left. Is it merely another coincidence that you were in Honolulu yesterday and that she comes up here to-day?"

It was not exactly jealousy that prompted the questions, for it has been said that unless there is love there cannot be jealousy, and Gloria was quite sure that she was not in love with Alan Armitage, although she had been at pains to win his love. It may have been some sort of unreasoning fear or suspicion that Alan had brought the other woman on the scene again with the object of showing her that her triumph was not complete. And she hated the very idea of Sybil

Laval as a rival, either active or potential. "My dear girl, what on earth is in your mind?" exclaimed Alan in astonishment. "Do you imagine that 

I went to Honolulu yesterday to invite her to come along and be a confounded nuisance to us all—as I fear she may be?" "Havers!" interjected Bunty in her abruptly direct way. "Ye werna in the Imperial Hawaiian more than five minutes yesterday. We'd better go and get dressed, Miss Gloria, unless you want to spend the whole day in your bathing costume.' 

"Bunty, I don't believe it is mere chance that has brought that woman here," said Gloria a little later, as she began to dress for lunch. "Maybe no'," responded Bunty. "But if ye're thinking that Mr. Armitage has onything to do wi' her coming here, I'll guess ye've got bats in the belfry." 

Gloria flashed an angry glance at her, but checked 196

the impulse to make an indignant retort and merely shrugged her shapely shoulders and pouted. "It may be pure coincidence, Bunty," she resumed, after a pause, as she vigorously brushed her glossy, silken, chestnut hair. "It may be perfectly true that 

Rodmell encountered her by chance for the first time that night on the waterfront, but, all the same, it is very strange. I don't understand how she discovered that we were here, unless, perhaps, she followed you or Rodmell yesterday, but, leaving that out, I don't believe her story about being bored by the Imperial Hawaiian, and I think that she must be in love with Rodmell Armitage and traced him here if the whole thing isn't a coincidence." "Yer payin' yerself a very poor compliment if you think that Alan Rodmell Armitage would forsake you for a painted doll," remarked Bunty. CHAPTER XVI 

8 

HE game of love is always dangerous, but both

Gloria and Alan, who had never experienced it before, found it entrancing and fascinating. Gloria had decided that she was not really in love, but yet she found herself fascinated and unresisting when Alan's arms encircled her and their lips met in kisses that disturbed her heart in spite of herself. 

Alan had not the slightest suspicion that Gloria was regarding his love as a game, and was merely playing a part. It had amused him that Gloria had displayed a certain amount of jealousy of Sybil Laval, who was being extremely "nice" to both of them, yet he had found it difficult to convince the girl he loved that it was not by any pre-arrangement that Sybil had put in an appearance at Haleiwa, and that he had never seen her prior to their meeting on the waterfront.

Alan was living in a sort of ecstatic dream. The fact that Gloria loved him had transformed for him the whole earth and made Haleiwa a sort of super paradise. He was still very keen on making the first cruise of the Southern Glamour his honeymoon trip.

Gloria told him that she had written to her father, and Alan, without conceit, had decided that Cyrus Oke Cole would give his consent to the marriage in spite of film contracts. "I think there will be a woman on our cruise after all, captain," he told Sam Simpson when he went through to Honolulu a week or so later to receive the glad news that the Southern Glamour was almost ready for sea. "If things work out as I expect, Mr. 198

Simpson, I shall be bringing my wife aboard, and the 'boudoir' will be wanted after all." "I guess the lady in the case will be Miss Gloria 

Drake?" said Simpson, extending a horny hand and giving Alan a grip that almost made him wince. "Congratulations, Mr. Armitage, she's the most glamorous and beautiful creature I have ever seen in my life, but I've only seen her on the movies and I'll look forward to seeing her in real life.” "Thanks, captain," said Alan happily. "She is certainly the most beautiful and adorable woman in the world, and I am the luckiest man." 

He went back to Haleiwa glad and proud at heart to announce to Gloria that the Southern Glamour would be ready for sea next day but one, and to beg her once again to marry him in Honolulu and make the cruise a honeymoon trip. "Dear, darling heart, I love you and want you so," he whispered as he strained Gloria close to his breast and kissed her again and again. "Say that you will be mine and come with me on the yacht. What does your father's opinion matter? What do contracts and all the rest of it matter? What does anything matter except love? The Southern Glamour will be ready for sea the day after to-morrow and you are coming with me as my wife."

Gloria disengaged herself from his arms and forced herself to laugh. "I think-I think perhaps you'd better take Miss

Sybil Laval on the cruise instead of me," she said, trying to speak steadily, although her heart was throbbing so violently that it seemed to be throbbing in her throat. "I've taught you your lesson, Rodmell Armitage. You boasted that you were not interested in women and I meant no more to you than any decrepit charwoman, yet I've made you love me. I boasted for 199

my part that I'd vamp you, and I've done it. I'm not going to marry you, and I don't love you. I've only been playing a part and amusing myself. The Southern 

Glamour will sail without me."

The effect of her words on Alan almost frightened her, although she had nerved herself for the occasion. 

He staggered slightly as if he had been dealt a blow, and his lean, bronzed face went grey, while his greyblue eyes dilated. And then he laughed, laughed—but it was a laugh with pain and no mirth in it, such a laugh as a man being tortured in the Star Chamber or by the Spanish Inquisition might have emitted in defiance of his torturers. "Rodmell I-I didn't want to hurt you too hard," 

Gloria gasped, "but-but-" "My dear girl, I quite understand, and I have been taugh my lesson," responded Alan as she paused at a loss for words. He had recovered himself with an effort and he was suddenly the calm, collected, somewhat supercilious English gentleman she had first met. "I was foolish enough to think that you were sincere, and for the first time in my life love was something more than a joke, something that meant to me more than anything else in heaven or earth. It may interest you to know that in the course of amusing yourself you have broken my heart. Your vamping has been most successful and I congratulate you on your success. 

But I think I should tell you, my dear Gloria, that no one up to now has succeeded in double-crossing me without my having been revenged. Still, I suppose the joke's on me, and I don't really bear you any ill-will.

Even if the Southern Glamour has to sail without you, perhaps you will do me the honour of having dinner aboard with me when I have a sort of house-warming, as one might say, to celebrate the fact that I am technically her owner, and before I try to forget the 200

amusement you must have enjoyed in vamping me. 

Good evening. Perhaps I'd better go and find Miss 

Sybil Laval."

He disappeared into the darkness, and the little beach of silvery sand on which he and Gloria had found tiny pink cowrie shells was his Gethsemane. As he came back to the hotel at last he literally ran into Sybil Laval who, by some means, had contrived to fasten herself like a clam on to Blossom that evening. "Sorry!" he apologised without recognising the couple in the darkness and in his distress of heart and mind, but Sybil caught his arm. "Why, it's Mr. Armitage, isn't it?" she exclaimed. "How lucky! I have just been telling Mr. Flower that 

I've sent a cable ordering my yacht to be brought back to Honolulu, and I'd love to have you and Miss Gloria and Mr. Flower come and dine with me on board the 

Clarissa before I leave. You have all been so kind to poor little lonely me while I've been here in Haleiwa and I'd like you to give me a chance to show my gratitude."

Sybil flattered herself that she had played her cards well and ingratiated herself with Gloria and Alan, as well as Blossom, although at times she had been compelled to wonder if the friendship she thought she had won was not merely formal politeness. However, she did think that all of them esteemed her sufficiently to accept an invitation to dine with her aboard the

Clarissa and fall into the trap she and Otto Hagenberg, in conjunction with Spider Sneyd, had designed. "Thank you, Miss Laval, but I am afraid I shall be too busy arranging about getting my own yacht ready to accept any invitations,” Alan answered rather curtly. "You must excuse me. I am not feeling very well."

He hurried into the hotel, and Blossom seized the opportunity to excuse himself, explaining to Sybil that 201

his "boss" would need him if he was feeling unwell.

Blossom had caught a glimpse of Alan's haggard face and knew that something untoward had happened, and the man he loved and served so faithfully was sorely hurt. Yet when he went up to the rooms that he and 

Alan shared he appeared to devote his attention chiefly to the sideboard, on which was a large decanter of whisky. "Drink this, Alan, and then tell me what's happened," he ordered, turning from the sideboard and holding out to Alan a large mixture of whisky and soda. "Don't argue, man, drink it. I've always found whisky and soda a very pleasant help in time of trouble." "Thanks, Blossom," said Alan, accepting the drink with a wry smile. "Your prescription is whisky for sorrow, whisky for joy, whisky to celebrate a loss or a win, a birth, a wedding or a death." "Yes, or merely to celebrate the fact that I am still alive," replied Blossom, seating himself. "Salue, sahib!" "Salue!" Alan responded absently, then drank deep and set his glass aside. "Old man, we have more in common now than ever," he resumed, after a long pause during which Blossom sat silent and motionless, watching him intently. "Do you think, Blossom, that the Almighty created women to make Paradise a Hell and to break men's hearts?" "My God, Alan! You don't mean to say that Gloria has-has-_____"} 

Blossom was on his feet, his blue eyes goggling through his big spectacles, the colour fading out of his round face, his glass trembling in his hand so violently that he spilled some of its contents on the floor. "Yes." Alan nodded without meeting his eyes as he groped for a cigarette. "You may remember, Blossom, 202

that I thought it amusing at first to be vamped. Now, old man, I don't find it in the least amusing to discover that Miss Gloria Drake has merely been amusing herself." "I know, old man, I know." Blossom began to walk up and down the room, his face twitching and with tears in his pale blue eyes. "I know the cruel hurt of it too well even to attempt to spout platitudes about time healing the wound or advising you to try to put her out of your mind." "I shall never be able to put her out of my mind,” said Alan in a toneless voice, throwing away his cigarette and rising to stare unseeingly out of the open window into the starlit darkness. "The hurt rankles worse than the stab of a poisoned dagger.' 99 Blossom scarcely heard him. He had taken off his big glasses and was agitatedly polishing them, at the same time trying to blink away the tears that were blinding his eyes and to master his emotion. He well knew that Alan, despite his habitual sang-froid, was highly sensitive, knew what a devastating blow

Gloria's perfidy must have dealt him and how sorely he must be wounded. "Oh, God! How could she be so cruel?" he burst out again hoarsely. "I did not think it was in her nature. I knew of her avowed intention to vamp you, but I would have staked my life that she was genuinely and whole-heartedly in love with you. Don't tell me about it unless-unless you feel that it would ease you to talk, although you'd prefer to suffer in silence. . . 

Curse it, I hardly know what I'm saying, but what I means is 'Give sorrow words, the grief that does not speak stifles the o'erfraught heart and bids it break.'" "There isn't much to tell, Blossom," said Alan in a tired voice, turning from the window, and Blossom saw dimly that he seemed to have aged, and looked 203

as if the fire of youth had died within him. “One moment she was in my arms, her heart throbbing against my breast, the next she was telling me mockingly that she'd merely been amusing herself and had no intentions of marrying me. She merely won my love, took my heart and trod it underfoot to gratify her own vanity and avenge herself for my impetuous remark weeks ago that she meant no more to me than any charwoman. . . . Give me another drink."

He flung himself down in a chair with a sigh of utter weariness, and Blossom hastened to refill his glass. "I had prided myself that she loved me well enough to agree to make the first cruise of the Southern 

Glamour our honeymoon trip," resumed Alan with a mirthless laugh after another silence. "Hell! I'd rather she'd knifed me."

Scarcely knowing what he was doing, he lit another cigarette only to rise and throw it out of the open window. Then he began to pace restlessly up and down the length of the room. Blossom, feeling helpless, watched him in sympathetic silence, agitatedly sipping his whisky, and he dropped his glass in his confusion when Alan suddenly stopped in front of him and gazed at him with a gleam that seemed almost maniacal in his grey-blue eyes. "Blossom, I won't take it lying down," Alan almost hissed at him. "She shall-she will come for a honeymoon trip on the Southern Glamour. Understand? 

You can think what you damned well like about me, but my mind is made up." "You mean-or-you think it possible she may relent?" stammered Blossom, vaguely realising that he was trembling again. "I shall not relent," answered Alan cryptically, with an eerie laugh. "I am going out-out into the paradise that now seems to me a hell." 204

Blossom clutched at his arm as he turned towards the door and held fast to him. "Alan! Alan, dear lad, don't do anything desperate!" he almost sobbed, and to his relief the maniacal gleam faded from Alan's eyes, and the tense expression from his haggard face. "Dear old Blossom, I assure you I am not going to do anything desperate to-night," he said, almost in his normal tone and manner. "But I must get out into the open to try to think, or try to stop thinking, and try to find peace for my damned soul. Let go my arm, Blossom. I'm going to make a fight of it.' 99 "Alan, you will remember, when you encouraged me to make a fight of it while I was depressed when you stopped the dope, you told me suicide was the coward's way out," quavered Blossom. "Promise me, Alan, before I let you go, that you will remember your own assertion and will not take the coward's way out.' 99 Tears were streaming down his pink cheeks again, and Alan, in spite of his own mental turmoil, was touched by the sight of his companion's distress. "Blossom, old man, I am not going to die, but to live, even if by living I die a thousand deaths," he said quite gently, laying both hands on Blossom's shoulders as Blossom released his clutch on his elbow. "Don't worry about me. Gloria Drake thinks she has taught me a lesson, but it may be that she may yet have something to learn. I am going out to talk to the stars about it. Go to bed, Blossom, and don't worry."

Blossom did not go to bed. He sat down, telling himself that he was becoming a sentimental old fool, and even as he rose absent-mindedly to help himself to whisky the years-old hurt was back in his heart, and he found himself cursing all women as he thought of what Alan must be suffering. Yet, when he had controlled his emotion, it was a woman he sought, or, 205

rather, invited, to help him. He rang the bell after doing some rapid thinking. "Please discover if Miss Janet Gilmour is still awake, and, if so, ask her if she will be good enough to come down to the lounge, as Mr. Horatio Flower wishes to discuss a matter of pressing importance," he instructed the Japanese servant who answered his ring. "Do you understand that quite clearly?" "Yees, sir," the Japanese replied with a deferential bow, and repeated the message word for word. 

Blossom went down to the lounge, which was now completely deserted, for it was past midnight, and wandered about restlessly, waiting and hoping that Bunty had not retired for the night and would put in an appearance in response to his message. His hopes were realised, for within five minutes or so Bunty, wearing a gaily-coloured dressing-gown over equally gaily-coloured pyjamas, came scampering down the stairs in slippered feet. But her expression was not in the least gay. She looked white and troubled, and as she clasped the hand which Blossom instinctively held out to her she began to cry. "I wanted to come to you, Blossom, as soon as I heard what's happened, but I thought Mr. Armitage might be wi' ye," she exclaimed brokenly. "Oh, Blossom, it's terrible, and my heart is wae for Rodmell

Armitage. I've served and loved Miss Gloria for seven years nearly, and I never thought she could be sae cruel and heartless. It seems unnatural, and I don't understand it ava. I'm feeling as if my ain heart was broken, Blossom." ""Don't cry, little woman," said Blossom, gently patting Bunty on the shoulder. "My heart is sore, too, at the thought of the misery and pain my dear friend and master must be enduring. You and I have got to 206

try and do something about this matter, Bunty dear. 

So Gloria told you what she had done?"

Bunty nodded, dropped into a chair and wiped her eyes. "Aye, she told me, but I kent something had happened before she telt me," she answered. "She was greetin' and laughin' baith at the same time, and I'm no' sure whether she's proud o' hersel' or black burning ashamed. Gee! Blossom, if I was in her shoes I guess I'd feel like some sort of muckworm. I never thought Gloria was cruel, but to break a heart in jest is the cruellest kind of cruelty I can think of." "You're right, Bunty. To break a heart in jest is the cruellest sort of cruelty," agreed Blossom solemnly, still absently patting Bunty's shoulder. "I don't know what to make of it all. Alan has taken it very badly, and just for a few minutes I feared he had lost his reason. Do you think it will be possible for us to do anything?" "If you were to leave off patting my shoulder as if 

I were a pet dog and give me a cigarette, maybe I might be able to think," snapped Bunty, feeling rather annoyed with herself for having had an inclination to weep on Blossom's shoulder. 

Blossom, looking and feeling flustered and foolish, supplied the cigarette and a light, and sat down facing Bunty and began to answer as best he could her bombardment of questions. "Now, I wonder what's in Mr. Alan Rodmell's mind?" Bunty soliloquised aloud. "Have you any notion, Blossom, of what he intends to do?" "Not the faintest, Bunty," answered Blossom. “He talked and behaved rather wildly, but promised not to do anything desperate. Men are apt to behave and talk rather wildly in such circumstances. I-I remember that I, myself, in somewhat similar circumstances 207

temporarily lost my reasoning powers. But I don't think Alan Armitage will be quite such a fool as I was, or try to make such a mess of his life as I was doing when he found and rescued me from myself." 99 "Aye, ye told me, Blossom, and you'll mebbe mind

I told you that you should have taken yon fine lady by the scruff of the neck and shook the life out o' her,' remarked Bunty slowly and reflectively, after a pause. "I am wondering if Mr. Alan is feeling inclined tae tak' Gloria by the scruff of the neck and deal wi' her as she deserves." "My dear Bunty," gasped Blossom, looking quite horrified. "Alan would never think of doing such a thing. He is a sportsman and a gentleman." "I am not doubting that, Blossom, but I'll tell ye that although I think a lot o' Miss Gloria, I'll not be sorry if Alan Rodmell gets his ain back, as the saying goes.' "Thanks, Miss Gilmour, that's very interesting hearing," drawled a cool voice behind her, and both she and Blossom started violently and looked up to find Alan Rodmell Armitage regarding them with a quizzing expression.

They had both been so engrossed in their discussion that they had not noticed the passage of time, and had not seen Alan, who had purged his soul to some extent by taking a long walk in the starlit darkness, enter quietly by the veranda door. "My dear Alan," stammered Blossom, crimson with confusion, "I-er-I hope you won't think this any sort of disloyalty on my part, or on the part of Bunty, I—er—I—er—thought I should try to-er-discover from Miss Gilmour what the real feelings of Miss 

Gloria are." "I think, my dear Blossom, that I am much more interested in the opinions I just overheard expressed 208

by Bunty than I am in the feelings of Miss Gloria," drawled Alan. "I wish I could guess what's in your mind, Mr.

Armitage," said Bunty, recovering herself and regarding him searchingly.

She saw that he was deathly pale beneath the tan and that he looked weary and like a man whose endurance had been taxed to the uttermost. Yet she knew by the gleam in his eyes that he was not a beaten and a broken man, and that his unconquerable soul was still strong within him. "What's in my mind at the moment is that as it is somewhere about three o'clock in the morning it might not be a bad idea to go to bed," drawled Alan, still smiling. "I feel sure dear Blossom will offer me a nightcap before I retire. . . . By the way, Blossom, do you think I should stand any chance of success as an actor?" 

Blossom met his quizzing gaze uncomfortably.

He was uneasy in mind and troubled at heart, for he was conscious, instinctively, that some subtle change which he found difficult to define had taken place in Alan in the space of a few hours. "I rather think you are doing quite an excellent piece of acting now, Alan," he replied, after a few moments of hesitation, then turned to Bunty, who was as much perplexed as he. "Shall I see you to your room, Bunty?" "Thanks, I ken the road fine, Blossom," responded 

Bunty, forcing a smile, "and my reputation might suffer if I was seen being seen to my room by a man at three o'clock in the morning. Guid nicht tae ye both."

She hurried away and Blossom and Alan stood regarding each other in silence for a full minute. "Well, Alan?" asked Blossom at length, "have you anything to tell me?" 209

"Yes, I think I begin to understand, Blossom, why you enjoy whisky," drawled Alan, "and I think it would be quite a good idea to sample another before we go to bed. That's all." 210

CHAPTER XVII 

AN N hour hour later Alan was sitting up in bed smoking one cigarette after another without even realising that he was smoking, and thinking, thinking, thinking, his head throbbing and his heart feeling as if it would burst. It seemed to him that the very air was supercharged with electricity, and even in his dazed and bemused state of mind he was probably right, for outside his open window the stars were clouded over, and it seemed as if a dank, breathless silence had enveloped the world. 

Then with a startling suddenness that only Hawaii and the Pacific know, the storm broke. There came a flash of blinding, dazzling, blue-white light that was lightning, followed by a crash that seemed to shake the very earth to its foundations, a crash of thunder which sounded as if all the hounds of hell and all the guns of the Great War had suddenly given voice at one and the same time. And then came the rain, such rain as only the tropics know, rain in a sheet that was a deluge and which washed some of the shrubs out of the garden of the hotel and made the little murmuring stream within a few minutes into a roaring torrent, while the blinding lightning continued to flash almost continuously and the thunder intermittently to roar. 

Alan jumped out of bed, conscious of a strained excitement, agitation and exultation. He went to the open window of his bedroom and shouted aloud as the thunder crashed, but covered his eyes with his hands as the lightning flashed. It seemed somehow to him, in his abnormal state of mind, that it was right 2II

that the elements should devastate the earth as Gloria had devastated his heart, and he continued to shout with each crash of thunder in a sort of triumphant exultation.

The storm ceased as suddenly as it had begun. The rain stopped like a running water tap suddenly cut off from the main, the skies cleared and the stars came out again looking like great incandescent electric globes.

Alan, realising that he had been behaving in a daft sort of fashion, drew a long breath, ran his fingers through his hair and sat down on the edge of his bed. 

He could hear the sound of scared voices and of people moving about in the corridors of the hotel, and then he heard another sound which brought him to his feet -the sound of faint scratching at his bedroom door and a faint whimpering.

Almost he bounded to the door and flung it open, and what looked like a white slug with a faintly wagging tail tottered into the room (of course, slugs aren't white and they don't totter and they haven't got tails) and collapsed almost at his feet. The "white slug" was, of course, Rodmell Homestall King, otherwise Imp, and Imp had had his first experience of a tropical storm and did not like it one little bit. He, like some of the humans in the hotel, was scared, puzzled and bewildered.

He had been in close attendance on his beloved master and the female woman who seemed to love him, but he had fallen asleep while they were talking and when he suddenly woke, his doggy intelligence telling him that something was wrong, it was to find that both had disappeared. Whereupon Imp gave an imitation of something approaching a vacuum cleaner, picked up the scent of his beloved master and dashed out into the gardens, only to lose the trail again and 212

again, find himself eventually a little forlorn Sealyham on a deserted beach, where he had sat down and cried and complained to high heaven without any result. 

And then when he was trying to make his way back to the hotel, high heaven had started to let off terribly big fireworks (Imp objected to fireworks), and to make terrible, frightening loud noises, and then to pour water all over him (Imp objected to having water poured all over him), and even when he crouched down under a bush the bush seemed to be trying to fall on him and water seemed to be coming up from the ground to try to wash his short stumpy legs away from under him. So it was a very scared, drenched and forlorn little dog, who, after shaking himself time and again on the veranda, had managed to effect an entrance to the hotel and to crawl up the stairs to his master's bedroom. "Why, Imp, old son, where have you been?" cried

Alan in concern, picking the little dog up from the floor only to set him down again almost at once. "Half a minute, Imp, I'll get a towel."

Imp didn't like towels very much, for they reminded him of baths and Imp didn't like baths very much; but he submitted to being towelled vigorously by his beloved master, and managed to muster enough energy to shake himself vigorously when Alan finished the towelling. "Been out in the storm, old chap?" asked Alan, throwing aside the towel and gathering Rodmell 

Homestall King up into his arms. "Dash it all, Imp, you ought to bite me. I'd actually forgotten all about you, and never even remembered to enquire where you were. You must forgive me, Imp. You've only been out in one storm to-night, but I've been through two. 

I wonder if you understand." 

Rodmell Homestall King, either to express his for- 213

giveness or to explain that he quite understood, licked Alan's ear vigorously, and then wriggled out of his master's arms, flopped down on the bed and curled himself into a sort of ragged ball, explaining with a doggy grunt that he was too tired to have any arguments. "Perhaps you are right, Imp, and I should try to follow your example," murmured Alan, giving Imp an affectionate pat before switching off the light. "You've helped me to stop thinking about things I don't want to think about and I'll try to get some sleep."

He took Imp with him when he left a few hours later for Honolulu to have another conference with Captain Sam Simpson about the fitting out of the Southern Glamour and certain other matters in connection with the sailing of the yacht which had not hitherto been mentioned. Incidentally, it had pleased him, at the outset, that Imp appeared to have taken an immediate fancy to Gloria, but now he found himself hating the thought of seeing his dog snuggled close to her breast with her arms around him. "I'm puzzled, Mr. Armitage," said Sam Simpson, shaking his grizzled head after a long and confidential talk with Alan, "but anything you say goes, and I'll obey your orders without asking questions. I'm master of the vessel, but you're the owner, and I'm not disputing your right to tell me when to sail or where to sail. I'm not worrying, either, about what passengers, guests or whatever you like to call them, you bring or invite aboard. Old Pa Leddra brought some queer birds aboard, as I've told you, and I asked no questions, but just carried on with my job of running the Southern Glamour and bringing her safely back to port. I quite understand your orders and I'll carry them out, but I'll confess, Mr. Armitage, that I'm puzzled." 214

"You're not the only one who's going to be puzzled during the next few days, Captain," remarked Alan with a queer laugh. "I'm going back to Haleiwa to try to puzzle other people. . . . Come on, Imp."

He did puzzle everyone immediately concerned when he got back to Haleiwa that afternoon. To start with, he seemed to be in high spirits and to have forgotten all about the events of the previous night, a fact that piqued Gloria, who had slept no better than he had, bewildered Blossom and Bunty, and completely puzzled Miss Sybil Laval.

Sybil, at the first opportunity, had approached Blossom and enquired with an appearance of anxiety and distress, which was not altogether assumed (for she realised that her plans seemed likely to go awry), why Mr. Armitage had snubbed her and so curtly refused her invitation to dine with Gloria aboard the 

Clarissa. And Blossom, in an unguarded moment, had told her that Gloria had heartlessly jilted Alan. 

Miss Laval, therefore, in the absence of Armitage, had been devoting herself to Gloria, subtly suggesting that the safest thing for Gloria to do was to board the Clarissa as soon as it arrived at Honolulu, and, at the same time, she had made up her mind that she would sympathise with Alan, win his heart on the rebound, as it were, and at least induce him to accept her invitation to board the Clarissa. But she found herself baffled again, for Alan seemed to be impervious to all the arts of fascination she tried to exercise on him, and he seemed to be inwardly amused about something, a fact which secretly scared and enraged her, for she wondered if he suspected her motives. "Thanks very much, Miss Laval," said Alan, when

Sybil repeated her pressing invitation, "but I am so interested in my own yacht, and so excited at the prospect of taking command of her, that you must 215

excuse me for declining your kind invitation in the circumstances. Some other time, perhaps. I expect to be cruising about in the South Seas for months, and possibly we may encounter one another again." "I've heard what happened-I mean between you and Miss Gloria—and I am really and truly frightfully sorry for you, dear Mr. Armitage, and sympathise with you with all my heart," said Sybil, concealing her chagrin. "Thanks very much, Miss Laval," drawled Alan again; and again it seemed to Sybil that inwardly he was laughing at her. "I assure you, my dear Miss

Laval, that your sympathy is wasted. Now, if you will excuse me, I want to have a talk with Miss Gloria-a private and confidential talk."

The conversation had taken place in the garden within sight, but out of hearing, of Gloria, who was seated on the veranda of the hotel with Rodmell Homestall King comfortably curled up in her lap, and she was conscious of a certain sense of relief as she recognised, with feminine intuition, that Sybil Laval was annoyed. Yet she was puzzled and annoyed by the fact that Alan was smiling as he came towards her, and settled himself languidly in a long cane chair by her side. "Lovely afternoon after the storm, Gloria," drawled Alan, producing a cigarette-case. "Have a cigarette?... No? Well, perhaps you're right. I don't know that I altogether approve of women smoking.

Do you know that in the old days in novels and on the stage it was only adventuresses and villainesses who smoked, and I am told that the gallery, which in England, as you probably know, is called 'the gods,' hissed like anything when the beautiful villainess puffed her cigarette and told the hero that she had him completely in her power." 216

"Very interesting," drawled Gloria, imitating to some extent his voice and manner. "Perhaps the fact that I have refused your offer of a cigarette proves that I am not an adventuress or a villainess? You seem to be amused about something." "I am." Alan sat up in his chair and regarded her keenly with a quizzical smile on his lean, bronzed face. "I have been laughing at myself all morning. Do you know, Gloria, that I actually took last night's scene, affair, or whatever you like to call it, between us, seriously! I was fool enough to imagine that you were in earnest, and I experienced two storms-one the thunderstorm, and the other an emotional storm, but now I realise that you were only jesting, and punishing me in a jesting fashion without really intending to hurt me for saying something weeks ago in a wild moment about your meaning no more to me than any charwoman. I see that Imp has forgiven you, so I suppose

I had better do the same. I notice that Miss Laval is watching us and so is one of the Japanese waiters, so we can't very well kiss and make it up here at the moment, but perhaps we might shake hands without attracting too much attention or occasioning comment."

Still with the same queer smile on his face he extended a lean, brown, sinewy hand, but Gloria ignored it. She was gazing at him with a puzzled and halfangry expression in her wonderful eyes. "Are you quite crazy, Rodmell Armitage?" she asked, her husky voice unusually curt. "If you think

I was merely jesting last night, you are very much mistaken. Let me assure you again that you mean no more to me than any decrepit charman, although, of course, I am still grateful to you for saving my father and frustrating the kidnapping gang. But as for 217

kissing and making it up, that is out of the question, and seems to me like a sort of bad joke."

She half expected and half feared an outburst from Alan, and her perplexity and inward annoyance increased as he continued to regard her with his queer smile, without even wincing or displaying the slightest sign of distress. "So you broke my heart in jest, Gloria?" drawled

Alan at last, after a prolonged silence that made Gloria uneasy and uncomfortable, and without knowing that he was repeating the words of Bunty and 

Blossom. "That's another proof, I suppose, that women have a sense of humour, although certain philosophers have advanced arguments to the contrary. 

As you say in America, the joke's on me. Are you quite sure you won't have a cigarette? I'm sure you've earned it!" "Meaning that you regard me as a villainess and an adventuress ?" exclaimed Gloria, her lovely face flushing deeply. "Rodmell, you brought it on yourself. 

You almost boasted of your indifference to women and the fact that no girl had ever stirred your heart, and 

I boasted for my part that I'd vamp you. I guess we're all square now. 

"Not quite," responded Alan, doing his best to continue to smile, but his smile had become a little crooked and unreal. "I guess the next move is up to me. You made me believe, Gloria mia, that you loved me, and even now I believe you love me, and are being false to your own heart. In spite of what has happened,

Gloria, I love and want you with all the intensity of my being. Let's treat what happened last night as a joke which we will both try to forget, and say that you will marry me to-morrow or as soon as I can fix things up. I want to make you my wife and am de- 218

termined that you shall sail with me as my wife on the 

Southern Glamour. Gloria, I am in earnest."

Gloria rose to her feet and trilled out a laugh that rang false. "You flatter yourself, and apparently you have not yet learnt your lesson," she said abruptly. “As you want to treat the matter as a joke, let me tell you that the very idea of marrying you makes me feel inclined to laugh." "Well, that seems promising, anyhow," commented

Alan. "We have at least something in common, for I want to continue smiling myself. You will remember this is the third time I have asked you to marry me.

However, as you want to treat the whole affair as a joke, and to laugh at the idea of marriage, I shall do my best to continue smiling and even to join in your laughter. You have hurt me sorely, but have taught me my lesson, and I can only hope that I shall find compensation and peace of mind again when the Southern 

Glamour sets out on her cruise.' 99 "I hope so too, Rodmell," said Gloria, a trifle unsteadily, "and—and I hope you won't continue to hate me and bear me ill-will." "As you hope so too, Gloria, everything should be quite all right," responded Alan, clasping the hand that she instinctively held out to him. "As for hating you, or bearing you ill-will, haven't I just assured you that I still love you and want you? So let's try to smile and pretend we are happy." "I don't believe you really care, and I have an idea that you are still making fun of me," exclaimed Gloria, snatching her hand away. "I shall yet prove-and soon-how much I care, my dear Gloria," Alan said abruptly, his smile vanishing. "Meantime, as you say I mean no more to you than any decrepit charman, what does it matter 219

whether I care or not? I am afraid, my dear, that your sense of proportion has got out of gear again. But what does that matter? What does anything matterexcept love? Sit down, Gloria, and let's talk and behave like friends instead of lovers." "Rodmell Armitage, I think you are the most exasperating man in the world," cried Gloria, but she sat down again, nevertheless. "Give me a cigarette, I don't care whether you think I am a villainess or not."

Alan's smile returned as he proffered his case and lit her cigarette before beckoning to the watchful waiter and ordering drinks without enquiring if Gloria wanted one. "You paid me the compliment, Gloria, a few days ago, of saying that you felt safe here in Haleiwa while 

I was with you," he said a few minutes later. "What do you intend to do now that-er-circumstances have changed and that Blossom and I shall be leaving within the next three days?" "I think I shall 'phone Max Van Rosen and Inspector Harrigan to-morrow, and arrange to go back to the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel before you leave," answered Gloria after a few moments of thoughtful silence. "Miss Laval has invited me to go cruising on her yacht for a week or so, but I have declined the invitation. I don't like her, although she professes to like and admire me, but that needn't prevent you from lunching or dining with her when her yacht returns to Honolulu."

Alan was in a state of inward turmoil, yet outwardly he seemed perfectly calm, and he continued to smile in the fashion that Gloria had found so exasperating. "As a matter of fact, I am not sure that I am very much in love with Miss Sybil Laval myself, and it might interest you to know that only a little while ago 220

I politely declined her invitation to be a guest aboard the Clarissa," he answered. "But I am going to have a little house-warming party—or whatever the correct term is for a dinner to celebrate the recommissioning of the Southern Glamour-and I feel sure that you and Bunty will not decline to be my guests on the evening before we sail." "Are you inviting Sybil Laval?" queried Gloria. "Nobody except you and Bunty," was the answer. "Say you will come, Gloria." "Why, of course, I'll be pleased to come, Rodmell, if only to show I bear you no ill-will," answered Gloria readily. "I expect Inspector Harrigan will be able to arrange to have me guarded on the way to and from your yacht." "That will hardly be necessary," said Alan. "Blossom and I will act as your escort. My idea is to call for you in the afternoon or early in the evening, so as to have time to show you over the vessel before dinner and see if you approve of the furnishings and decoration. When you have seen the Southern Glamour 

I think you may change your mind about not wanting to sail with me.' 99 "If you don't mind we won't start on that subject again," responded Gloria with a frown. "I'll be glad to see the yacht and to dine with you, and I hope you will be happy afterwards." "I am sure I shall," exclaimed Alan with a queer laugh. "You must excuse me now; I have to confer with Blossom about some matters of business. 

Come along, Imp." 

Imp opened one eye, cocked his head on one side for a moment and curled himself up again, having evidently decided he would prefer to remain where he was. 221

"The darling doesn't want to go with you," laughed 

Gloria. "Imp seems to have fallen in love with me." 999 "Humph! It seems as if we shall have to reverse the old proverb and make it 'Love my dog, love me,' said Alan coldly. "I am afraid you would find it difficult to break Imp's heart, even in jest. . . . Rodmel Homestall King at once!" 

At the sound of the name and the stern voice Imp instantly leapt from Gloria's arms and dashed after his beloved master, who strode away without another word.

Gloria saw him but seldom during the next two days, for he had much business to transact in Honolulu in conjunction with Captain Simpson. The Southern 

Glamour had been refloated and the painters were putting the finishing touches on her, while supplies of every description were being loaded. Gloria herself was kept fully occupied and had little time in which to brood had she been so inclined. If the truth be told, it was annoyance rather than remorse that she felt when she met Alan in the evenings, for he still maintained his attitude of aloof amusement and seemed to be elated about something. His talk was all of the 

Southern Glamour and of the places he intended to visit on his long, leisurely cruise. 

Van Rosen, vastly relieved to find that Gloria was not going to marry Alan, as he had feared, spent hours at Haleiwa dealing with neglected correspondence and business affairs, and Inspector Harrigan personally visited Gloria twice to assure her that she need have no fears for her safety. Bunty, unusually silent and preoccupied, was busy packing and repacking, daily (and sometimes twice daily) visiting the Imperial 

Hawaiian Hotel, where, unknown to anyone immediately concerned, she had earnest conferences with Alan. 

Blossom found himself completely bemused by the 222

turn of events, and utterly at a loss to understand Alan's attitude or even to guess what was in his mind. 

He knew how sorely Alan had been wounded and the apparent high spirits of his comrade-employer puzzled him completely. He began to wonder uneasily if Alan -as he had done in somewhat similar circumstanceshad taken to drugs in an effort to forget, but when he ventured to mention his fears to Bunty she only laughed and looked at him queerly. "Havers! I thocht ye knew him better than that, Blossom," she remarked dryly. "It's no' drugs, but love and the prospect o' getting his ain way that's makin' him behave so oddly and go about wi' a smile although the heart o' him is sairly sore. He's got a way wi' him, as I dinna need tae tell ye, but I'm tellin' ye, ye big guy, that I guess he'll get his way with Miss 

Gloria and get away with her."

Blossom shook his pink head and took off his big glasses and polished them, which was always a sign that he was worried. "I think, my dear Bunty, that I know Alan Rodmell 

Armitage better than anyone living or dead, but I'm puzzled and worried about him," he said after a pause. "I don't know how you imagine that he's going to have his way with Miss Gloria after all. She seems utterly indifferent to him, although they are quite friendly in their manner towards each other." "Imphmn! Yin o' these days, Blossom, you'll mebbe or mebbe no' begin to understand something about women," commented Bunty. "What was it that the painted doll was talkin' to ye about so earnestly before she left this afternoon to join her yacht at Honolulu ?" "You mean Miss Laval?
