Hawaiian Hotel to-morrow, when I understand the 

Southern Glamour will be ready to sail." "Imphmn! And I can tell ye, Blossom, that Miss

Gloria and myself and Mr. Armitage will not be accepting Miss Laval's invitation for to lunch or dine with her on board the Clarissa, but Miss Gloria and me'll be aboard the Southern Glamour to-morrow, and maybe there will be a reconciliation of a kind that will surprise you." "I am delighted to hear that, Bunty," cried Blossom, replacing his glasses on his nose and beginning to beam. "You think-you think- 99 "There's whiles I think, Blossom, that ye're no' very gleg at the uptak'; but we'll leave it at that," interposed Bunty. 224

CHAPTER XVIII 

83 

APTAIN SAMUEL SIMPSON, wearing a brand-new uniform ornamented with plenty of gold braid, and a white-topped gold-braided cap, was conducting a final inspection of the Southern Glamour, anchored in Honolulu harbour with steam up, ready to put to sea at any moment. And Samuel Simpson was pleased with himself and with the vessel which he considered the "smartest and prettiest little craft in the whole Pacific." He was pleased, also, with his crew, composed mostly of men who had sailed with him many times before. He had justified his boast that he would sign on the best two cooks in the Hawaiian Islands, and the cooks, when he inspected them in their refitted galley, were engaged in preparing a dinner which promised to be a banquet (the house-warming dinner to which Gloria Drake and Janet Gilmour had accepted the new "owner's" invitation). "Everything seems O.K. and yet I'm a bit puzzled about the whole affair," remarked Simpson to his first officer, when he had completed his tour of inspection. "The new boss is a queer bird, but I like him, and I'm not complaining. He and his comic secretary-man have gone ashore to fetch the ladies from the Imperial

Hawaiian Hotel, and Mr. Armitage told me he expected to bring them aboard round about six o'clock, when I've agreed to take them for a tour of inspection. 

You quite understand your orders?" "Yes, sir," the first officer responded promptly, "and

I've seen to it that the chief steward understands as well. You're dining with the party, and when you give 225

the signal the chief steward comes on deck and signals to me and I take charge and give orders until you take over and give me further instructions. The engineers will be standing by and I don't need to tell you that Macfarlane knows his job." "Yes, Macfarlane knows his job," drawled Captain 

Simpson, looking thoughtful. "I hope I'm a patriotic

American citizen, but when it comes to engines I like to know there's a Scotsman in charge down below. 

I guess everything'll be O.K. Seems our passenger list won't be very heavy-two men, two women, and one small dog, and I'll tell you that that small dog is a holy terror. When he came round to the shipyard when the Southern Glamour was laid up, he killed more rats and created more disturbance than anything of its size 

I've ever known. I wonder if the new boss has rigged up things as well ashore as I think we've rigged up things aboard here?" 

Things ashore were going "according to plan" (Alan's plan). He and Blossom had taken tea in the lounge of the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel with Gloria and Bunty, and they had been joined by Sybil Laval, who had deserted a hawk-faced, scraggy-bearded individual (who was Spider Sneyd) in order to join them, and intent upon being "nice" with the intention of discovering their plans.

The Clarissa had arrived the previous day and there had been hell to pay aboard when Sybil reported to Otto Hagenberg. Dandy Hagen considered that she had been wasting both time and money and had accomplished nothing, since she had failed to decoy either Alan or Gloria aboard the Clarissa, or to win their confidence, and he had stormed and raved and cursed until Spider Sneyd intervened. "Listen, Otto," said his jackal, in his harsh yet secretive voice. "I don't think Sybil has done so badly. 226

It's true you and the gang can't go ashore without the danger of being recognised and arrested, but if

Sybil stays on at the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel she should be able, after the Englishman has sailed in his yacht, to persuade Gloria Drake either to come on board the Clarissa or to get her to some place where we can get hold of her."

So Sybil was ashore again and had "gate-crashed" into the little tea party to discover that Gloria and 

Bunty were going to dine aboard the Southern

Glamour. 

"I make no apology for not having invited you, Miss Laval," said Alan, who still seemed to be in high spirits and was behaving and talking almost as if he had been drinking. "You see, I am sailing to-night, and 

I knew that your guests aboard the Clarissa would expect you to resume your position as their hostess. However, as we shall apparently both be cruising about for months, we may yet have opportunities of exchanging further invitations." 

It was just about six o'clock in the evening when 

Alan and Blossom, escorted by Rodmell Homestall 

King, who was probably wondering what it all meant, helped Gloria and Bunty into the boat that had been sent ashore from the Southern Glamour. Imp seemed to have caught some of his master's excitement, for he barked at everybody and everything in the boat, and then raced madly round the deck when eventually the whole party safely boarded the Southern Glamour, on which Captain Samuel Simpson received and greeted the ladies with bluff courtesy, before inviting them to make a tour of the whole vessel. "This is what Mr. Armitage calls the honeymoon suite," he said to Gloria, as he showed her through the cabins when the tour of inspection was almost at an end. "Mr. Armitage planned all the decorations 227

and ordered the furnishings himself. I think you will agree with me that he has done a good job?" "I think it is perfectly lovely," answered Gloria, and she seemed to be quite sincere in her admiration as she looked around. "If I wanted to furnish and decorate a suite for my own personal use I could not possibly have chosen better, and might possibly have chosen worse." "Thank you for these few kind words, dear lady," said Alan smilingly. "I am so glad you are pleased. 

Now, good people, what about cocktails before dinner? 99 Darkness was falling as the little company of five -Captain Simpson, Alan, Blossom, Gloria and Bunty -sat down to dinner in the brilliantly, but softly and harmoniously, lighted saloon. There was actually a sixth member of the party, for Rodmell Homestall King was prowling around the saloon sniffing for imaginary rats and trying to attract attention to himself by growling in corners; but, as he had visited the ship's galley during the course of the tour of inspection and found the cook very hospitable, he was not really interested in the proceedings.

It was a most excellent dinner, and the host laughed and jested and seemed to be in the highest of high spirits. The chief steward himself attended the serving of the dishes and dispened the wine, and it was about the time when the third course had been served that Captain Simpson, who, like Imp, seemed to have caught the infection of Alan's high spirits, made a signal to the chief steward, and asked him to switch on the radio. The steward switched on the radio and then disappeared from the saloon. The radio was broadcasting band music, and it was so loud that even the brazen-voiced Captain Simpson had to shout to make himself heard. 228

"I'll go and get the wireless operator to see to this darn' thing myself," he bawled, rising from the table. "I'll have it disconnected or toned down or something, Mr. Armitage. Meanwhile I'll leave you in charge of the party. Excuse me, ladies."

The radio continued to blare and went on blaring until the assistant stewards had served the dessert and Alan had refilled the wine glasses. Then suddenly there came a silence as the radio was cut off and gradually Gloria became conscious of a throbbing sound, and of the fact that the knife and fork on the plate before her were vibrating slightly. "Why, the boat is moving!" she cried, starting up from her chair and looking wide-eyed at Alan, who nodded and smiled at her in the enigmatic manner that she had found so exasperating at Haleiwa, and yet with a new excitement in his manner. "Yes, by God! We are moving, and moving at high speed!" exclaimed Blossom. "What does it mean, Alan?" "It means, my dear Blossom, and my dear Gloria, that the Southern Glamour set out on her cruise-her honeymoon cruise-ten minutes or a quarter of an hour ago while the radio was blaring. We have now rounded Diamond Head and are heading for Raiatea, in the Marquesas, which I am told is truly a beautiful spot, but, of course, much less sophisticated than Honolulu. Captain Simpson tells me it will be about a five days' run, but time does not matter on a cruise like this."

The colour drained out of Gloria's face. She was standing tense, gripping the back of her chair, and still gazing at him with dilated eyes. "Surely you are carrying the joke too far, Rodmell?" she exclaimed after a tense pause, scared at heart by Alan's unwavering gaze and queer smile. 229

"Yes, I quite agree, Miss Gloria," added Blossom, swallowing his drink before rising to his feet to gaze reproachfully at Alan through his big glasses. "You are surely carrying the joke-if it is a joke-too far, Alan. Miss Gloria and Bunty came aboard as your guests, and you can't mean to say that you intend to take them to the Marquesas against their will?" "Why suggest it is against their will, my dear Blossom?" drawled Alan with exasperating slowness, sipping his wine and then lighting a cigarette. "Kidnapping seems to be America's most popular and profitable pursuit at the moment, so why shouldn't I take a hand in the game? . . . Have you any objections to visiting the Marquesas, Bunty?"

Bunty seemed to be the only unmoved member of the party. She sat at the table twiddling round the stem of her wine glass between her fingers, her eyes downcast. 

'Anywhere in the South Seas will suit me and interest me, I'm thinking, Mr. Armitage," responded 

Bunty without looking up. "I expect you planned your honeymoon trip before you married Miss Gloria this morning." "Married Miss Gloria this morning?" echoed Blossom with a gasp. "Married me this morning!" cried Gloria. "Are you crazy?" "You'll have to decide whether it's you or me that's crazy if you are going to deny that you didna marry Mr. Armitage this morning at the third time of asking," retorted Bunty deliberately, but with a suspicion of a flush on her face as she picked up her glass and emptied it. "You'll be tellin' me next, I suppose, that ye didna know the boat was goin' tae sail sae suddenly, and that ye didna give me orders to have your luggage sent on board?" 230

Gloria's voice broke and with a sudden intake of 233

breath she made a frantic dash for the door, only to find herself in Alan's arms, crushed close to his breast with his lips crushed devouringly on her own and seeming to sap away all her powers of resistance. She ceased to struggle and offered no resistance as he picked her up in his arms again and carried her into the inner cabin which in turn gave access to the "boudoir." Then for a time came oblivion. Drowsily Gloria opened her eyes to find it was day and the sun was sending shafts of golden light through the port-holes. Her head was throbbing dully, as it did occasionally after a long, tiring day under the glaring lights in an overhead studio, and at first, while the lees of sleep still lingered, she thought vaguely that what had happened had been but a sort of confused but thrilling nightmare dream.

She turned on her pillows, and next moment had started up with a gasping cry as she caught sight of

Alan. He was sleeping peacefully, half smiling in his sleep, and in repose he looked just a handsome boy rather than a worldly man of thirty-four. But to 

Gloria, judging by her expression of horror at that instant, he seemed like some hideous ogre or poisonous reptile. "Oh God!" breathed Gloria, her hand pressed convulsively to her wildly-throbbing heart. "So it is true, and not a dream! It happened, and I-I-oh, merciful heaven !"

Alan stirred and opened his eyes, stretching himself languidly. "Darling," he murmured sleepily; and in a flash was wide awake as he looked up into Gloria's lovely face, almost grey with horror. Quickly he sat up, still with a smile on his lips, but now the smile was forced. "Good morning, dear bride !" he exclaimed in an altered tone, 234

Oh, don't you realise what you have done?" "This is a crazy world, Gloria mia, and I suppose

"I did not really want to break your heart, Rodmell, but only to make you love me," said Gloria, who was now crying weakly. "You certainly succeeded, but you may remember

"The world-your world, as I have already told you, now knows that we were secretly married in Honolulu yesterday and that you have eloped with me. I telephoned 

Inspector Harrigan, telling him that the first trip of the reconditioned Southern Glamour was to be our honeymoon tour and he provided Bunty with special facilities for passing through the Customs and bringing your wedding trousseau aboard, said he had guessed what was going to happen and asked me to convey his congratulations. Your intention in the first place, I take it, was to use me for purposes of publicity, so you may be interested to know that I left a letter for Mr. 

Max Rosen at the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel, with instructions that it should be delivered to him a little while after the time I had fixed for the Southern

marry you and take you with me on this cruise, and he cabled back his blessings." "But I'm not married to you!" protested Gloria, who had been listening somewhat dazedly, and with conflicting emotions. I think, my dear Gloria, you had better accustom yourself to the idea that you are married to me, if only to avoid complications. Captain

Simpson and his officers and crew might be shocked if you inform them that you are not my wife, and might have a poor opinion of us both. Just think for a moment: Your father believes we are married, so does 

Max Van Rosen and so do Inspector Harrigan and your Bunty. Why do you refuse to believe it yourself in the circumstances? Why not play the part of the happy bride, and charm my heart anew?" "You are making mock of me," cried Gloria, beginning to sob half hysterically. "I did not think you could be so cruel and heartless.' 

"You will remember, Gloria mia, that you took my heart from me," said Alan quite gently. "You taught me the meaning of love, and now I am going to teach you the meaning of love and take your heart from you to heal what is left of my own. The day is yet young. Let me begin another lesson now by kissing away your tears.' 

Gloria struggled and cried out as his arms enfolded her, but within a few minutes she was lying passive against his breast. 237

CHAPTER XIX 

LOSSOM instinctively felt that something was wrong, and he was sorely troubled in heart and mind. So troubled, indeed, that he had failed to find any consolation in whisky, had spent a troubled night, and daybreak found him pacing the deck of the Southern Glamour with Imp at his heels. He paused at last in his restless perambulation of the deck, deserted save for a couple of sailors busy with mops and a hosepipe, to rest his arms on the rail and gaze unseeingly across the great stretch of placid blue waters to where Hawaii was becoming a mere blur on the horizon. Imp, after a few minutes, rose on his hind legs and pawed at Blossom's trousers with a whimper, and Blossom bent down and patted the little fellow's head. "I don't know what to make of it all, Imp," he said, and the Sealyham grunted as if he understood, and licked his hand. 

Rodmell Homestall King did not know what to make of things either. He had tracked his beloved master and the female woman he had "adopted" to the door of their suite, but although he had begged, implored and demanded admission by scratching, whimpering and growling and barking, the humans inside had taken no notice. That had troubled and distressed him, who naturally had no knowledge of sound-proof cabins, and was accustomed to having the door opened by his master when he begged or demanded admission in his own doggy language. If the cabins were soundproof, however, the gangways or corridors leading to them were not, and Rodmell Homestall King made 238

such a hullabaloo that at length Blossom had picked. him up and carried him into his own cabin, where he had spent the night protesting at intervals.

So when Alan eventually appeared on deck, both 

Blossom and Imp pounced on him immediately, the first-named metaphorically, of course, the latter 

"Hoi! Hoi! Hoi! Hoobloominray!" barked Imp, hurling himself at his beloved master and trying to wag his stumpy tail while expressing his delight vocally and at the same time to leap up into Alan's arms with the idea of licking his ears off. "Ow, master, I am so glad to see you! I thought you were lost for ever, and 

I've been so miserable. Hoi! don't look at me in that sad way, master. I've been a good dog and haven't done anything wrong. 

He wriggled delightedly and licked profusely as

Alan, smiling in spite of himself, picked him up in his arms after pulling his ears, patting his head and giving him an affectionate shaking. "All right, all right, Imp, that will do, thank you," exclaimed Alan, tucking the dog into the crook of his arm. "I am afraid I quite forgot about you, old chap.. I hope he wasn't much of a nuisance during the night, Blossom." "He kicked up a deuce of a noise when he found he couldn't get into your cabin, but he only swore mildly at me during the night," answered Blossom, looking hard at Alan through his big glasses. "Have a cigarette? . It isn't Imp I've been worrying about,

Alan, but you and Miss Gloria-or should I say Mrs. 

Alan Rodmell Armitage? Tell me, Alan, is Gloria your wife?" .

Alan lit the cigarette he had extracted from Blossom's case before replying. "My dear Blossom, Bunty has told you we are 239

married, I can show you a cable from Mr. Cyrus Oke

Cole giving his blessing on the union, and if you were to return to Honolulu, Mr. Max Van Rosen and Inspector Harrigan would both be able to tell you they had been informed of the marriage," he drawled, with an odd smile. "Incidentally, I should think you would find the American papers full of romantic and totally fictitious details of the sensational elopement of the famous Miss Gloria Drake, the film star, and idol of all hearts." "But I don't understand," objected Blossom, shaking his head. "I have talked to Bunty and she is as cryptic as you are. If you got married yesterday why didn't you let me be best man, or whatever they call it in America, or, at least, one of the witnesses ?" "Perhaps it was to save you the cost of a wedding present, old chap," responded Alan, still smiling. "Have you any whisky in your cabin, Blossom? It isn't often, as you know, that I drink in the morning, but I think we ought to take the opportunity of toasting the bride, and you can toast the bridegroom if you like. 

Incidentally, I suppose it isn't customary for a bride and bridegroom to take their secretary-companions with them on their honeymoon, but-well, the circumstances are exceptional."

Blossom took off his glasses and polished the lenses with a big silk handkerchief-a sure sign that he was perturbed. "Yes, of course I've got whisky in my cabin, and I'll be only too pleased to drink to you and Gloria, but I am still puzzled," he commented after a pause. "I'm puzzled about Bunty, too, Alan, and I'm wondering why, after you had carried off Miss Gloria last night, she seemed for a little while to be in tremendously high spirits and then suddenly she burst into tears and rushed away from me." 240

"Women are damnably queer, aren't they, Blossom, and at times they behave in the most mysterious and illogical fashion," retorted Alan. "I knocked on the door of Bunty's cabin-which apparently isn't soundproof-before I came on deck, and asked her to attend on Gloria, and Bunty called back that she'd be with her in a few minutes. What about that whisky?" 

They went below to Blossom's cabin, and Blossom dispensed whisky as he thought it should be dispensed, even at such an early hour of the morning, and they talked at random, more or less, as they drank, for Blossom was too sensitive and self-conscious to ask any direct question, and Alan had no inclination to offer any explanations. Blossom was as puzzled by Alan's attitude and reticence as was Gloria by Bunty's manner when she brought in her morning cup of tea, brewed, as usual, by herself, with water boiled on a spirit stove. "I havina been on deck yet, but it's a lovely morning frae what I can see o' it out o' the port-holes, and we seem to be out o' sight o' land," remarked Bunty with a sort of forced cheerfulness, and without meeting 

Gloria's eyes, after one swift glance at her. "What do you think you would like to wear this morning? I think it's going to be hot, so I'd suggest either a white silk frock or yon two-piece white pique yachting costume." 

Gloria did not answer. She sat up in bed, sipping her tea and watching Bunty as the latter busied herself unpacking a trunk, and carefully laying out, and smoothing out, frocks and costumes. Suddenly she set her cup down with a clatter. "Bunty Gilmour, am I mad?" she demanded, in a voice that brought Bunty upright in an instant, and made her turn to meet the direct, compelling glance of her eyes. "Mad?" Bunty repeated, looking just a trifle dis- 241

concerted, but recovering herself almost immediately. "I'm thinking you were a bit mad a week or two ago, but I am hoping you've now returned to sanity. Do you think you'd prefer the silk frock or the white costume after I've got your bath ready?" "Has Rodmell Armitage bewitched everybody?" was the unexpected reply from Gloria, as she pushed back the mass of silky chestnut hair from her brow. "Why do you say I have been mad? Why did you say last night that I am married to Rodmell Armitage? 

Why have you helped him to take possession of me?" "Listen to me, Miss Gloria, or Mrs. Armitage," said Bunty very deliberately. "I have served you faithfully for nearly seven years, and I think I am serving ye faithfully now. Alan Rodmell Armitage saved your father's life, and he and Blossom saved you from the

Dandy Hagen gang at the risk of their lives. At

Bunty's voice broke and suddenly she covered her face with her hands and dropped down on her knees by the bedside. It was the first time that Gloria had ever seen her in tears and she was touched in spite of herself and began to cry in sympathy. 242

"Bunty, dear Bunty, don't make things worse," she exclaimed brokenly. "I'm not really a vamp, or such a heartless beast as you seem to think. It was only because I thought he despised women, and me in particular, that I did what I did. And now—now-oh, Bunty, he has made me pay in full!" 

She in turn broke down, and Bunty, recovering herself, rose from her knees, blinked away her own tears, took her in her arms and soothed and comforted her as she might have comforted a hurt child. And having soothed her she suddenly took her by the shoulders, gave her a sound shaking and pushed her back among her pillows. "I've wanted tae shake ye for the past week, and now I've done it," Bunty said rather breathlessly. “If

If I'd won the love of a man like Alan Armitage I'd be proud and happy, and if I'd tried tae break his heart in jest or to satisfy my own vanity, I'd be thinking black, burning shame of myself.

This was a new and strange Bunty confronting her, quite a different person from the calm, capable, coldly logical secretary-companion-chaperon who had served her and protected her so well for years, and Gloria was conscious of a feeling akin to fear as she met the blaze of the Scotswoman's dark eyes. She looked up to find Bunty regarding her with a queer expression. "You see the idea, Bunty?" she resumed as Bunty made no comment, but merely continued to gaze at her fixedly. "9 "I think I will wear the two-piece pique costume,' said Gloria, pretending not to hear her, but feeling vaguely disturbed. "I'll have my bath now."

It said much for her power as an actress that when she appeared on deck half an hour or so later she seemed to everyone to be radiantly happy, and she certainly looked even more adorably lovely than ever 246

in her white outfit. Blossom, as he greeted her, was immediately convinced that all his doubts and fears had been completely groundless, and he felt almost inclined to offer Alan some sort of grovelling apology.

Yes, he was more perplexed than ever he had been in his life, for all day Gloria continued to play the part of the radiantly happy bride, and it was not until night had fallen and the stars of the Southern Cross were gleaming and leering and blinking from a blue-black velvety sky, that he dared to ask the question that had set his heart and brain once more into a turmoil. 

They were promenading the deck after dinner, both silent although they had talked and laughed with affected gaiety during the meal, and both were conscious of a certain tension, and felt instinctively that something—some unknown "something"-might happen at any moment. It was a night that seemed to be made for love and loving, calm, fine clear yet dark, with only a soft, warm breeze which touched their cheeks like a caress as the Southern Glamour purred her way through the phosphorescent Pacific. "Gloria, does this mean that-that you have found your heart, that you really care, and have discovered 249

that you love me?" burst out Alan suddenly, his voice husky and unsteady with pent-up emotion.

He gripped Gloria's hands as he spoke, but she drew away from him. "It merely means that I have decided to make the best of the situation, although I hate you," Gloria answered, herself breathless and husky. "To me your idea of loving is utterly detestable, and if I have a heart it does not belong to you. I am being honest with you. I am prepared to pretend to the others that I am happy as Mrs. Rodmell Armitage, but-but I don't think I shall ever be able to forgive you."

She felt, rather than saw, Alan wince as he dropped her hands, and then he laughed; but it was a laugh that had no mirth in it and was not good to hear. His contorted face seemed to her to gleam whitely and strangely in the starlight, and again for a few moments she felt terrified and remorseful as she realised how sorely she must have hurt him. But before she could make up her mind to say anything more Alan had recovered himself. "So you are being honest with me-eh?" he drawled, taking a cigarette from his case and lighting it with slow deliberation. "I wonder if you are being honest with yourself, and to yourself? As she opened the outer door of the cabin a sort of shaggy white whirlwind struck her ankles, yelped a staccato apology and hurled itself into the boudoir and into Gloria's arms. The minature white whirlwind was, of course, Rodmell Homestall King, who had been making strange noises, and giving imitations of a 257

vacuum cleaner with the suction pipe out of order, at the cabin door for some time, without attracting attention. 

Imp had become bored, after sitting in the smokeroom where even his beloved master merely shuffled a pack of cards without taking any notice of him, and did not even offer him one of the cards to play with, although he gave five to each of the three other men seated at the table with him, so Imp had decided to go in search of the female woman, and had traced her without difficulty to the boudoir suite. "I'm thinking that's maybe a good omen," said 

Bunty, looking back from the door to see Imp "kissing" Gloria's chin and then snuggling his square, shaggy head against her breast with a grunt of satisfaction as she cuddled him in her arms. "He'll be company for ye, anyway, while I'm seein' Blossom."

She saw Blossom, but did not have an opportunity (nor attempt to make an opportunity) to discuss affairs with him that night. She looked into the halfopen door of the smoke-room to see Blossom, Alan, Captain Simpson and Mr. Macfarlane engaged in what appeared to be a somewhat hectic game of poker, judging by the amount of money on the table, but 

Bunty knew her world too well to attempt to call one of the players away from the poker table to tell him a tale of woe, knowing full well that, whether he was winning or losing, and no matter how well-intentioned he might be normally, he would feel like cursing her for spoiling the game. She paused long enough in the doorway, however, to notice that Alan's eyes seemed unnaturally bright, that there was a hectic spot of color on each cheek-bone of his lean, tanned face, that his movements were quick and nervous, and that he seemed to be helping himself liberally from Blossom's bottle of whisky. 258

Just as she had decided that it was hopeless to do anything that night and was turning away from the door of the smoking-room, the engine-room bell clanged, and Captain Simpson dropped his cards, as also did Macfarlane, and both sprang to their feet when the bell clanged a second time and the Southern 

Glamour stopped and then went full speed astern.

Both men swept the money in front of them on the table into their pockets, and the captain made a dash for the bridge, and the chief engineer to the engineroom, bringing the poker game to an abrupt close. 

Captain Simpson reached the bridge to receive a report from his first officer that the Southern Glamour had struck something and the look-out man had reported in a shout, just a minute or two too late, that a Kanaka canoe was crossing the bows of the yacht. The look-out man was to be pardoned, for, of course, the natives aboard the canoe were showing no lights, which was why she had not been seen until it was too late to prevent the canoe being cut in twain by the Southern 

Glamour. 

Already, in response to a shout from the first officer, and before Captain Simpson reached the bridge, before the engine-room bell clanged a third time, signalling "Stop," members of the crew were hastily preparing to lower a boat. There was a miniature searchlight fitted on the bridge and Captain Simpson switched it on as the boat swizzed down from the davits, at the same time yelling instructions. 

Within ten minutes half a dozen Kanakas, doing their best to smile, but actually feeling very scared and very sorry for themselves, had scrambled aboard the Southern Glamour from the boat that had picked them up. To be thrown into the sea, by accident or otherwise, means little or nothing to a Kanaka, for, as a rule, he can swim in a way that might well make 259

a fish jealous, if a fish were capable of displaying jealousy, and he laughs or smiles even if he has been looking into the face of Death. 

These Kanakas, discovered Captain Simpson, who could speak their lingo, had every excuse for not being able to smile or laugh, for they had been struck by a sudden typhoon while fishing somewhere off the Marquesas, and although by some miracle they had managed to keep afloat, they had been adrift at sea without food or water for two days and nights before being run down by the Southern Glamour, and three men of their company had died. "They'll be all right after the stewards have given them something to eat and drink, and they'll make themselves quite comfortable on deck, or wherever we like to stow them," said Captain Simpson to Alan, who, of course, had come on deck, accompanied by Blossom, to discover the cause of all the commotion. "As soon as I've got the boat aboard again I can signal 'Full Speed Ahead' to the engine-room. Maybe you'll give me my chance of revenge at poker tomorrow night, Mr. Armitage." "Oddly enough, Captain, I remarked only comparatively speaking a little while ago that revenge takes a variety of forms," responded Alan with a queer laugh. "I think I had better go to bed, although Canopus and the Cross overhead are inviting me to spend the night on deck communing with them. But they might induce me to think, and I don't want to think, either about the things that were, or the things that might be or might have been."

Ten minutes later the Southerin Glamour was again heading full speed ahead for the Marquesas, the Kanakas were being fed exceedingly well on food that was strange to them, and Blossom and Alan were sitting facing each other in the latter's cabin with a bottle of 260

whisky on the table between them. Blossom was puzzled. Never before had he known Alan to drink whisky in the reckless way that he did himself, and never before had he known him to talk at random about nothing in particular, as he was now doing. "I think, my dear Alan, you had better go to bed," suggested Blossom at last, and then added, quite innocently: "Won't Gloria be waiting for you?”

Alan looked hard at him, and then laughed again in the same queer way that he had done when Captain Sam Simpson had asked him for his revenge at poker. "Blossom, old man, I know you are at heart a sentimentalist, although you try to disguise the fact, but there are times when I suspect you of being an ironist, a satirist, and a humorist," he drawled. "However, your advice is invariably sound, so I think I will go to bed. Where's Imp?" "Imp?" Blossom looked around as if he expected to find Imp somewhere on the floor of the cabin, and then he shook his pink head. "I don't know, Alan. Last time I saw Imp he was sitting in the smoke-room trying to attract your attention while we were playing poker. I'll go and enquire about him if I don't find him asleep in the smoke-room."

The steward who was cleaning out the smoke-room told him he had seen the little dog whimpering outside the door of the boudoir suite and seen him admitted by Miss Gilmour, so Blossom returned to Alan with that information. "So Imp is with her, is he?" said Alan, and once again emitted that queer laugh. "It's a dangerous thing, Blossom, to love a dog, as you probably know, for it means that you break your heart about once every seven years. It's a much more dangerous matter to love a woman, for a woman can break your heart without dying, which is the only difference between 261

women and dogs. I love my dog, Blossom, and I don't see why Gloria Drake should have Rodmell Homestall

King as well as my heart to play with or ill-treat. I want my dog back and I am going to get it—and now." 

He jumped up and strode out of the cabin, leaving

Blossom looking and feeling dazed and bewildered, and a minute later his thumb was on the bell-push on the jamb of the door of the boudoir suite. He had to ring twice before Bunty, in pyjamas and dressing-gown, opened the door suspiciously, but her face lit up at the sight of him. "You've relented, Mr. Armitage," Bunty exclaimed breathlessly. "Sorry to disturb you, but I want my dog," drawled 

Alan. "I understand it is in here." "She's got it, and—and I'm thinkin' Imp has been some sort o' comfort to her," said Bunty. "She is still up. Will ye no' come in, Mr. Armitage?" "Well, of course, I could whistle to spare myself the trouble of coming in to collect him, but possibly he might not hear me, and one looks and feels rather absurd standing and whistling to a dog which takes no notice," responded Alan. "Yes, I suppose I'd better come in, Bunty."

He was in a strange frame of mind as he walked into the inner cabin to find Imp curled up comfortably in Gloria's lap, seemingly fast asleep. But the instant he heard his beloved master's voice he sprang up, then down, yelping with excitement and pleasure. Gloria also sprang up, her eyes wide with amazement not unmixed with apprehension, her heart throbbing wildly. "Sorry to disturb you, but I want my dog," Alan repeated, in the same tone that he had used to Bunty. "Almost it would seem as if you had affected him in some way, since he now seems ready to desert me after 262

having given me the impression that he loved me and regarded me as a sort of demi-god."

Gloria winced and a sudden flush stained her white face. "At least Imp is kinder than you, for I am sure he would never deliberately have tried to hurt me," she retorted with an intake of breath. "Surely you realise that if your dog loves me I cannot be the fiend in human form that you seem to imagine I am?" "I've no great faith in dogs as judges of human nature," drawled Alan, as he stooped to pick up Imp, who squirmed delightedly and tried to lick his face. "I've known crooks who loved dogs, and dogs who loved crooks and other persons who to me were highly objectionable. I apologise again for disturbing you. 

Good night." 

To his utter amazement, as he turned towards the door Gloria flung herself on her knees before him and stretched out her hands in appeal. "Rodmell, forgive me, oh, please, please forgive me!" she cried breathlessly. "I did not mean to hurt you so badly, but wanted to pay you out for imposing your will upon me. I did not realise what I was saying.

I was acting and-and trying to punish you. Forgive me. I did not mean what I said about using you for publicity purposes, and I feel sure you did not mean what you said when you threatened to shame and defame me. 

"Quite an admirable piece of acting!" drawled Alan, trying to appear unmoved, and conquering an impulse to take her in his arms. "Please get up, Miss Drake. 

Imp evidently thinks you are playing some new sort of game, and if I let him go he may spoil your tragic pose by licking your face or something of the sort." "Oh, Rodmell, I swear I'm not acting," Gloria al most wailed. "I'm sorry for what I said to-night, and 263

I want you to forgive me and to promise you will marry me." She rose to her feet and clasped his free hand in both her own, tears welling to her eyes as she did so. "Rodmell, I know that until yesterday you were a man of honour and that perhaps I was to blame for driving you to desperation. As an English gentleman you will not defame me and be so cruel as to refuse to marry me. You must-you must make me your wife in the eyes of the world even if-if-" She broke off, apparently overcome by emotion, released his hand and buried her lovely, tear-stained face in her handkerchief. Alan's mind was again in a turmoil, he did not know what to believe or how to act, but her appeal to his sense of honour had touched a responsive chord in his heart of hearts. "Since you now wish to go through the formality of marriage, after having thrice refused it, I suppose I cannot decline to grant your request," he said slowly, after a pause, and the look of relief in Gloria's eyes as she dropped her handkerchief from her face gave him an odd sensation. It struck him suddenly as being strangely akin to the joyous look in Imp's eyes when he returned after an absence. "Thank you, Rodmell," gasped Gloria, and would have taken his hand again, but he withdrew from her slightly. "The Marquesas are a French possession, so it is almost a certainty there will be a priest at Raiatea qualified to perform the ceremony and a French official. of some sort who should be able to give you a certificate," he resumed in the same deliberate manner. "I shall arrange the matter as soon as we arrive at 

Raiatea. Good night again."

He strode quickly from the saloon before Gloria could collect her thoughts, and she heard the door of the outer cabin bang behind him. 264

"Oh, Gloria, my dearie, I'm so thankful ye've come tae yer senses at last, and I hope frae my heart that ye'll both be happy now," exclaimed Bunty, reappearing suddenly. "There isn't any question of happiness, Bunty, but 

I had to fool him into promising to marry me," said 

Gloria with a nervous laugh. "You know, you can't get divorced unless you first get married."

Alan spent the night communing with Canopus and the Southern Cross after all. 265

FOU 

OUR days later, when Bunty brought Gloria her early morning cup of tea in the boudoir suite,

Gloria, after stretching herself luxuriously, suddenly realised that the Southern Glamour was motionless. "We're anchored in the Lagoon at Raiatea," explained Bunty in response to her exclamation and question. "Ye should go up on deck and have a look at the place as soon as you have finished your tea. 

I've been up mysel' and I'm tellin' ye, kid, that the place is so lovely that I can hardly believe it's real. 

Come up and see for yersel', and ye'll think ye're dreaming."

Five minutes later Gloria was on deck, wearing only a gaily-coloured kimono over her green silk pyjamas and feeling as if she were indeed enjoying a delightful dream, although fully awake. Before her was Raiatea, jagged of skyline, volcanic peaks jutting heavenwards like many minarets, with vividly green palms and banana trees clustering round their bases and growing in profusion right down to the silvery shore.

The waters of the Lagoon itself were a miracle of loveliness. Crystal-like in their clearness, they were intershot with colours more marvellous than any rainbow, turquoise and emerald alternating with peacock blue and jade green, and through these brightly coloured fishes, gleaming like jewels, darted and sported in hundreds. "As you said, Bunty, it seems almost too wonderful to be true," murmured Gloria after a few minutes, 266

leaning her arms on the rail and gazing shorewards. "It seems like—well, like a dream come true.' 99 Bunty did not reply immediately. She was gazing down into the multi-coloured waters of the Lagoon, her dark brows drawn together in a thoughtful frown. "I'm wondering what you've been dreaming about for the last three nights," she said slowly at last, without raising her head to look at Gloria. "I'm wondering if you've been acting for the last three days or just trying to behave natural. I'm wondering if you have a heart, and I'm wondering how you're feeling now you've seen Raiatea and know that to-day will likely be your wedding day." 

Gloria, with her elbows on the rail and her chin cupped in her hands, made no answer for what seemed to Bunty a long, long time, but continued to gaze fixedly at the silver shore of Raiatea. Bunty had mentioned acting. To Gloria it seemed as if everyone, including herself, had been acting for three or four days. She guessed that Bunty had confided in Blossom, and she knew that Blossom's attempts to appear his usual jovial and genial self when in her company had. been mere pretence. Even Bunty's manner had seemed to her somehow unnatural, and as for Alan, she had found herself completely baffled. Even when she had contrived to be alone with him he had treated her much in the way she imagined that he would have treated any young and amorous woman who had tried to flirt with him on a pleasure cruise, and had adroitly changed the subject whenever she attempted to mention anything that had happened in the immediate past or anything that might happen in the near future. That had hurt her pride more than any display of anger, hatred, or even contempt would have done, hurt her more even than if Alan had again imposed his will upon her. 267

"My wedding day," she murmured at length, just when Bunty was beginning to wonder if she had fallen into a trance. "You mentioned dreams. If ever I had day-dreams about marriage, I pictured myself in white satin with a flowing train, wearing a wreath of orange blossom and carrying a sheaf of lilies." "And was the bridegroom ye dreamed about anything like Alan Rodmell Armitage?" interposed Bunty, looking at her sideways.

Gloria did not answer, for the simple reason that she did not know how to answer. She was trying to persuade herself that she hated and despised Rodmell Armitage, and that she would take delight in fooling him again and in telling him she had fooled him, immediately the marriage ceremony had been performed, yet at the back of her mind there was something, some suppressed emotion, which she could not analyse. "I think we had better go and dress," she said abruptly. "Incidentally, it's rather surprising that there is no one on deck except ourselves to admire Raiatea." "Mr. Armitage and Mr. Flower and Captain Simpson went ashore in a boat half an hour or so ago, if that's what ye're meaning," remarked Bunty as they went below again. "Mr. Armitage shouted tae me that he expected to be back to breakfast."

Clad in spotless white and wearing a broad-brimmed white sun-hat, Gloria was on deck again within an hour, just in time to see Alan, Blossom, Captain Simpson and a black-bearded French official, in a uniform ornamented by many brass buttons and a considerable amount of gold braid, boarding the yacht by the accommodation ladder. Captain Simpson saluted her, navy fashion, the French official bowed low, and Alan and Blossom raised their pith helmets as she wished them "Good morning." "Don't wait for me if you are hungry, you folks, but 268

start on breakfast right away," cried Captain Simpson, in his breezy fashion. "I've got a bit of official business to transact with my old friend here, Capitaine Lefèbre, who thinks the English breakfast is a barbarous habit." "No, no, non! Pas de tout!" laughed Capitaine 

Lefèbre, bowing again to Gloria. "But I do not eat in ze morning." "But I guess you can still drink in the mornings," said Simpson, with a smile. "Come along to my cabin and I'll fix you." "While the captain is transacting his bit of official business with Monsieur Lefèbre, it may interest you to know that I have arranged for our little bit of official business to be transacted about eleven o'clock this morning," remarked Alan in a casual sort of tone to Gloria, as the stewards began to serve breakfast a few minutes later. "There is a charming old French priest ashore, eager and willing to perform the ceremony, quite delighted, in fact, at the thought of uniting in marriage two white people, which it seems he has never done before during the course of his long stay in Raiatea."

Gloria crimsoned slightly as she met his challenging glance, but forced herself to smile, conscious of the fact that Blossom and Bunty were watching her. "How splendid!" she exclaimed. "I like the thought of being married by a French priest. The ceremony at Honolulu seemed so casual and unofficial that I-I really didn't understand that it constituted a marriage."

The others at the table could not help but admire her for the way in which she carried the matter off, and Alan, in particular, once again found himself puzzled, but "played up to her." "Yes, it was rather a casual sort of ceremony at Honolulu," he said as he devoted his attention to his 269

grapefruit; "but I feel sure the French padre ashore will contrive to give this morning's service an odour of sanctity."

He was right, as it transpired. When they went ashore to the delectable island of Raiatea shortly before eleven o'clock, they found the French padre, who looked more like the patron of an estaminet in the Quartier Latin than a missionary-priest in the South Seas, awaiting them, attired in full canonicals, at the door of his little chapel, around which squatted a group of smiling, inquisitive natives who jabbered excitedly to each other at sight of the lovely white "Mary woman."

The ceremony was in French, which Gloria did not understand, but the priest knew enough English to translate the important part for her benefit and understanding, and she gasped involuntarily when Alan slipped a ring on to her finger. Something seemed to be choking her as the priest bade her kneel to receive his blessing, and she found Alan, grim-faced and tightlipped, kneeling beside her.

The religious ceremony over, a lean, weather-beaten French official in uniform, who had been seated at a small table, produced a book and several official forms for signature, and after much signing and countersigning, questions and answers, he handed Gloria a document which she guessed were her "marriage lines,' gabbled congratulations in French and kissed her hand. 

"I think it is the best man's privilege to kiss the bride," exclaimed Blossom, and suited the action to the word.

Gloria looked as if she were going to cry. She held out her right hand rather uncertainly to Alan and he merely raised it, touched it lightly with his lips and let it drop. "Congratulations, Mrs. Alan Rodmell Armitage," 270

he said quietly, with an unmoved expression. "Allow me to conduct you and Bunty back to the boat which is waiting to take you to the Southern Glamour. Blossom and I are going to do a tour of exploration to discover whether it will be safe to take you for a trip round or through the island later on."

Neither Bunty nor Gloria spoke as they were rowed back to the yacht. Each hesitated to ask the other questions and both found themselves quite perplexed by Alan's attitude. They climbed aboard the Southern 

Glamour to be greeted by Captain Simpson as they reached the deck. "Looks as if we're going to have company, Mrs. 

Armitage," said Sam Simpson with a gesture. "The 

Clarissa is just coming into the Lagoon. 99 Gloria glanced in the direction indicated by the 

Captain and saw the Clarissa making away from open water into the calm of the Lagoon, heard the rattle of chains as she let her anchor down and saw from the distance several white-clad figures moving rapidly up and down the deck. "Now that's queer," remarked Captain Simpson, narrowing his eyes and wrinkling his brows to gaze across the translucent waters at the new arrival. "Why has she anchored so close to the reef, instead of coming inshore and anchoring alongside us? I'll bet her skipper has never been to Raiatea before." "I want to go aboard the Clarissa, Captain Simpson, "exclaimed Gloria impulsively, turning to him after a few moments' silent contemplation of the other yacht. "The owner is a friend of mine. Will you order the men in the boat to take me across in a few minutes to the Clarissa?" "Sure, Mrs. Armitage," said Captain Simpson and bawled an order to the sailors in the boat to "stand by." 271

Escaping frae what?" "From Rodmell Armitage, of course," Gloria answered impatiently. She rushed out of the cabin before Bunty had any opportunity to make any response or protest, and a few minutes later she was being rowed swiftly across the Lagoon from the Southern Glamour to the 

Clarissa. Captain Simpson, watching through his binoculars, saw her welcomed on deck of the Clarissa by another woman in white, beside whom stood a lean individual with a scraggy beard, whose face was shaded by a wide-brimmed Panama hat. 

He saw the two white-clad women stand as if eagerly conversing on the deck of the Clarissa for a few minutes, and then they disappeared below, followed by the individual in the Panama hat; then he saw the boat belonging to his own vessel cast off and begin to row back, evidently acting on instructions from one of the officers aboard the Clarissa. 

What he did not, and, of course, could not see or hear was Gloria's cry of amazement and terror, and her look of horror, as Sybil Laval led her into the saloon and she found herself confronted, as Spider Sneyd slammed the door behind her, by Dandy Hagen, otherwise Earl Winter, otherwise Otto Hagenberg. 

She recognised him instantly, and realised that she had deliberately delivered herself into the hands of the kidnapper from whom she had fled in terror from Hollywood. "So we meet again, dear Miss Gloria Drake, and this time you have no mad Englishman to protect you!" exclaimed Dandy Hagen with a leering, mocking smile. "This must surely be the first time on record that anyone has voluntarily surrendered to a kidnapper. Perhaps you have brought your own ransom on board with you to save trouble?" 

Panic-stricken, Gloria turned towards the door of the saloon, but at sight of Sybil Laval grinning, and Spider Sneyd leering at her evilly, she had a revulsion 273

of feeling, and recaptured some of the courage of her Devonian ancestors. She drew herself up and looked from one to the other with a sort of regal contempt and loathing which made even Spider Sneyd feel inclined to grovel and squirm. "I should have known that you were only a female jackal, a liar and a deceiver," she said deliberately, addressing Sybil Laval, and unconsciously she adopted something of the manner of Alan Armitage when he found himself in a tight corner. "So I have delivered myself into the hands of a gang of kidnappers and blackmailers, have I? Perhaps you are already congratulating yourself on an easy capture? I would remind you that my husband and his friend who rescued me at the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel are quite capable of dealing with scum like you, and that this time you will not escape so lightly as you did last." She wheeled round and faced Dandy Hagen, still with the air of an indignant princess. "I would advise you, you skunk, to put me aboard a boat and send me back at once to the Southern Glamour, and then to clear out of here as fast as you can if you value your life and liberty," she continued. "If you encounter Rodmell Armitage again you will not escape as you did from Honolulu." "Quite a good bit of acting, sweetie, and you sure do play the swell game well," commented Dandy Hagen in his guttural voice, showing his white teeth in a mocking smile. "So you are married to Alan Rodmell 

Armitage, are you, and you think I'm afraid of him and his fat friend? Well, we'll just keep you aboard here until your bridegroom comes to find you, and then 

I'll have two ransoms to come instead of one, and, incidentally, I'll show you how Otto Hagenberg deals with any gor-darned swine of an Englishman who tries to get the better of him. Scum, are we, and you dare to call me a skunk! I'll teach you civility, my beauty!" 274

He made a spring at Gloria as he spoke, and as she recoiled from him, prepared to offer the most desperate resistance, Spider Sneyd and Sybil Laval seized her from behind and the former snapped a pair of handcuffs on her wrists. Gloria screamed involuntarily, but at once Sybil viciously thrust a handkerchief into her mouth, stifling her cries, and she laughed as Dandy

Hagen seized the captive and dragged her from the saloon into a cabin. may "So you're Rodmell Armitage's wife, are you?" exclaimed Hagenberg breathlessly, gloating over

Gloria as he flung her down on the bed. Oh, I think I shall enjoy making violent love-very violent love-to the beautiful Gloria Drake without any possibility of being repulsed." "You can cut that stuff out, Otto," Sybil Laval snapped behind him. "I'll tell you if you're thinking of starting any of your 'love' business again, I'll signal to the Southern Glamour and blow the gaff."

Otto Hagenberg turned on her, his eyes glinting evilly, his hands clenched, but as he met the threatening gaze of Sybil's china-blue eyes, he decided that it would be best to be diplomatic, so he winked meaningly and forced a smile. "You should know me well enough by now, Sybil, to know that I like doing a bit of the dramatic business, and when it comes to loving, the only woman who matters to me is you," he said. "I hope he does come aboard with Armitage, for I've a score to settle with him as well as with the other swine of an Englishman." 

Blossom did not come aboard the Clarissa with

Alan. As it happened he met in Raiatea, to his intense surprise and pleasure, a man he had known intimately at Cambridge, engaged now on archæological exploration work, in which Blossom was keenly interested, and Alan had left the couple ashore, Blossom having begged him to excuse him from returning to lunch aboard the Southern Glamour as he wished to accompany the Cambridge professor to the place where he had been unearthing prehistoric relics. 

Alan was not interested in Polynesian antiques, but even if he had been keenly interested he had matters of much greater importance to think about. He had married a girl who had charmed the very heart out of his body, only to trample it underfoot, who had won his love and broken his heart merely to gratify her 277

own vanity and prove she was the supreme possessor of what Hollywood tersely terms "S.A." and "IT"; married a girl whom he had taken and conquered in the manner of a cave-man, breaking and humbling her and hating and despising himself in the doing; married a girl whom he half believed loved him although he had bent and broken her pride. Some lines from 

Henley flashed through his mind as he returned to the 

Southern Glamour: 

"The old resentment lasts like death, For you love, yet you refrain. 

I break my heart on your hard unfaith, And I break my heart in vain."

He saw Bunty on deck and waved his hand to her, but she did not return the salute, and as the boat reached the accommodation ladder he saw she was pale and troubled and realised instinctively that something was seriously amiss. "Gloria's gone," announced Bunty, without preamble. "She's gone aboard the Clarissa yonder, and she doesna intend to come back to ye." 278
