88 

LAN gazed at Bunty as if he did not fully comprehend her words, and then he turned his head to stare across the sparkling waters of the Lagoon at the 

Clarissa, his eyes narrowing and his brows drawing together. "She's been gone an hour or more," resumed Bunty. "She ordered me to get her things packed and said she'd send a boat for them and me if Miss Laval agreed to take her, but the boat hasn't arrived yet, and of course I haven't packed her things. I decided to wait for you before doing anything.' 

"Thanks, Bunty," drawled Alan, drawing a deep breath and still staring in a preoccupied fashion at the 

Clarissa. "I suppose I'd better go across and fetch her back. Life is certainly not lacking in variety." 

His nonchalant air positively annoyed Bunty as he lit a cigarette before descending again to the motorboat. He had no definite idea of how he was going to act, but decided that the situation certainly seemed to call for delicate diplomatic handling, and he would probably have to resort to strategy and persuasion. 

He saw a white-clad figure that he recognised as

Sybil Laval appear on the deck of the Clarissa, and wave a handkerchief to him, and he returned the greeting by waving his sun-hat. Sybil was smiling her artificial smile as he climbed the ladder to the deck a few minutes later and shook hands, and he noticed immediately that she was trembling slightly, as if with suppressed excitement, and her china-blue eyes looked unnaturally bright. 279

"I am so glad to see you again, Mr. Armitage," gushed Sybil rather breathlessly. "Dear Gloria has been talking rather wildly, but I think she will actually be glad to see you, and I feel sure that between us we will bring her to reason. I guess that she is already sorry for having acted so impulsively." 

Alan had no suspicion of the hidden meaning of those last words, but was merely wondering subconsciously as he followed Sybil down the companionway why none of her guests were on deck. He did not sense danger, so he was totally unprepared when Dandy

Hagen and Joe Kruger sprang at him from the shadows as he reached the foot of the companion and felled him with rubber life-preservers. "Got you, you damned Englishman!" snarled 

Hagenberg with a yell of triumph, and kicked the unconscious man savagely as Spider Sneyd and Al Casey came rushing forward to tie up Alan hand and foot with ropes. "Lug him along and chuck him into the cabin along of the girl," resumed Dandy Otto when he had satisfied himself there was no possibility of Alan escaping from his bonds. "Better chuck some water over him. I'll deal with him when he regains consciousness. Yes, by God, I'll deal with him, and the schweinhund will rue the day he ever met Dandy Hagen!"

Alan opened his eyes dazedly ten minutes or so later, his head throbbing painfully, without any recollection of what had happened. Vaguely he thought that he had just awakened from a nightmare dream, or was still in the throes of a nightmare, when he tried to put up his hands to his aching head and found himself unable to move either hands or feet. He realised that he was lying on a bed, and he blinked his eyes as he turned on his side and discovered Gloria lying quite near him, her wonderful eyes dilated with terror, her 280

hands behind her back, her lovely face twisted and drawn as if she were suffering agonies of pain. 

Gloria had contrived to push out with her tongue the handkerchief that Sybil Laval had stuffed into her mouth as a gag, and at the sound of her voice Alan realised he was not in the throes of a nightmare. "Oh, Rodmell, they've got you, too!" Gloria gasped huskily. "The Dandy Hagen gang have got us both!" "Yes, Dandy Hagen has got you both," snarled a guttural voice, and Alan turned his head and looked up to see the fair, golden-haired, waxy-faced individual he had last encountered in the grounds of the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel, and in a flash he realised that he had walked into a trap, and that Sybil Laval was no more than a sort of female jackal for a gang of kidnappers. 

The mists seemed to clear from his brain in an instant. "So Earl Winter is Dandy Hagen, is he?" he drawled, looking up into the gloating face of his captor. "Perhaps I should congratulate you on your cleverness in engaging such an ally as Miss Sybil Laval, while refusing to commend your bravery in striking down a defenceless man. If you care to untie my bonds and face me as man to man, Dandy Hagen, I shall have much pleasure in saving the American authorities the trouble of putting you in the electric chair at Sing Sing.' "You dare to talk to me like that, you schweinhund 

Englisher!" Otto Hagenberg snarled at him. "I'll teach you, you dog! Take that!"

He carried in his hand a length of thick rubber tubing, with which he began to belabour Alan across the face and body in a very frenzy of fury. His was the mentality which delights in torturing, injuring, or even killing if his victim had no chance of hope of retaliating. "I'll make you squirm and scream, you swine," he 281

raved, almost foaming at the mouth; but Alan Armitage neither squirmed nor screamed, but only gritted his teeth and glared defiance from his blue-grey eyes at his torturer. "Stop, stop, you brute, you fiend!" screamed Gloria distractedly. "Let him go-let us both go and I'll pay any ransom you like to name. Stop, you beast, you cur!" "You call me cur-eh?" Hagenberg hissed at her. "A taste of Dandy Hagen's medicine may bring you to your senses as well. Take that, and that!"

He lashed at Gloria viciously with the rubber piping, and for the first time Alan cried out in an agony of impotent pain and rage, and writhed in a hopelessly ineffectual attempt to break his bonds. "You infernal coward and dastard!" he cried gaspingly, scarcely knowing what he was saying. "I swear by the Living God I'll kill you for this, kill you with my bare hands and send you back to the hounds of hell who gave you birth!"

In his efforts to free himself and to get at Hagenberg he fell off the bed and crashed to the floor of the cabin, whereat Hagenberg laughed and kicked him in the back as he lay prostrate. "I'll do the killing," he raved with an oath, as he kicked Alan again. "I haven't finished with you yetnor with her. I'll tell you I'm going to take her later with you looking on, and when Dandy Hagen lets you go after the ransoms have been paid, you'll both be damaged goods. I'll hand you over alive-but only just." "Otto, Otto, my boy, have you gone quite crazy?" broke in the rasping voice of Spider Sneyd, who seized

Hagenberg by the arm as he was about to renew his onslaught on the helpless Alan, who was battered, 282

bruised and almost fainting with pain, but still unconquered. "Stop this madness or one of the crew may give the alarm. Do you hear me?" He clawed at Otto's elbow half-frantically, and tore the rubber tubing from his hands. "We can't trust all the crew, and you know not what even men who have been promised big pay may do if they hear a woman scream in pain." 66 'And you know not what another woman may do!" said the high-pitched voice of Sybil Laval from the cabin door. "I heard what you said, Otto, and it's the second time you've said it. I warned you if you started any of your violent love stuff I'd blow the gaff, and 

I mean it. Leave them alone, or I'll go up on deck right now and signal for help to the Southern Glamour." "Damn you, I'll give you a taste of what I've given them!" yelled Hagenberg in a sort of frenzy, and made a spring at her, but Sybil was too quick for him. 

She slammed the door of the cabin in his face, sped along the gangway and up the companion-way on to the deck, around which she raced with Hagenberg in pursuit and Spider Sneyd in pursuit of both. "Otto, I didn't mean it!" she screamed breathlessly as Hagenberg overtook her and seized her roughly by the arm. "You know I wouldn't double-cross you.' 99 "Otto, Otto, for ze God's sake be sensible," cried 

Spider Sneyd, grabbing Otto's arm again. "You must be mad. Sybil has got Gloria Drake and the Englishman as well for you, and now you start making the trouble with her, although unless she helps we may not make a gateaway successful."

Hagenberg glared from one to the other, and then he realised suddenly that he had indeed been behaving like a maniac. His hands dropped limply to his sides, 283

the flush died out of his waxy face and the fire of anger out of his pale, steely eyes. "I-I guess I rather lost my head, Spider," he said jerkily, licking his lips. "Sorry, Sybil. It won't happen again. Let's go below and have a drink and talk business."

They went below, but from the deck of the Southern Glamour Bunty Gilmour, watching intently through a pair of binoculars in the hope of seeing Gloria and Alan appear on deck together, had seen them and had recognised both Dandy Hagen and the scrubbybearded, hawk-faced man she had seen in Sybil Laval's company at the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel. "Dandy Hagen!" breathed Bunty, dropping her glasses, and the colour drained out of her face, and the blood seemed to drain away from her heart, to which she pressed her hand convulsively. "The Lord have mercy!"

She looked about her with a feeling of complete helplessness, and with a dazed expression in her dark eyes. The Kanakas who had been rescued from a watery grave were laughing and chattering in the sun in the bow of the yacht, and a couple of deck hands and two stewards were playing cards as they squatted on the deck under an awning. Captain Simpson, the chief mate, and the chief engineer had all gone ashore together, and the rest of the officers and crew were presumably taking a siesta in their cabins. "The Lord have mercy!" repeated Bunty aloud, and was conscious of a feeling of annoyance as she realised that she was trembling. "Pull yourself together, Janet Gilmour," she added, and proceeded to do her best to obey her own bidding, and to collect her thoughts. A minute later she had interrupted the card game under the awning. "Yin o' ye go and find 284

the second mate or whaever is supposed to be in charge o' this boat, and tell him I must see him at once. And be slick about it, guy, 'cause it's urgent as an S. O. S." 

Everybody liked Bunty, so one of the deck hands went off at the double to do her bidding without question, and within a few minutes returned accompanied by the second mate, a staid, stolid, emotionless sort of individual, who listened quite unmoved to 

Bunty's excited and dramatic announcement that

Dandy Hagen was on board the Clarissa and that Gloria and Alan had, apparently, fallen into the clutches of a notorious and ruthless gang of kidnappers. "I don't see what I can do, Miss Gilmour, without orders," he said, scratching his head. “As you know, the skipper and the first officer went ashore in the motor-boat after lunch along of Mr. Macfarlane, with two of the crew to tend the boat, and another couple to get fresh water." "Yes, yes, I ken that, ye gomerel," exclaimed Bunty, almost dancing with impatience. "Order a boat to be lowered and send some of the crew ashore to find 

Captain Simpson and Mr. Flower-particularly Mr. 

Flower-as quick as ye possibly can. Oh, for the love o' Mike, get a move on." 

The second mate frowned and scratched his head again. He did not like taking orders from a woman, even if she did happen to be the secretary-companion of the owner's wife, but there seemed nothing else to do in the circumstances but to accept Miss Gilmour's suggestion, so he bellowed out an order and presently some of the crew came tumbling up on deck, lowered a boat and tumbled into it. "Oh, if only Blossom were here he'd ken what tae 285

do," said Bunty, still talking aloud to herself, and beginning to pace distractedly up and down the deck as the boat pulled off for the shore. "Blossom, ye fool, come aboard quickly and help your Bunty. Gloria and 

Alan are in danger o' their lives. Oh, Blossom, come and help Bunty to save them, you great big pink stiff!" Bunty's voice had risen almost to a scream, and she suddenly realised that she was losing control of herself, realised, also, that she was probably being watched from the Clarissa, and she pulled herself together and dived down the companion-way to the boudoir suite, out of which Imp hurled himself and tried to climb up her silk-stockinged legs to welcome her. 

Rodmell Homestall King was worried. First of all his beloved master had deserted him early in the morning when he went away with the pink man who supplied his biscuits, and then he had come back and gone away with the female woman who smelt so nice and had such cuddley arms for a little Sealyham, and then the female woman had come back and gone away again without taking any notice of him, and then his master had gone away without taking any notice of him. It was all dreadfully mysterious and heartbreaking to Imp. "Ye poor wee thing!" cried Bunty impulsively, stooping to gather the dog into her arms and strain him close to her breast. "Ye seem to be as worried as I am, and to ken the folk we love are in danger. 

Oh, Imp, what can you and me do? Cry to Blossom and Captain Simpson to come back quickly, quickly."

She set down Imp on the floor as impulsively as she had picked him up, and Imp, as if he understood, barked and barked and wailed and whimpered in a variety of voices, but all his appeals in his doggy 286

language, and all Bunty's prayers did not bring Blossom and Captain Simpson back to the boat until the short, tropical twilight was beginning swiftly to fade into darkness. The Cambridge professor, interested in Polynesian research, had taken them some distance into the interior of Raiatea, but his servants had also taken with them some excellent whisky from the professor's bungalow. "My God, the Dandy Hagen gang in possession of the Clarissa and Miss Gloria and Mr. Armitage in their power!" exclaimed Captain Sam Simpson, suddenly stone-sober and going white beneath the alcoholic flush superimposed on his tanned, weather-beaten face. "What can we do, Mr. Flower?"

Blossom also paled a trifle, took off his big spectacles and proceeded to polish the lenses with his silk handkerchief. "I perceive, captain, that they have lowered a speedboat from the Clarissa and it appears to be coming to us," he said with a preoccupied air, as he replaced his spectacles on his nose. "You will remember, incidentally, that it was in a speed-boat the Dandy

Hagen gang escaped from Honolulu after their defeat in the gardens of the Imperial Hawaiian. I wonder if we can defeat them again. Yes, the speed-boat is heading straight for us, and I should imagine it will probably bring some message." His presumption was correct. The speed-boat from the Clarissa raced across the Lagoon and came to a stop alongside the gangway of the Southern Glamour, down which one of the sailors scrambled to accept from one of the men in the speed-boat an envelope addressed to "Mr. Flower and Miss Gilmour." This he handed to the captain when he hastened back on deck and Sam Simpson in turn handed it to Blossom, who tore it open and read: 287

DEAR MR. FLOWER AND MISS GILMOUR, 

I have persuaded Mr. and Mrs. Armitage to stay to dinner and afterwards take a short cruise with me on the Clarissa. We shall probably be back tomorrow evening. 

Yours sincerely,

SYBIL LAVAL. 

Blossom handed the note to Bunty, then snatched it from her before she had time to make any comment and dashed to the head of the gangway. "Tell Miss Laval that everything is O.K. and we shall look forward to entertaining her and her guests aboard the Southern Glamour as soon as she comes back with Mr. and Mrs. Armitage," he shouted down to the men in the speed-boat. "What's the bright idea, Mr. Flower?" enquired

Captain Simpson as the speed-boat began to roar its way back to the Clarissa. "Oh, Blossom, de ye no' ken that letter is a lie?" added Bunty. "Ye ken they're both prisoners." 

Blossom did not seem to hear her. He folded up 

Sybil Laval's letter carefully, replaced it in the envelope in which it had arrived and handed it to the captain, who unfolded it again and read it by the last rays of light. Twilight was swiftly deepening into darkness, and the only sound, save for the fast-retreating noise of the speed-boat, and the waters of the Lagoon, agitated by it, lapping against the sides of the Southern Glamour, was the singing of the Kanakas squatting in a bunch in the bows of the yacht. Blossom took off his glasses and began to polish them again. "Oh, Blossom, for the Lord's sake, say somethingdo something!" burst out Bunty; but the only indication Blossom gave that he heard her was to stretch out one hand towards her after he had once more replaced his glasses. 288

"Sunset and evening star, and one clear call for me,' he said as if talking to himself, as Bunty gripped his extended hand: "The Kanakas we picked up may prove to be a godsend after all. Some of the crew of the 

Clarissa are Kanakas, the rest are probably low whites and toughs in the pay of the Dandy Hagen gang. I think I've got the idea." His rotund form seemed to stiffen as he wheeled round on Sam Simpson jerking away his hand from Bunty. "Captain, you speak their lingo," he continued, and now he was like unto a general giving orders to his staff. "Tell a couple of those

Kanakas for'ard to drop overboard, swim to the Clarissa, climb aboard and discover where Alan and Gloria are aboard the yacht and what has happened to them. 

They can swim pretty fast but without noise, and can scramble up the side of a vessel, as you know, like flies walking up a wall.” "I get you, Mr. Flower," said Captain Simpson, thrusting Sybil Laval's letter into his pocket and striding forward to talk to the natives, who jabbered back at him excitedly as he gave orders. He came back a few minutes later, after two of the Kanakas had dived overboard, making little more noise than a trout leaping from the water at a fly on the surface. "These

Marquesian natives are both brave and intelligent, Mr. Flower," Simpson resumed calmly. "I'll bet they get aboard the Clarissa and one at least of them will bring back the information you want. What then?" "I think we had better go below to discuss matters," replied Blossom. "And I think I shall need a whisky to stimulate my ideas and fortify me for the job ahead." "Oh, Blossom, how can ye think o' whisky at a time like this," exclaimed Bunty in distress as Blossom led the way down to the saloon and poured himself out a drink from the decanter on the sideboard. 

Again Blossom did not seem to hear her. He swallowed the drink and blinked at the captain. 289

"Tell MacPherson to get steam up," he ordered, and it struck both Sam Simpson and Bunty Gilmour that Horatio Flower had suddenly become a dictator and man of affairs instead of a sort of Pickwickian character. "You may have to chase the Clarissa if my plan fails, and I happen to get killed. How many men are there aboard that we can rely on to risk their lives in an attempt to rescue Alan and Gloria ?" "Well, there's myself, to start with, and where Captain Sam Simpson goes you can rely on the whole ship's company to follow him if he leads the way, even if he's told them that the idea is to storm the Gates of 

Hell," answered the skipper. "Let's hear your plan, Mr. Flower." "I can't plan definitely, captain, until the Kanakas come back and report to you," said Blossom, sitting down and putting the tips of his podgy pink fingers together. "If they can report in what part of the Clarissa

Alan and Gloria are imprisoned, my idea is to swim to the Clarissa with the rest of the Kanakas-I think I can say without conceit that I can swim as well and as fast as any native, although I cannot remain under water so long-my idea is to overpower, with the help of the natives, anyone who happens to be on deck and then to surprise the Hagen gang in the cabin. You, meanwhile, will launch a rowing-boat from the port side of the yacht and follow me and the natives as quickly as possible with as many fully armed men of the crew as care to volunteer for a dangerous job." "But why swim with Kanakas, and why use a rowing-boat when we have a motor-launch ?" "If they are keeping a strict look-out on the Clarissa, which seems likely, a motor-boat would be detected by its noise immediately, and the gang might be ready to repulse an attack. But even the most keen-eyed look-out man is hardly likely to be able to distinguish 290

between human beings and fish in the water at night, and a rowing-boat may escape notice, whereas a motorboat would be detected and located at once. Go and arm the crew, captain, and give them their instructions, then help me by acting as interpreter, when the Kanakas come back."

Captain Simpson stood looking at him steadily and in silence for a few moments, then he shot out a big, horny hand. "I'll say you're a man, Mr. Flower, as well as the champion whisky absorber on God's earth, and what you say goes with Sam Simpson," he exclaimed. "About this swimming to the Clarissa: you're not going to chance going aboard naked and unarmed?" "I shall wear my bathing-suit, and I have a waterproof bag in which I can carry a couple of automatics slung around my neck during the swim," explained

Blossom calmly, taking off his glasses and laying them down carefully behind the decanter on the sideboard. "The Kanakas can be armed with knives, which they can carry in their mouths, as you know. It promises to be quite an interesting evening."

A strangled cry made him turn to find Bunty gazing at him with tears welling into her dark eyes, and he blinked at her short-sightedly. “Why, Bunty!" he stammered as Bunty clasped one of his hands in both her own, raised it to her lips and kissed it. "Oh, Blossom, my dear, I wish ye werena going, but I'm proud o' ye," Bunty exclaimed disjointedly. "Oh, boy, ye're a man, a real man, and I hope to God ye'll be able tae save Gloria and her man, and come through safe yersel'." "Amen!" said Blossom and kissed the hands that clasped his own. 291

CHAPTER XXIII 

8 OTH Kanakas returned safely to report that two white men armed with guns were patrolling the deck of the Clarissa, but they (the Kanakas) had been able to get aboard unseen and talked to some of their fellow natives who had told them that a white man and a white woman were locked in a cabin on the starboard side of the yacht (that being the side farthest away from the Southern Glamour). One of the Kanakas drew a sort of diagram with a wet finger on Captain Simpson's table to explain that the cabins could be reached either from the fo'c'sle or through the main saloon, and that in the main saloon the boss white men were having a much heap big party with very much lots of liquor. "You find out, as I tell you, how many white men on board?" asked Sam Simpson in the native language and the intelligent Marquesans nodded, showing their white teeth in a characteristic grin.

Yes, they had found out there were, besides the two armed men on deck, two boss white men, one old and one young, and a boss white woman, besides a "red man" (this, of course was the native way of describing the ginger-headed, ginger-whiskered Bill Jones) who was not a member of the crew. The crew of the Clarissa totalled twelve whites and sixteen Kanakas. The latter, the Marquesan assured the captain, could be relied upon to remain neutral in the event of a fight. "So the 'guest' Miss Sybil Laval talked about existed only in her imagination, and the yacht must have been specially chartered for this kidnapping job," com- 292

mented Blossom thoughtfully, when the foregoing had been translated to him. "The 'boss white men' to which the natives refer are, I expect, Dandy Hagen and the bearded individual Bunty saw with the Laval woman at Honolulu. The other three will be the gunmen who accompanied Hagen in his attempted raid at the Imperial Hawaiian." "I guess you're right, Mr. Flower," nodded Captain

Simpson. "The job doesn't seem so formidable after all, particularly if we take them by surprise, for it is unlikely that the crew will be armed, or will offer any real resistance to my men, all of whom have guns and know how to use them.' 

"The chief danger, of course, is the two armed men on deck, but even if they are keeping a sharp look-out they will be watching the Southern Glamour and will not expect to be boarded from the starboard side of the Clarissa," said Blossom. "With the help of the

Kanakas I must disarm them and lay them out before giving you the signal to come aboard with your men." "Tell me exactly what you want the Kanakas to do when you climb aboard, and I'll interpret."

Blossom gave concise instructions which Simpson translated to the natives, all of whom nodded understanding and grinned as if the dangerous exploit in which they were about to engage was an enjoyable game to which they were looking forward with keen anticipation. Five minutes later they and Blossom were swimming rapidly through the waters of the Lagoon towards the Clarissa.

In the cabin in which Alan and Gloria were imprisoned much had happened that affected the lives of the two prisoners after Dandy Hagen had left them to chase Sybil Laval, and thus reveal himself to Bunty Gilmour. Alan, lying helpless and dazed on the floor, 293

became conscious of a voice-Gloria's husky, musically attractive voice-saying: "Rodmell, dear heart, I'm sorry, sorry, sorry! Sorry for having led you into this, sorry for having been such a fool-such a wicked fool. I loved you from the very start, Rodmell, and I want you to forgive everything as I forgive everything. . . . Do 

Do you hear me, belovedest ?" "I hear, Gloria," Alan answered faintly. "What was it that came into my mind? . . . Yes, I remember. . . . 

Something from W. E. Henley: 'You love yet you refrain, and I break my heart on your hard unfaith, and I break my heart in vain. . . .' That swine will probably kill me-unless I kill him. . . . Gloria, do you really mean that you love me ?" 

For answer Gloria, with her hands pinioned behind her back, rolled over from the bed, stepped out, lay down beside him and kissed him on the lips. "Darling, I-I love you, I love you—I've loved and wanted you from the very first," she gasped, interspersing her broken words with passionate kisses. "I wanted you to take me, and yet when you did I thought 

I hated you, and yet—and yet—oh, you wouldn't understand, and I don't even understand myself. But if

God grants that we escape together from this, beloved, I am yours for ever and ever, if only you will forgive me. . . . Rodmell, darling, tell me that you forgive me, tell me that you love me and nothing that can happen will matter, and we shall be able to face even death together.' 

The husky, disjointed words and the passionate kisses made Alan wonder if he were dreaming. And then his brain cleared and it seemed almost as if the words to which he had listened-the confession of love spoken from the heart of Gloria-seemed to bring balm to his own troubled heart and to ease his hurts. 294

"Kiss me again, darling Gloria mia," he said scarcely above a whisper, and added, when Gloria gladly had complied: "I love you with all my heart and body and brain and soul, and if I have anything to forgive, my forgiveness is granted as freely as the forgiveness you granted me. Perhaps it was pre-ordained that we should live for this moment, Gloria mia, and even if Hagen kills me, as I think he intends to do, life will have been worth living, for we have fulfilled ourselves. 'While the light lasts I shall remember, and in the darkness I shall not forget.' Kiss me again, belovedest." It was soon after that Dandy Hagen unlocked the cabin door and entered to find them lying close together. He had been drinking with Spider Sneyd in the saloon after his chase of Sybil round the deck, and his waxy face was slightly flushed, so that he looked something like a china doll on which someone had superimposed a little golden moustache, but much more evil in appearance than any china doll, or even a Chinese idol. "Lovebirds-eh?" he exclaimed, and spat in Alan's face. "Watch me kiss your darling wife as she should be kissed, you schweinhund Englishman, and later I will show you something more while you lie helplesssomething that will make you writhe as you writhed when you made your empty boast about killing me with your bare hands."

He dragged Gloria roughly to her feet as he spoke and kissed and kissed her in spite of her struggles, for, handcuffed as she was with her hands behind her back, she could offer but little resistance, but she succeeded in kicking him in the shins as he strained her to his breast. "Lovely little spitfire!" he ejaculated breathlessly, as he picked her up bodily and flung her on the bed. "I shall be back later, my beauty, to make more violent 295

love to you when I have finished celebrating the capture of you and your much distinguished and extinguished husband, and when the cursed engineer has repaired the defect which prevents us from sailing immediately. . . . To-morrow, Englishman, when we have sailed, I have planned to have some sport with you which you will not enjoy."

He kicked the helpless Alan and spat on him again before going back to the saloon to start a carouse with Spider Sneyd and Bill Jones, with whom were Sybil and the captain of the Clarissa, a hard case who had served a term of imprisonment for rum-running, and had jumped at the chance of taking his share in the ransom money of a kidnapping expedition. And as he drank his manner became more and more arrogant toward everyone, and his manner towards Sybil Laval became more and more offensive. 

That was why, when, a few hours later, Blossom shinned up the anchor chain and jumped nimbly into the bows of the Clarissa, he found himself almost stepping on a white-clad huddled figure that he recognised instantly, in spite of the darkness, as Sybil Laval.

Just for a few moments Blossom found himself completely nonplussed, for the woman was sobbing bitterly, and obviously she was as unaware of his presence as Al Casey and Joe Kruger were of the presence of the Kanakas from the Southern Glamour, who had accompanied him in the swim through the darkness to the Clarissa, and had clambered aboard silently on different parts of the yacht. He heard a muffled shout, followed by some scuffling sounds, and knew that his Kanakas had dealt with the armed guards without raising any alarm.

It was contrary to Blossom's every instinct to do any harm to any woman, despite the fact that a woman had harmed him more than any man could harm any 296

woman, but he remembered that the woman who seemed to be sobbing at his feet had acted as decoy duck for a gang of ruthless kidnappers and blackmailers who had in their power at that moment the one man on earth who claimed his love and devotion and the woman that man loved, so he stooped swiftly and took 

Sybil Laval by the throat. "If you scream or attempt to give the alarm I'll strangle you," he hissed at her, and tightened his grip on her throat as involuntarily she tried to scream in terror. "Where are Alan and Gloria ?"

Sybil had been absolutely terror-stricken, but now she recognized the voice of the tubby little figure whose wet hands had seized her throat as that of Horatio 

Flower, and she experienced something amounting to a revulsion of feeling. "Let me go and I'll tell you-help you," she gasped as Blossom relaxed his grip on her throat. "I won't scream or give the alarm, I swear. I-I'm glad you've come. I'll double-cross Otto Hagenberg as he's doublecrossed me. I got him Gloria Drake, and Rodmell

Armitage as well, and now he says he's got no use for me, and to-night he meant to take Gloria; but now-now. . . . Oh, my God, let me go, and I swear 

I'll help you, Mr. Flower. . . . But Joe Kruger and

Al Casey are on the deck and they are armed, and will shoot at sight," she added as Blossom released her throat but grabbed her by one wrist as she struggled to her feet, while at the same time with his free hand he took an automatic pistol from the waterproof bag slung around his neck "We'll go and see what's happened to Joe Kruger and Al Casey," said Blossom, in almost conversational tones, having recovered his breath and collected his scattered wits, "and if you try to double-cross me, my 297

lady, I'll put a bullet through you without the slightest compunction. 

A thrill of triumphant joy went through him when he found that the Kanakas had done their work excellently well. They had taken the two gunmen completely by surprise, disarmed them and half strangled them, and were now grinning delightedly as they bound the two thugs up after having gagged them. 

A white sailor, who had nominally been on watch, and who had heard and seen something of the scuffle, came racing along the deck, but stopped dead at sight of a tubby little man in a scanty bathing costume, holding in one hand an automatic pistol and grasping with the other the wrist of the lady he recognised as nominal owner of the Clarissa. "Keep still, and make no sound, or you're a dead man," snapped Blossom, and the deck hand caught in his breath and put his arms up above his head. "What's all this mean, lady?" he demanded, his eyes goggling. "It means you've got to keep your mouth shut, Bates, or the Kanakas will treat you like they've treated Kruger and Casey," responded Sybil Laval, recovering her self-possession to some extent. "Let me go, Mr. Flower," she added, turning to Blossom, who was keeping a wary eye on the startled seaman. "I swear to God I am on your side now, and I'll help all I can. But how the hell do you expect to get Mr. 

Armitage and Gloria away single-handed, or helped by only a few natives?" "Within a few minutes I shall not be single-handed if you will help," said Blossom dropping her hand but still on the alert-very much on the alert. "To show you are sincere and are not planning fresh treachery," he continued, swiftly producing a second automatic from the bag slung round his neck, "you can take an 298

electric torch out of the bag from which I have produced these pistols and flash it three times into the air and then three times in the direction of the Southern Glamour, and then as swiftly as possible afterwards you can take me to the cabin in which Alan and Gloria are imprisoned. Quick!"

Without a word Sybil Laval took the electric torch from his waterproof bag and flashed it as instructed, so giving the signal which indicated to Captain Sam Simpson that the armed guards had been overpowered. "Thanks," said Blossom as formally as if he were in a drawing-room. "Now lead the way to the prisoners, and we will go by way of the fo'c'sle and not through the saloon, if you please. . . . Jump to it! 

You lead the way, and if you attempt any treachery you'll be found shot in the back." "I'll take you, but the cabin door's locked, and Otto has the key," Sybil explained breathlessly. "You had better go to the saloon and hold up the bunch and I'll take the key off Otto." "No, thanks, I'm not walking into a trap, and there are more ways of opening a cabin door than with a key," Blossom snapped at her, then called to the

Kanakas in bêche-de-mer English that he wanted two of them to accompany him and the Mary woman down below, without turning his head or taking his glance away from Sybil and the deck hand, who had now lowered his arms. "This way then," said Sybil breathlessly and quickly led the way down the fo'c'sle companion followed by Blossom with two of the Kanakas at his heels. 

They met no one except a steward, who gaped in amazement at the spectacle of a man in a bathingsuit with a pistol in each hand and a couple of Kanakas armed with knives. "This door," resumed Sybil, coming to a halt pointing. 299

Blossom still feared treachery, and he did not take his eyes off her as he indicated to the Kanakas what he wanted them to do. "Bash um down heap quick," he ordered, and the natives understood.

The door looked to be of substantial mahogany, but the lock must have been a comparatively flimsy affair, because it gave way at the first charge. Gloria screamed involuntarily as the door crashed open and one of the Kanakas was precipitated into the cabin. 

Next moment she caught sight of Blossom and cried out again, but this time the cry was not one of terror. 

Blossom took in the situation in one swift glance. "Take the knife of one of the Kanakas and cut his bonds," he snapped at Sybil, motioning with one of his pistols to the bound figure of Alan on the floor of the cabin. "Be quick, and if you try any tricks I'll put a bullet through you without hesitation."

Without a word Sybil obeyed. At least, she took the knife of one of the natives, began to saw at the ropes that bound Alan without making much impression on them. The second Kanaka saw what she was attempting to do, went to her assistance, and in a few seconds had slashed through the ropes with his own knife.

Alan was free, but so tightly had he been bound and for so long that at first he was unable to move either his hands or his legs, and it was only by a great effort that he managed to scramble to his feet. "Take one of these pistols, Alan, and make the natives understand that they are to guard this woman. 

Tell them to knife her if she attempts to escape."

Even as he spoke he heard the sound of excited shouting both from the deck above him and from the saloon, and he sprang up swiftly into the corridor at the sound of a shot just as the door leading from the saloon to the cabins was flung open and three 300

men came rushing towards him. Without hesitation 

Blossom fired and the foremost man, the gingerheaded gunman, Bill Jones, crashed to the floor with a shot through his chest. The other two halted involuntarily. "Stick 'em up instantly!" Blossom yelled at them, and fired a shot over their heads as he recognised one of them as Dandy Hagen. "Put 'em up, you swine, or I'll kill you!"

With a snarl of impotent rage Hagenberg and 

Spider Sneyd slung up their hands above their heads just as Alan staggered, rather than walked, out of the cabin, pistol in hand, to the aid of his good comrade. Simultaneously Captain Simpson, followed by four of his men, all armed with guns or revolvers, appeared from the saloon behind the kidnappers. "Bravo, Mr. Flower! Bravo!" cried Simpson, excitedly and rather breathlessly. "Bully for you! 

We've cornered the rats!" "Search them, then have them tied up securely, captain," ordered Blossom without for an instant relaxing his vigilance in spite of the reinforcements and the success of the coup. "When you've disarmed them and tied 'em both up, send one of your men to help the Kanakas to fix the woman, Sybil Laval, who is in the cabin behind me. Then I'll attend to that ginger gunman I shot." "I get you, sir," said Sam Simpson, and snapped out gruff orders to his men, who seized on Dandy Hagen and Spider Sneyd, handling them none too lightly as they disarmed them and then handed the contents of their pockets to their skipper. "Take them into the saloon and fix 'em," he ordered next, and the brawny sailors grabbed Hagenberg and Sneyd by the scruff of their necks, at the same time threatening them with 301

revolvers, and dragged and hustled them back into the saloon. "Good old Blossom!" exclaimed Alan huskily and unsteadily as the men disappeared with their captain. "Blossom, old man, I'm-I'm all in. Will you-will you get me a drink?"

He seemed literally to crumple up as he spoke and collapsed in a heap at Blossom's feet. "Bring brandy, captain," ordered Blossom, little realising what an incongruous figure he presented in his skimpy bathing costume, while issuing orders. "I expect there's plenty in the saloon, and it may be that Miss Gloria is in as bad a way as Alan. If there isn't brandy there's sure to be whisky."

It did not occur to Sam Simpson to resent taking orders from the tubby little man in a bathing-suit, and he dashed away into the saloon, returning with a bottle of brandy, to find Blossom on his knees beside

Alan, gently massaging the discoloured wrists and ankles, into which the rope with which he had been bound had bitten. Alan was only half conscious, but he recovered after Simpson had poured some brandy down his throat, and he sat up choking and spluttering. "I-I'll be all right," he gasped jerkily. "See to

Gloria. She may be in a worse state than me. Don't bother about me. See to Gloria." 

Blossom and Simpson took him at his word and entered the cabin to find Gloria lying on the bed with her hands, of course, still handcuffed behind her back, and Sybil Laval standing, very white and scared, in the corner of the cabin with two grinning Kanakas, each with a knife in his hand, guarding her. "Drink some of this, lady," said Captain Simpson, slipping his arm around Gloria, putting the lip of the bottle which had revived Alan to her lips, and gently forcing her to swallow some of the brandy. "No need 302

to worry any more, little lady, you're safe now, and we've got the gang bottled up, thanks to Mr. Flower and the Kanakas." 

He put the bottle aside as he saw the flush came back into Gloria's face, after she had gasped and spluttered much as Alan had done, plunged his hand into his pocket and produced some of the things which his men had taken from the pockets of Dandy Hagen and Spider Sneyd, after he had talked in their own lingo to the Kanakas, one of whom nodded understanding and ran out. "Know anything about handcuffs, Mr. Flower?" he enquired, turning to Blossom, who was bending over 

Gloria solicitously. "I guess one of these keys should fit." 

Within three minutes Gloria's hands were free and

Blossom was massaging her wrists in the same way as he had massaged those of Alan a few minutes previously. And Alan was standing at the door of the cabin, looking rather dazed, but doing his best to appear his usual self when the Kanaka to whom Captain Simpson had given orders reappeared, accompanied by one of the men from the Southern 

Glamour carrying a length of thin rope. "You don't mean you've ordered them to tie me up," screeched Sybil Laval as she realised the intentions of the men when Captain Simpson merely jerked his head in her direction, "Mr. Flower, I helped you! 

You won't let them treat me now as if I was one of the gang?" "You double-crossed Miss Gloria and Mr. Armitage, you tried to double-cross Miss Gilmour, you have double-crossed your friend Otto Hagenberg, and now 

I suppose, you think you can double-cross me," said 

Blossom, his voice and his eyes as hard as steel. "If you expect me to show any mercy to the decoy duck 303

and jackal of a kidnapping and blackmailing gangpardon me if I have made a sort of Irishism by mangling decoy ducks and jackals, which are of two different species; if as I was saying, you expect any mercy the answer is most decidedly in the negative.

Tie her up, my lad, and make a good job of it." 304

CHAPTER XXIV 

8 "WEL 

YELL, what do we do now, Mr. Armitage?" enquired Captain Simpson early next morning. "We got the Dandy Hagen gang all nicely trussed up aboard the Clarissa, with my chief mate and five of the crew, besides three of our Kanakas, guarding them, and keeping the crew of the Clarissa under their thumbs so to speak. As you know, I had the yacht searched, and every weapon that was aboard of her is now aboard the Southern Glamour. The skipper of the Clarissa says it's piracy and incidentally, he didn't say it very politely, but I guess I've got that guy ticked off and know his record won't bear investigation. 

He's in with the Hagen gang, and we've got the Hagen gang, but what do we do about it?"

There had been much coming and going between the Clarissa and the Southern Glamour on the previous night after Blossom had led the successful raid. 

First of all, Captain Simpson, leaving his mate in charge on the Clarissa, had taken Alan and Gloria back to the Southern Glamour and placed Gloria in the tender care of Bunty, who, for once in a way, had thrown all her Scottish reserve to the winds and behaved like what she herself would have described as a "daft thing" at sight of Alan and Gloria, and on learning that Blossom had brought off his great coup successfully and was unhurt.

Then the captain in the motor-boat had taken out to the Clarissa Blossom's clothes, Blossom having announced his intention of remaining aboard the enemy yacht, explaining with a twinkle in his blue eyes that 305

he had discovered it was well stocked with an excellent brand of whisky and reminding the captain to bring his spectacles as well as his clothes. "It's like this, you see, Mr. Armitage," continued 

Captain Simpson. "We got the notorious Hagen gang and the Clarissa in our power. What do we do with them, sir? It's up to you. Do we take the whole gang aboard the Southern Glamour and sail back with them to Honolulu to deliver them over to the authorities? 

That means leaving the Clarissa here, and I guess the skipper and some of the crew are in league with the gang.' 99 Alan sat silent for a full minute before replying, absently rubbing his bruised and chafed wrists and working his fingers, then after drawing a deep breath he produced a cigarette-case. "That will depend, Mr. Simpson," he drawled with a queer smile, selecting a cigarette and lighting it with deliberation. "One of the gang, to wit, the chief of the gang, although he may not be the brains of it-I mean the man variously known as Dandy Hagen, Earl Winter and Otto Hagenberg-is not going, and if he does I shall not go back. I made a vow, skipper, that I would kill him with my bare hands, and I pride myself on being a man of my word. Of course, he may kill me, in which case I must leave it to you to decide what to do in the circumstances." 

Captain Sam Simpson asked questions and received laconic answers and sat and stared half admiringly and half perplexedly at Alan, who seemed to be enjoying his cigarette, and to be secretly amused about something. "So you are going to take on the thug, although your wrists and ankles ain't in anything like a fit state, and you know he won't even try to fight fair," he commented at last. 306

"I think we'll start fairly level, Captain," responded 

Alan. "You will remember that he has been tied up all night, although probably not nearly so tightly as I was tied, and I have not kicked him in the back and ribs as he kicked me. I think, if you don't mind, we'll go over to the Clarissa now and get the thing over. If

Hagenberg happens to win and I don't come back, there's a letter here that I would like you to give to 

Glor-to my wife."

Captain Simpson took the letter, pocketed it, stood silent for a minute, and then shot out his big hand impulsively. "You're a queer guy, Mr. Armitage, but I like you, and I'll say you're a man," he said, and his deep voice was curiously husky. "Let's get it over."

He shouted orders and a few minutes later he and Alan were in the motor-boat speeding towards the 

Clarissa, aboard which they cilmbed to find all the 

Kanakas singing, the crew of the Clarissa going about with scowling faces, and the armed section of the crew of the Southern Glamour looking as if they were thoroughly enjoying themselves. Captain Simpson conferred with his mate and then he and Alan went down to the saloon, on the lounges of which Otto Hagenberg, Spider Sneyd, Al Casey, and Joe Kruger, all tied up with sailor-like thoroughness, were lying. Bill 

Jones, seriously but not fatally wounded, had been deposited in another cabin, where he had been tended with the skill of a surgeon by Blossom, and Sybil Laval had been deposited in the cabin in which Gloria had been imprisoned. Kruger and Casey were asleep, but

Sneyd and Otto Hagenberg were awake, and it was to the latter that Alan addressed himself. "You may probably remember, Mr. Otto Hagenberg, otherwise Earl Winter and better known as 

Dandy Hagen, that I promised to kill you with my 307

bare hands," he drawled. "I propose to do it during the next half hour. It will probably be a slow and painful process, but I promise to do my best to dispose of you as quickly as possible." "You would kill a defenceless man-and you an 

Englishman!" exclaimed Hagenberg hoarsely, with fear in his pale eyes. "You will have your hands, you swine, and your feet if you like to use them but nothing else," responded Alan. "I do not kick a man when he is down. 

It will be man to man and a fight to the death. Understand that... .? Captain Simpson, order your men to untie him and give him half an hour or so to recover himself, then bring him on deck. You will forgive me if I forget all about Queensbury Rules, for I know he will not fight fair, and I am determined to kill him."

There was a sort of steely hatred and determination in Alan's calm, drawling voice that struck fear into the heart of Otto Hagenberg and made even Sam 

Simpson feel inclined to shudder as he gave orders to one of his men. He had never imagined that Alan 

Armitage would be capable of such coldly passionate ruthlessness, and yet he found himself glorying in the prospect of witnessing such a fight as he had never seen in his life.

He went on deck a little later, after Hagenberg had been freed from his bonds and allowed every opportunity (under surveillance) of preparing himself for the fight, to find Alan and Blossom, the latter now immaculately clad in a white suit and pith helmet, talking earnestly together. "But why-why, Alan, take the risk?" burst out 

Blossom as he approached. "You can hand him over to the authorities and send him to the electric chair." "Have you ever known me refuse to take a risk, or to fail to keep a promise, Blossom?" retorted Alan, 308

and smiled into his friend's eyes. "Understand this, and make the others understand. This is going to be man to man, and neither you nor anyone else must interfere, even if I am getting the worst of it. Now we will take a little trot around the deck just to get my breathing apparatus into something approaching order."

He was wearing only shorts, canvas shoes and an open-necked shirt, and he tore off his shirt as soon as Hagenberg was brought up on deck about half an hour or so later. Captain Simpson, himself in a state of suppressed excitement, had rapped out orders, a space on the deck had been completely cleared, and an awning taken down. Hagenberg was wearing slacks and a shirt, but he followed Alan's example and discarded the shirt.

There were no preliminaries except that Captain 

Simpson in two languages, ordered the Kanakas, who were jibbering with excitement, and the crew, both of his own vessel and the Clarissa, to keep back. The two men faced each other, one the lean, sinewy, clean-cut 

English type, the other, the blond, Teutonic, athletic type, and in the face of one there was only calm determination and in the waxy face of the other hatred and an expression seen in members of the cat family when at bay.

Hagenberg charged at Alan, his fists swinging like flails, but Alan side-stepped him and steadied him with a smashing blow to the ribs which made him. grunt, but he was not lacking in courage of a sort and realised that his life was a stake. They fought with fists at first like men in a frenzy, yet always the onlookers had the idea that the Englishman was not losing his self-possession, and was keeping a cool head, although, as the fight went on, Hagenberg, the heavier man, seemed to be getting the advantage. 309

The primitive instincts of everyone, including the combatants, were stirred. The Kanakas howled and jibbered like animals when Hagenberg drove a straight right into Alan's face, drawing blood, and some of the white sailormen yelled and cursed in disgust when a minute later he lowered his head and charged at Alan like a gigantic goat, butting him in the pit of the stomach and sending him staggering backwards against the rails of the vessel. Two or three would have interfered, but were restrained by Blossom and the captain of the Southern Glamour, although both were sorely concerned for Alan and would have liked to interfere themselves.

They would have interfered, in spite of Alan's previously expressed commands, when Hagenberg following up his advantage, seized his opponent and hurled him headlong to the deck and began to kick him savagely, had not Alan somehow contrived, while prostrate, to seize his enemy's ankle and, by an adroit ju-jitsu twist, bring him crashing heavily to the deck alongside him, where immediately he grappled with him.

There followed in the next five minutes such an exhibition of "all-in" wrestling as might well have made the most bloodthirsty follower of the "sport" shudder, an exhibition that set the usually peaceful 

Kanakas positively raving and dancing with excitement, and made every white onlooker yell. Even Blossom found himself shouting and cursing, literally howling: “Get him, Alan, get him!" over and over again.

Both men now were covered with blood. Hagenberg was fighting tooth and nail, clawing and scratching and biting whenever opportunity presented itself. He seemed still to hold an advantage, but he realised with fear in his heart that he was weakening, that his opponent was stronger than he and more expert in 310

the deadly mixture of ju-jitsu and all-in wrestling, and that if Alan only contrived to get a proper grip on him he could dislocate or break his limbs, or even his neck. 

He struggled desperately, taking and giving a deal of punishment, and at last, with a supreme effort, managed to break from Alan's steel-like, deadly hold and scramble to his feet.

Alan was up as swift as he, and the two faced each other again, panting for breath, both crouched like tigers ready to spring, but both, unlike tigers, wiping the blood away from their eyes and mouths before renewing the battle.

It was Alan who took the offensive. Damaged as he was, and with blood leaking from his nose and mouth, he knew instinctively that he had got his enemy beaten and as soon as he had caught his breath he waded into Hagenberg, boxing again. A straight left sent Hagenberg backwards, but he clinched, and as he did so Alan battered him in the ribs, and then, breaking from him suddenly, flashed an upper cut that sent his opponent down for what would have been the count if it had been a boxing match.

Temporarily Alan forgot that he was engaged in a life or death struggle and stood back, but as Hagenberg recovered and scrambled to his feet again, he went berserk. Dandy Hagen tried to protect himself, but Alan battered him mercilessly, battered him with his bare hands, the knuckles of which were now bleeding, while the crew of the Clarissa and the men from the Southern Glamour shouted and roared and the 

Kanakas screamed and danced.

Again Hagenberg went down, and again, instead of kicking him, as his opponent had kicked him, Alan stood back, drawing in his breath in great, difficult gasps. "Get up, Dandy Hagen," he panted. "I haven't 311

finished with you yet. You thrashed my Gloria and me while we were helpless, and now I am going to kill you kill you with my naked hands, as I swore I would do. Get up, you cur!" "Kamerad! Oh, have mercy, have mercy!" screamed Hagenberg breathlessly, as he recovered himself and scrambled to his feet, and then, instead of facing Alan, make a dash for the rail of the yacht, scrambled over it, and dived into the sea. 

For the space of a dozen heart-beats everyone, including the Kanakas, were stricken into silence, then all on deck, save Alan, made a dash for the side of the yacht over which Dandy Hagen had dived, causing the vessel to tilt visibly. Captain Sam Simpson shouted orders, to which no one paid any attention, for Hagenberg seemed to have sunk like a stone and did not reappear. "Mr. Armitage," yelled Simpson, turning to the place where last he had seen Alan standing, "I want to tell you, sir, that you're the bravest . . . Oh, my God!"

He saw Alan, covered with blood, crumpled up in a heap on the spot where he had challenged Dandy Hagen to the final contest, and he thought he was dead. The words he had intended to say were strangled in his throat, and he stood, half dazed, as Blossom appeared as if from nowhere, gathered Alan into his arms, and shouted for someone to bring water and brandy. At that moment the blast of a siren, which was answered by a blast from the siren of the Southern 

Glamour, made him jerk round his head to see the

United States destroyer Lincoln steaming into the lagoon. Hours later Alan opened his eyes, and for the second time struggled back to consciousness. First time 312

he had tried to scramble to his feet, with the idea uppermost in his mind that he had not finished with Dandy Hagen, but he had been restrained by Blossom, who had poured water over him, poured brandy down his throat, tenderly sponged his bruised Body, and treated his hurts with arnica and iodine. "Good old Blossom!" was all he had gasped out, after Blossom had assured him that he had beaten

Hagenberg and fulfilled his vow; and then he had lapsed back into unconsciousness.

Now he came to life again to realise by degrees that his hands and head were bandaged, and to wonder vaguely if he were dreaming when he saw Gloria, Bunty, Blossom, Inspector Harrigan, Captain Sam

Simpson, and another man in uniform, whom he had never seen before, all gathered round the bed on which he was lying. He did not know he had been treated aboard the Clarissa by the surgeon of the U.S. destroyer Lincoln (the man in uniform he had never seen before), and that he had been taken back to the Southern Glamour on an improvised stretcher on the motor-boat. Alan decided he must be dreaming, but he managed, after blinking his eyes a few times at the people gathered round his bed, to struggle up-aided by Blossom-into a sitting posture. "What's the bright idea?" he enquired in his drawling voice, gazing around as the mists of sleep and dope began to clear from his brain. "Holding a wake over me, or something of the sort?" "Rodmell, my dear, my dear!" gasped Gloria, huskily and brokenly, taking one of his bandaged hands and holding it against her cheek. "The doctor says you'll get better, my darling." "Yes, you'll get better all right," said the brindleheaded, grey-moustached surgeon from the Lincoln, smiling kindly at Armitage. "You've taken a lot of 313

punishment, sir, but except for a couple of damaged ribs, which I've fixed up as best I can, you have no serious injuries. And I guess you've got a constitution like a horse." "Or an ass," retorted Alan, his lean face twisting into a smile, although he winced in pain as he smiled. "I say, what does this all mean? How did you get here, Mr. Harrigan?" "It's like this, Mr. Armitage," explained Inspector 

Harrigan, moving closer to the bedside and patting Alan's shoulder gently, his face crinkling into a sympathetic smile. "I suspected the woman, Sybil Laval, from the first, and when I identified the man I had seen with her at the Imperial Hawaiian Hotel as

Seth Sneyd I guessed she was connected with the 

Dandy Hagen gang. That's one of the reasons why

I turned up at Haleiwa. To cut the story short, when the Clarissa sailed, soon after you had sailed on the

Southern Glamour with Miss Gloria Drake-I beg your pardon, I should say Mrs. Alan Rodmell Armitage and I'll tell you, sir, it touches my heart to see your lovely lady holding your hand against her lovely face like that-when, as I was going to say, I tumbled to the fact that the Dandy Hagen gang were aboard the Clarissa, I sent out an S O S message in code, and the destroyer Lincoln arrived in Honolulu within twelve hours, took me aboard, and here I am. . . 

Thanks to you and Mr. Flower, we've got the whole gang, except Dandy Hagen himself, and I guess the 

United States Government will want to thank you for ridding the world of a swine like him. . . . Thank you and bless you, Mr. Armitage, and—and I hope from my heart you and your dear, lovely lady will be happy all the days of your life. God bless you both!"

He patted Alan gently on the shoulder again, and the surgeon from the Lincoln, Bunty and Blossom, 314

followed him out as he took his leave. Half dazedly 

Alan turned his head as the door of the cabin closed behind them to look at Gloria. She was still holding his bandaged hand against her cheek and the tears were streaming unchecked from her lovely eyes. "Dear, lovely lady, tell me that you meant what you said when you told me you loved me and were all mine," said Alan. "Darling Alan, my own dear man, I love and love and love you, and am all, all yours for ever and ever, amen!" sobbed Gloria, throwing her arms around him and straining him close. "Nothing on earth shall ever part us. Kiss me, my own beloved, kiss and kiss me, darling heart." "Woof! Hoi! Hoi, female woman, what are you doing to my beloved master?" demanded Rodmell

Homestall King, appearing suddenly from beneath the bed, his stumpy tail quivering. "Oh, I see, you're loving him. . . . Hoi, woman! Help me up and I'll lick his face as well as you.

L'ENVOI you should happen to visit Mr. Cyrus Oke Cole, either at his office or his private residence, it is certain that he will show you at least two photographs, both of which have appeared in the Sketch and Tatler.

Sealyham in the foreground as Imp, otherwise Rodmell Homestall King. The other is of "Mr. Horatio

Flower, the eminent scientist-novelist, with his wife, in the garden of their Sussex home near Rodmell," and you will recognise Mrs. Horatio Flower as Bunty. 316
